[I] Cheerfulness Again Asserts Itself at Blooms - End, and Clym Finds His Vocation “ To sorrow I bade good morrow, And thought to leave her far away behind; [/]
[I] But cheerly, cheerly, She loves me dearly; She is so constant to me, and so kind. [/]
[I] I would deceive her, And so leave her, [/]
[I] But ah! [/]
[I] she is so constant and so kind. ” PREFACE The date at which the following events are assumed to have occurred may be set down as between 1840 and 1850, when the old watering place herein called “ Budmouth ” still retained sufficient afterglow from its Georgian gaiety and prestige to lend it an absorbing attractiveness to the romantic and imaginative soul of a lonely dweller inland. [/]
[I] Under the general name of “ Egdon Heath, ” which has been given to the sombre scene of the story, are united or typified heaths of various real names, to the number of at least a dozen; these being virtually one in character and aspect, though their original unity, or partial unity, is now somewhat disguised by intrusive strips and slices brought under the plough with varying degrees of success, or planted to woodland. [/]
[Narrator] It is pleasant to dream that some spot in the extensive tract whose southwestern quarter is here described, may be the heath of that traditionary King of Wessex — Lear. [/]
[Narrator] T. H. _ July _, 1895. [/]
[Narrator] BOOK FIRST — THE THREE WOMEN I. A Face on Which Time Makes but Little Impression A Saturday afternoon in November was approaching the time of twilight, and the vast tract of unenclosed wild known as Egdon Heath embrowned itself moment by moment. [/]
[Narrator] Overhead the hollow stretch of whitish cloud shutting out the sky was as a tent which had the whole heath for its floor. [/]
[Narrator] The heaven being spread with this pallid screen and the earth with the darkest vegetation, their meeting - line at the horizon was clearly marked. [/]
[Narrator] In such contrast the heath wore the appearance of an instalment of night which had taken up its place before its astronomical hour was come: darkness had to a great extent arrived hereon, while day stood distinct in the sky. [/]
[Narrator] Looking upwards, a furze - cutter would have been inclined to continue work; looking down, he would have decided to finish his faggot and go home. [/]
[Narrator] The distant rims of the world and of the firmament seemed to be a division in time no less than a division in matter. [/]
[Narrator] The face of the heath by its mere complexion added half an hour to evening; it could in like manner retard the dawn, sadden noon, anticipate the frowning of storms scarcely generated, and intensify the opacity of a moonless midnight to a cause of shaking and dread. [/]
[Narrator] In fact, precisely at this transitional point of its nightly roll into darkness the great and particular glory of the Egdon waste began, and nobody could be said to understand the heath who had not been there at such a time. [/]
[Narrator] It could best be felt when it could not clearly be seen, its complete effect and explanation lying in this and the succeeding hours before the next dawn; then, and only then, did it tell its true tale. [/]
[Narrator] The spot was, indeed, a near relation of night, and when night showed itself an apparent tendency to gravitate together could be perceived in its shades and the scene. [/]
[Narrator] The sombre stretch of rounds and hollows seemed to rise and meet the evening gloom in pure sympathy, the heath exhaling darkness as rapidly as the heavens precipitated it. [/]
[Narrator] And so the obscurity in the air and the obscurity in the land closed together in a black fraternization towards which each advanced halfway. [/]
[Narrator] The place became full of a watchful intentness now; for when other things sank brooding to sleep the heath appeared slowly to awake and listen. [/]
[Narrator] Every night its Titanic form seemed to await something; but it had waited thus, unmoved, during so many centuries, through the crises of so many things, that it could only be imagined to await one last crisis — the final overthrow. [/]
[Narrator] It was a spot which returned upon the memory of those who loved it with an aspect of peculiar and kindly congruity. [/]
[Narrator] Smiling champaigns of flowers and fruit hardly do this, for they are permanently harmonious only with an existence of better reputation as to its issues than the present. [/]
[Narrator] Twilight combined with the scenery of Egdon Heath to evolve a thing majestic without severity, impressive without showiness, emphatic in its admonitions, grand in its simplicity. [/]
[Narrator] The qualifications which frequently invest the façade of a prison with far more dignity than is found in the façade of a palace double its size lent to this heath a sublimity in which spots renowned for beauty of the accepted kind are utterly wanting. [/]
[Narrator] Fair prospects we d happily with fair times; but alas, if times be not fair! [/]
[Narrator] Men have oftener suffered from, the mockery of a place too smiling for their reason than from the oppression of surroundings oversadly tinged. [/]
[Narrator] Haggard Egdon appealed to a subtler and scarcer instinct, to a more recently learnt emotion, than that which responds to the sort of beauty called charming and fair. [/]
[Narrator] Indeed, it is a question if the exclusive reign of this orthodox beauty is not approaching its last quarter. [/]
[Narrator] The new Vale of Tempe may be a gaunt waste in Thule; human souls may find themselves in closer and closer harmony with external things wearing a sombreness distasteful to our race when it was young. [/]
[Narrator] The time seems near, if it has not actually arrived, when the chastened sublimity of a moor, a sea, or a mountain will be all of nature that is absolutely in keeping with the moods of the more thinking among mankind. [/]
[Narrator] And ultimately, to the commonest tourist, spots like Iceland may become what the vineyards and myrtle gardens of South Europe are to him now; and Heidelberg and Baden be passed unheeded as he hastens from the Alps to the sand dunes of Scheveningen. [/]
[Narrator] The most thoroughgoing ascetic could feel that he had a natural right to wander on Egdon — he was keeping within the line of legitimate indulgence when he laid himself open to influences such as these. [/]
[Narrator] Colours and beauties so far subdued were, at least, the birthright of all. [/]
[Narrator] Only in summer days of highest feather did its mood touch the level of gaiety. [/]
[Narrator] Intensity was more usually reached by way of the solemn than by way of the brilliant, and such a sort of intensity was often arrived at during winter darkness, tempests, and mists. [/]
[Narrator] Then Egdon was aroused to reciprocity; for the storm was its lover, and the wind its friend. [/]
[Narrator] Then it became the home of strange phantoms; and it was found to be the hitherto unrecognized original of those wild regions of obscurity which are vaguely felt to be compassing us about in midnight dreams of flight and disaster, and are never thought of after the dream till revived by scenes like this. [/]
[Narrator] It was at present a place perfectly accordant with man ’s nature — neither ghastly, hateful, nor ugly; neither commonplace, unmeaning, nor tame; but, like man, slighted and enduring; and withal singularly colossal and mysterious in its swarthy monotony. [/]
[Narrator] As with some persons who have long lived apart, solitude seemed to look out of its countenance. [/]
[Narrator] It had a lonely face, suggesting tragical possibilities. [/]
[Narrator] This obscure, obsolete, superseded country figures in Domesday. [/]
[Leland] Its condition is recorded therein as that of heathy, furzy, briary wilderness—“Bruaria. ” [/]
[Leland] Then follows the length and breadth in leagues; and, though some uncertainty exists as to the exact extent of this ancient lineal measure, it appears from the figures that the area of Egdon down to the present day has but little diminished. [/]
[Leland] “ Turbaria Bruaria”—the right of cutting heath - turf — occurs in charters relating to the district. [/]
[Leland] “ Overgrown with heth and mosse, ” says Leland of the same dark sweep of country. [/]
[Narrator] Here at least were intelligible facts regarding landscape — far - reaching proofs productive of genuine satisfaction. [/]
[Narrator] The untameable, Ishmaelitish thing that Egdon now was it always had been. [/]
[Narrator] Civilization was its enemy; and ever since the beginning of vegetation its soil had worn the same antique brown dress, the natural and invariable garment of the particular formation. [/]
[Narrator] In its venerable one coat lay a certain vein of satire on human vanity in clothes. [/]
[Narrator] A person on a heath in raiment of modern cut and colours has more or less an anomalous look. [/]
[Narrator] We seem to want the oldest and simplest human clothing where the clothing of the earth is so primitive. [/]
[Narrator] To recline on a stump of thorn in the central valley of Egdon, between afternoon and night, as now, where the eye could reach nothing of the world outside the summits and shoulders of heathland which filled the whole circumference of its glance, and to know that everything around and underneath had been from prehistoric times as unaltered as the stars overhead, gave ballast to the mind adrift on change, and harassed by the irrepressible New. [/]
[Narrator] The great inviolate place had an ancient permanence which the sea can not claim. [/]
[Narrator] Who can say of a particular sea that it is old? [/]
[Narrator] Distilled by the sun, kneaded by the moon, it is renewed in a year, in a day, or in an hour. [/]
[Narrator] The sea changed, the fields changed, the rivers, the villages, and the people changed, yet Egdon remained. [/]
[Narrator] Those surfaces were neither so steep as to be destructible by weather, nor so flat as to be the victims of floods and deposits. [/]
[Narrator] With the exception of an aged highway, and a still more aged barrow presently to be referred to — themselves almost crystallized to natural products by long continuance — even the trifling irregularities were not caused by pickaxe, plough, or spade, but remained as the very finger - touches of the last geological change. [/]
[Narrator] The above - mentioned highway traversed the lower levels of the heath, from one horizon to another. [/]
[Narrator] In many portions of its course it overlaid an old vicinal way, which branched from the great Western road of the Romans, the Via Iceniana, or Ikenild Street, hard by. [/]
[Narrator] On the evening under consideration it would have been noticed that, though the gloom had increased sufficiently to confuse the minor features of the heath, the white surface of the road remained almost as clear as ever. [/]
[Narrator] II. [/]
[Narrator] Humanity Appears upon the Scene, Hand in Hand with Trouble Along the road walked an old man. [/]
[Narrator] He was white - headed as a mountain, bowed in the shoulders, and faded in general aspect. [/]
[Narrator] He wore a glazed hat, an ancient boat - cloak, and shoes; his brass buttons bearing an anchor upon their face. [/]
[Narrator] In his hand was a silver - headed walking stick, which he used as a veritable third leg, perseveringly dotting the ground with its point at every few inches ’ interval. [/]
[Narrator] One would have said that he had been, in his day, a naval officer of some sort or other. [/]
[Narrator] Before him stretched the long, laborious road, dry, empty, and white. [/]
[Narrator] It was quite open to the heath on each side, and bisected that vast dark surface like the parting - line on a head of black hair, diminishing and bending away on the furthest horizon. [/]
[Narrator] The old man frequently stretched his eyes ahead to gaze over the tract that he had yet to traverse. [/]
[Narrator] At length he discerned, a long distance in front of him, a moving spot, which appeared to be a vehicle, and it proved to be going the same way as that in which he himself was journeying. [/]
[Narrator] It was the single atom of life that the scene contained, and it only served to render the general loneliness more evident. [/]
[Narrator] Its rate of advance was slow, and the old man gained upon it sensibly. [/]
[Narrator] When he drew nearer he perceived it to be a spring van, ordinary in shape, but singular in colour, this being a lurid red. [/]
[Narrator] The driver walked beside it; and, like his van, he was completely red. [/]
[Narrator] One dye of that tincture covered his clothes, the cap upon his head, his boots, his face, and his hands. [/]
[Narrator] He was not temporarily overlaid with the colour; it permeated him. [/]
[Narrator] The old man knew the meaning of this. [/]
[Narrator] The traveller with the cart was a reddleman — a person whose vocation it was to supply farmers with redding for their sheep. [/]
[Narrator] He was one of a class rapidly becoming extinct in Wessex, filling at present in the rural world the place which, during the last century, the dodo occupied in the world of animals. [/]
[Narrator] He is a curious, interesting, and nearly perished link between obsolete forms of life and those which generally prevail. [/]
[Narrator] The decayed officer, by degrees, came up alongside his fellow - wayfarer, and wished him good evening. [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman turned his head, and replied in sad and occupied tones. [/]
[Narrator] He was young, and his face, if not exactly handsome, approached so near to handsome that nobody would have contradicted an assertion that it really was so in its natural colour. [/]
[Narrator] His eye, which glared so strangely through his stain, was in itself attractive — keen as that of a bird of prey, and blue as autumn mist. [/]
[Narrator] He had neither whisker nor moustache, which allowed the soft curves of the lower part of his face to be apparent. [/]
[Narrator] His lips were thin, and though, as it seemed, compressed by thought, there was a pleasant twitch at their corners now and then. [/]
[Narrator] He was clothed throughout in a tight - fitting suit of corduroy, excellent in quality, not much worn, and well - chosen for its purpose, but deprived of its original colour by his trade. [/]
[Narrator] It showed to advantage the good shape of his figure. [/]
[Narrator] A certain well - to - do air about the man suggested that he was not poor for his degree. [/]
[Narrator] The natural query of an observer would have been, Why should such a promising being as this have hidden his prepossessing exterior by adopting that singular occupation? [/]
[Narrator] After replying to the old man ’s greeting he showed no inclination to continue in talk, although they still walked side by side, for the elder traveller seemed to desire company. [/]
[Narrator] There were no sounds but that of the booming wind upon the stretch of tawny herbage around them, the crackling wheels, the tread of the men, and the footsteps of the two shaggy ponies which drew the van. [/]
[They] They were small, hardy animals, of a breed between Galloway and Exmoor, and were known as “ heath - croppers ” here. [/]
[Narrator] Now, as they thus pursued their way, the reddleman occasionally left his companion ’s side, and, stepping behind the van, looked into its interior through a small window. [/]
[Narrator] The look was always anxious. [/]
[Narrator] He would then return to the old man, who made another remark about the state of the country and so on, to which the reddleman again abstractedly replied, and then again they would lapse into silence. [/]
[Narrator] The silence conveyed to neither any sense of awkwardness; in these lonely places wayfarers, after a first greeting, frequently plod on for miles without speech; contiguity amounts to a tacit conversation where, otherwise than in cities, such contiguity can be put an end to on the merest inclination, and where not to put an end to it is intercourse in itself. [/]
[Narrator] Possibly these two might not have spoken again till their parting, had it not been for the reddleman ’s visits to his van. [/]
[TheOldMan] When he returned from his fifth time of looking in the old man said, “ You have something inside there besides your load? ” “ Yes. ” [/]
[TheOldMan] “ Somebody who wants looking after? ” “ Yes. ” Not long after this a faint cry sounded from the interior. [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman hastened to the back, looked in, and came away again. [/]
[TheOldMan] “ You have a child there, my man? ” “ No, sir, I have a woman. ” [/]
[TheOldMan] “ The deuce you have! [/]
[TheOldMan] Why did she cry out? ” “ Oh, she has fallen asleep, and not being used to traveling, she ’s uneasy, and keeps dreaming. ” [/]
[TheOldMan] “ A young woman? ” “ Yes, a young woman. ” “ That would have interested me forty years ago. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps she ’s your wife? ” “ My wife! ” said the other bitterly. [/]
[TheOtherBitterly] “ She ’s above mating with such as I. [/]
[Narrator] But there ’s no reason why I should tell you about that. ” “ That ’s true. [/]
[TheOldMan] And there ’s no reason why you should not. [/]
[TheOldMan] What harm can I do to you or to her? ” [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman looked in the old man ’s face. [/]
[TheReddleman] “ Well, sir, ” he said at last, “ I knew her before today, though perhaps it would have been better if I had not. [/]
[Narrator] But she ’s nothing to me, and I am nothing to her; and she would n’t have been in my van if any better carriage had been there to take her. ” “ Where, may I ask? ” “ At Anglebury. ” [/]
[TheOldMan] “ I know the town well. [/]
[Narrator] What was she doing there? ” “ Oh, not much — to gossip about. [/]
[TheReddleman] However, she ’s tired to death now, and not at all well, and that ’s what makes her so restless. [/]
[TheReddleman] She dropped off into a nap about an hour ago, and ’ twill do her good. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ A nice - looking girl, no doubt? ” “ You would say so. ” [/]
[TheOtherTraveller] The other traveller turned his eyes with interest towards the van window, and, without withdrawing them, said, “ I presume I might look in upon her? ” [/]
[TheReddleman] “ No, ” said the reddleman abruptly. [/]
[TheReddleman] “ It is getting too dark for you to see much of her; and, more than that, I have no right to allow you. Thank God she sleeps so well, I hope she wo n’t wake till she ’s home. ” [/]
[TheOldMan] “ Who is she? [/]
[Narrator] One of the neighbourhood? ” “ ’ Tis no matter who, excuse me. ” [/]
[TheOldMan] “ It is not that girl of Blooms - End, who has been talked about more or less lately? [/]
[Narrator] If so, I know her; and I can guess what has happened. ” “ ’ Tis no matter. .. . [/]
[TheReddleman] Now, sir, I am sorry to say that we shall soon have to part company. [/]
[TheReddleman] My ponies are tired, and I have further to go, and I am going to rest them under this bank for an hour. ” [/]
[TheOldMan] The elder traveller nodded his head indifferently, and the reddleman turned his horses and van in upon the turf, saying, “ Good night. ” [/]
[Narrator] The old man replied, and proceeded on his way as before. [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman watched his form as it diminished to a speck on the road and became absorbed in the thickening films of night. [/]
[Narrator] He then took some hay from a truss which was slung up under the van, and, throwing a portion of it in front of the horses, made a pad of the rest, which he laid on the ground beside his vehicle. [/]
[Narrator] Upon this he sat down, leaning his back against the wheel. [/]
[Narrator] From the interior a low soft breathing came to his ear. [/]
[Narrator] It appeared to satisfy him, and he musingly surveyed the scene, as if considering the next step that he should take. [/]
[Narrator] To do things musingly, and by small degrees, seemed, indeed, to be a duty in the Egdon valleys at this transitional hour, for there was that in the condition of the heath itself which resembled protracted and halting dubiousness. [/]
[Narrator] It was the quality of the repose appertaining to the scene. [/]
[Narrator] This was not the repose of actual stagnation, but the apparent repose of incredible slowness. [/]
[Narrator] A condition of healthy life so nearly resembling the torpor of death is a noticeable thing of its sort; to exhibit the inertness of the desert, and at the same time to be exercising powers akin to those of the meadow, and even of the forest, awakened in those who thought of it the attentiveness usually engendered by understatement and reserve. [/]
[Narrator] The scene before the reddleman ’s eyes was a gradual series of ascents from the level of the road backward into the heart of the heath. [/]
[Narrator] It embraced hillocks, pits, ridges, acclivities, one behind the other, till all was finished by a high hill cutting against the still light sky. [/]
[Narrator] The traveller ’s eye hovered about these things for a time, and finally settled upon one noteworthy object up there. [/]
[Narrator] It was a barrow. [/]
[Narrator] This bossy projection of earth above its natural level occupied the loftiest ground of the loneliest height that the heath contained. [/]
[Narrator] Although from the vale it appeared but as a wart on an Atlantean brow, its actual bulk was great. [/]
[Narrator] It formed the pole and axis of this heathery world. [/]
[Narrator] As the resting man looked at the barrow he became aware that its summit, hitherto the highest object in the whole prospect round, was surmounted by something higher. [/]
[Narrator] It rose from the semiglobular mound like a spike from a helmet. [/]
[Narrator] The first instinct of an imaginative stranger might have been to suppose it the person of one of the Celts who built the barrow, so far had all of modern date withdrawn from the scene. [/]
[Narrator] It seemed a sort of last man among them, musing for a moment before dropping into eternal night with the rest of his race. [/]
[Narrator] There the form stood, motionless as the hill beneath. [/]
[Narrator] Above the plain rose the hill, above the hill rose the barrow, and above the barrow rose the figure. [/]
[Narrator] Above the figure was nothing that could be mapped elsewhere than on a celestial globe. [/]
[Narrator] Such a perfect, delicate, and necessary finish did the figure give to the dark pile of hills that it seemed to be the only obvious justification of their outline. [/]
[Narrator] Without it, there was the dome without the lantern; with it the architectural demands of the mass were satisfied. [/]
[Narrator] The scene was strangely homogeneous, in that the vale, the upland, the barrow, and the figure above it amounted only to unity. [/]
[Narrator] Looking at this or that member of the group was not observing a complete thing, but a fraction of a thing. [/]
[Narrator] The form was so much like an organic part of the entire motionless structure that to see it move would have impressed the mind as a strange phenomenon. [/]
[Narrator] Immobility being the chief characteristic of that whole which the person formed portion of, the discontinuance of immobility in any quarter suggested confusion. [/]
[Narrator] Yet that is what happened. [/]
[Narrator] The figure perceptibly gave up its fixity, shifted a step or two, and turned round. [/]
[Narrator] As if alarmed, it descended on the right side of the barrow, with the glide of a water - drop down a bud, and then vanished. [/]
[Narrator] The movement had been sufficient to show more clearly the characteristics of the figure, and that it was a woman ’s. [/]
[Narrator] The reason of her sudden displacement now appeared. [/]
[Narrator] With her dropping out of sight on the right side, a newcomer, bearing a burden, protruded into the sky on the left side, ascended the tumulus, and deposited the burden on the top. [/]
[Narrator] A second followed, then a third, a fourth, a fifth, and ultimately the whole barrow was peopled with burdened figures. [/]
[Narrator] The only intelligible meaning in this sky - backed pantomime of silhouettes was that the woman had no relation to the forms who had taken her place, was sedulously avoiding these, and had come thither for another object than theirs. [/]
[Narrator] The imagination of the observer clung by preference to that vanished, solitary figure, as to something more interesting, more important, more likely to have a history worth knowing than these newcomers, and unconsciously regarded them as intruders. [/]
[Narrator] But they remained, and established themselves; and the lonely person who hitherto had been queen of the solitude did not at present seem likely to return. [/]
[Narrator] III. [/]
[Narrator] The Custom of the Country Had a looker - on been posted in the immediate vicinity of the barrow, he would have learned that these persons were boys and men of the neighbouring hamlets. [/]
[Narrator] Each, as he ascended the barrow, had been heavily laden with furze faggots, carried upon the shoulder by means of a long stake sharpened at each end for impaling them easily — two in front and two behind. [/]
[Narrator] They came from a part of the heath a quarter of a mile to the rear, where furze almost exclusively prevailed as a product. [/]
[Narrator] Every individual was so involved in furze by his method of carrying the faggots that he appeared like a bush on legs till he had thrown them down. [/]
[Narrator] The party had marched in trail, like a travelling flock of sheep; that is to say, the strongest first, the weak and young behind. [/]
[Narrator] The loads were all laid together, and a pyramid of furze thirty feet in circumference now occupied the crown of the tumulus, which was known as Rainbarrow for many miles round. [/]
[Narrator] Some made themselves busy with matches, and in selecting the driest tufts of furze, others in loosening the bramble bonds which held the faggots together. [/]
[Narrator] Others, again, while this was in progress, lifted their eyes and swept the vast expanse of country commanded by their position, now lying nearly obliterated by shade. [/]
[Narrator] In the valleys of the heath nothing save its own wild face was visible at any time of day; but this spot commanded a horizon enclosing a tract of far extent, and in many cases lying beyond the heath country. [/]
[Narrator] None of its features could be seen now, but the whole made itself felt as a vague stretch of remoteness. [/]
[Narrator] While the men and lads were building the pile, a change took place in the mass of shade which denoted the distant landscape. [/]
[Narrator] Red suns and tufts of fire one by one began to arise, flecking the whole country round. [/]
[Narrator] They were the bonfires of other parishes and hamlets that were engaged in the same sort of commemoration. [/]
[Narrator] Some were distant, and stood in a dense atmosphere, so that bundles of pale straw - like beams radiated around them in the shape of a fan. [/]
[Narrator] Some were large and near, glowing scarlet - red from the shade, like wounds in a black hide. [/]
[Narrator] Some were Mænades, with winy faces and blown hair. [/]
[Narrator] These tinctured the silent bosom of the clouds above them and lit up their ephemeral caves, which seemed thenceforth to become scalding caldrons. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps as many as thirty bonfires could be counted within the whole bounds of the district; and as the hour may be told on a clock - face when the figures themselves are invisible, so did the men recognize the locality of each fire by its angle and direction, though nothing of the scenery could be viewed. [/]
[Narrator] The first tall flame from Rainbarrow sprang into the sky, attracting all eyes that had been fixed on the distant conflagrations back to their own attempt in the same kind. [/]
[Narrator] The cheerful blaze streaked the inner surface of the human circle — now increased by other stragglers, male and female — with its own gold livery, and even overlaid the dark turf around with a lively luminousness, which softened off into obscurity where the barrow rounded downwards out of sight. [/]
[Narrator] It showed the barrow to be the segment of a globe, as perfect as on the day when it was thrown up, even the little ditch remaining from which the earth was dug. [/]
[Narrator] Not a plough had ever disturbed a grain of that stubborn soil. [/]
[Narrator] In the heath ’s barrenness to the farmer lay its fertility to the historian. [/]
[Narrator] There had been no obliteration, because there had been no tending. [/]
[Narrator] It seemed as if the bonfire - makers were standing in some radiant upper story of the world, detached from and independent of the dark stretches below. [/]
[Narrator] The heath down there was now a vast abyss, and no longer a continuation of what they stood on; for their eyes, adapted to the blaze, could see nothing of the deeps beyond its influence. [/]
[Narrator] Occasionally, it is true, a more vigorous flare than usual from their faggots sent darting lights like aides - de - camp down the inclines to some distant bush, pool, or patch of white sand, kindling these to replies of the same colour, till all was lost in darkness again. [/]
[TheSoulsOfMightyWorthSuspendedTherein] Then the whole black phenomenon beneath represented Limbo as viewed from the brink by the sublime Florentine in his vision, and the muttered articulations of the wind in the hollows were as complaints and petitions from the “ souls of mighty worth ” suspended therein. [/]
[Narrator] It was as if these men and boys had suddenly dived into past ages, and fetched therefrom an hour and deed which had before been familiar with this spot. [/]
[Narrator] The ashes of the original British pyre which blazed from that summit lay fresh and undisturbed in the barrow beneath their tread. [/]
[Narrator] The flames from funeral piles long ago kindled there had shone down upon the lowlands as these were shining now. [/]
[Narrator] Festival fires to Thor and Woden had followed on the same ground and duly had their day. [/]
[Narrator] Indeed, it is pretty well known that such blazes as this the heathmen were now enjoying are rather the lineal descendants from jumbled Druidical rites and Saxon ceremonies than the invention of popular feeling about Gunpowder Plot. [/]
[Narrator] Moreover to light a fire is the instinctive and resistant act of man when, at the winter ingress, the curfew is sounded throughout Nature. [/]
[Narrator] It indicates a spontaneous, Promethean rebelliousness against that fiat that this recurrent season shall bring foul times, cold darkness, misery and death. [/]
[Narrator] Black chaos comes, and the fettered gods of the earth say, Let there be light. [/]
[Narrator] The brilliant lights and sooty shades which struggled upon the skin and clothes of the persons standing round caused their lineaments and general contours to be drawn with Dureresque vigour and dash. [/]
[Narrator] Yet the permanent moral expression of each face it was impossible to discover, for as the nimble flames towered, nodded, and swooped through the surrounding air, [/]
[Narrator] the blots of shade and flakes of light upon the countenances of the group changed shape and position endlessly. [/]
[Narrator] All was unstable; quivering as leaves, evanescent as lightning. [/]
[Narrator] Shadowy eye - sockets, deep as those of a death ’s head, suddenly turned into pits of lustre: a lantern - jaw was cavernous, then it was shining; wrinkles were emphasized to ravines, or obliterated entirely by a changed ray. [/]
[Narrator] Nostrils were dark wells; sinews in old necks were gilt mouldings; things with no particular polish on them were glazed; bright objects, such as the tip of a furze - hook one of the men carried, were as glass; eyeballs glowed like little lanterns. [/]
[Narrator] Those whom Nature had depicted as merely quaint became grotesque, the grotesque became preternatural; for all was in extremity. [/]
[Narrator] Hence it may be that the face of an old man, who had like others been called to the heights by the rising flames, was not really the mere nose and chin that it appeared to be, but an appreciable quantity of human countenance. [/]

[Wildeve] However, then she went on, and that ’s what made me bring up the story, ‘ Well, whatever clothes I ’ve won, white or figured, for eyes to see or for eyes not to see ’ (’ a could do a pretty stroke of modesty in those days), ‘ I ’d sooner have lost it than have seen what I have. [/]
[Wildeve] Poor Mr. Yeobright was took bad directly he reached the fair ground, and was forced to go home again. ’ [/]
[Narrator] That was the last time he ever went out of the parish. ” “ ’ A faltered on from one day to another, and then we heard he was gone. ” [/]
[Christian] “ D’ye think he had great pain when ’ a died? ” said Christian. [/]
[Humphrey] “ O no — quite different. [/]
[Humphrey] Nor any pain of mind. [/]
[Humphrey] He was lucky enough to be God A’mighty ’s own man. ” [/]

[Christian] “ I ’m glad I bain’t, for then ’ twon’t pain me. .. . [/]
[Christian] I do n’t think I be afeard — or if I be I ca n’t help it, and I do n’t deserve to suffer. [/]
[Christian] I wish I was not afeard at all! ” [/]
[Fairway] There was a solemn silence, and looking from the window, which was unshuttered and unblinded, Timothy said, “ Well, what a fess little bonfire that one is, out by Cap’n Vye ’s! ’ [/]
[Fairway] Tis burning just the same now as ever, upon my life. ” All glances went through the window, and nobody noticed that Wildeve disguised a brief, telltale look. [/]
[Narrator] Far away up the sombre valley of heath, and to the right of Rainbarrow, could indeed be seen the light, small, but steady and persistent as before. [/]
[Narrator] “ It was lighted before ours was, ” Fairway continued; “ and yet every one in the country round is out afore ’ n. ” “ Perhaps there ’s meaning in it! ” [/]
[Narrator] murmured Christian. [/]
[Wildeve] “ How meaning? ” said Wildeve sharply. [/]
[Narrator] Christian was too scattered to reply, and Timothy helped him. [/]
[Fairway] “ He means, sir, that the lonesome dark - eyed creature up there that some say is a witch — ever I should call a fine young woman such a name — is always up to some odd conceit or other; and so perhaps ’ tis she. ” [/]
[GrandferCantle] “ I ’d be very glad to ask her in wedlock, if she ’d hae me and take the risk of her wild dark eyes ill - wishing me, ” said Grandfer Cantle staunchly. [/]
[Christian] “ Do n’t ye say it, Father! ” [/]
[Narrator] implored Christian. [/]
[Fairway] “ Well, be dazed if he who do marry the maid wo n’t hae an uncommon picture for his best parlour, ” said Fairway in a liquid tone, placing down the cup of mead at the end of a good pull. [/]
[Sam] “ And a partner as deep as the North Star, ” said Sam, taking up the cup and finishing the little that remained. [/]
[Humphrey] “ Well, really, now I think we must be moving, ” said Humphrey, observing the emptiness of the vessel. [/]
[GrandferCantle] “ But we ’ll gie ’em another song? ” said Grandfer Cantle. [/]
[Narrator] “ I ’m as full of notes as a bird! ” “ Thank you, Grandfer, ” said Wildeve. [/]
[Wildeve] “ But we will not trouble you now. [/]
[Narrator] Some other day must do for that — when I have a party. ” “ Be jown’d if I do n’t learn ten new songs for’t, or I wo n’t learn a line! ” said Grandfer Cantle. [/]

[Narrator] All then took their leave, wishing their entertainer long life and happiness as a married man, with recapitulations which occupied some time. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve attended them to the door, beyond which the deep - dyed upward stretch of heath stood awaiting them, an amplitude of darkness reigning from their feet almost to the zenith, where a definite form first became visible in the lowering forehead of Rainbarrow. [/]
[Narrator] Diving into the dense obscurity in a line headed by Sam the turf - cutter, they pursued their trackless way home. [/]
[Narrator] When the scratching of the furze against their leggings had fainted upon the ear, Wildeve returned to the room where he had left Thomasin and her aunt. [/]
[Narrator] The women were gone. [/]
[Narrator] They could only have left the house in one way, by the back window; and this was open. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve laughed to himself, remained a moment thinking, and idly returned to the front room. [/]
[Narrator] Here his glance fell upon a bottle of wine which stood on the mantelpiece. [/]
[Wildeve] “ Ah — old Dowden! ” [/]
[Wildeve] he murmured; and going to the kitchen door shouted, “ Is anybody here who can take something to old Dowden? ” There was no reply. [/]
[Narrator] The room was empty, the lad who acted as his factotum having gone to bed. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve came back, put on his hat, took the bottle, and left the house, turning the key in the door, for there was no guest at the inn tonight. [/]
[Narrator] As soon as he was on the road the little bonfire on Mistover Knap again met his eye. [/]
[Wildeve] “ Still waiting, are you, my lady? ” [/]
[Narrator] he murmured. [/]
[Narrator] However, he did not proceed that way just then; but leaving the hill to the left of him, he stumbled over a rutted road that brought him to a cottage which, like all other habitations on the heath at this hour, was only saved from being visible by a faint shine from its bedroom window. [/]
[Narrator] This house was the home of Olly Dowden, the besom - maker, and he entered. [/]
[Narrator] The lower room was in darkness; but by feeling his way he found a table, whereon he placed the bottle, and a minute later emerged again upon the heath. [/]
[Narrator] He stood and looked northeast at the undying little fire — high up above him, though not so high as Rainbarrow. [/]
[Narrator] We have been told what happens when a woman deliberates; and the epigram is not always terminable with woman, provided that one be in the case, and that a fair one. [/]
[Wildeve] Wildeve stood, and stood longer, and breathed perplexedly, and then said to himself with resignation, “ Yes — by Heaven, I must go to her, I suppose! ” [/]
[Narrator] Instead of turning in the direction of home he pressed on rapidly by a path under Rainbarrow towards what was evidently a signal light. VI. [/]
[Narrator] The Figure against the Sky When the whole Egdon concourse had left the site of the bonfire to its accustomed loneliness, a closely wrapped female figure approached the barrow from that quarter of the heath in which the little fire lay. [/]
[Narrator] Had the reddleman been watching he might have recognized her as the woman who had first stood there so singularly, and vanished at the approach of strangers. [/]
[Narrator] She ascended to her old position at the top, where the red coals of the perishing fire greeted her like living eyes in the corpse of day. [/]
[Narrator] There she stood still around her stretching the vast night atmosphere, whose incomplete darkness in comparison with the total darkness of the heath below it might have represented a venial beside a mortal sin. [/]
[Narrator] That she was tall and straight in build, that she was lady - like in her movements, was all that could be learnt of her just now, her form being wrapped in a shawl folded in the old cornerwise fashion, and her head in a large kerchief, a protection not superfluous at this hour and place. [/]
[Narrator] Her back was towards the wind, which blew from the northwest; but whether she had avoided that aspect because of the chilly gusts which played about her exceptional position, or because her interest lay in the southeast, did not at first appear. [/]
[Narrator] Her reason for standing so dead still as the pivot of this circle of heath - country was just as obscure. [/]
[Narrator] Her extraordinary fixity, her conspicuous loneliness, her heedlessness of night, betokened among other things an utter absence of fear. [/]
[Narrator] A tract of country unaltered from that sinister condition which made Cæsar anxious every year to get clear of its glooms before the autumnal equinox, a kind of landscape and weather which leads travellers from the South to describe our island as Homer ’s Cimmerian land, was not, on the face of it, friendly to women. [/]
[Narrator] It might reasonably have been supposed that she was listening to the wind, which rose somewhat as the night advanced, and laid hold of the attention. [/]
[Narrator] The wind, indeed, seemed made for the scene, as the scene seemed made for the hour. [/]
[Narrator] Part of its tone was quite special; what was heard there could be heard nowhere else. [/]
[Narrator] Gusts in innumerable series followed each other from the northwest, and when each one of them raced past the sound of its progress resolved into three. [/]
[Narrator] Treble, tenor, and bass notes were to be found therein. [/]
[Narrator] The general ricochet of the whole over pits and prominences had the gravest pitch of the chime. [/]
[Narrator] Next there could be heard the baritone buzz of a holly tree. [/]
[Narrator] Below these in force, above them in pitch, a dwindled voice strove hard at a husky tune, which was the peculiar local sound alluded to. [/]
[Narrator] Thinner and less immediately traceable than the other two, it was far more impressive than either. [/]
[Narrator] In it lay what may be called the linguistic peculiarity of the heath; and being audible nowhere on earth off a heath, it afforded a shadow of reason for the woman ’s tenseness, which continued as unbroken as ever. [/]
[Narrator] Throughout the blowing of these plaintive November winds that note bore a great resemblance to the ruins of human song which remain to the throat of fourscore and ten. [/]
[Narrator] It was a worn whisper, dry and papery, and it brushed so distinctly across the ear that, by the accustomed, the material minutiæ in which it originated could be realized as by touch. [/]
[Narrator] It was the united products of infinitesimal vegetable causes, and these were neither stems, leaves, fruit, blades, prickles, lichen, nor moss. [/]
[Narrator] They were the mummied heathbells of the past summer, originally tender and purple, now washed colourless by Michaelmas rains, and dried to dead skins by October suns. [/]
[Narrator] So low was an individual sound from these that a combination of hundreds only just emerged from silence, and the myriads of the whole declivity reached the woman ’s ear but as a shrivelled and intermittent recitative. [/]
[Narrator] Yet scarcely a single accent among the many afloat tonight could have such power to impress a listener with thoughts of its origin. [/]
[Narrator] One inwardly saw the infinity of those combined multitudes; and perceived that each of the tiny trumpets was seized on entered, scoured and emerged from by the wind as thoroughly as if it were as vast as a crater. [/]
[AListener] “ The spirit moved them. ” [/]
[Narrator] A meaning of the phrase forced itself upon the attention; and an emotional listener ’s fetichistic mood might have ended in one of more advanced quality. [/]
[Narrator] It was not, after all, that the left - hand expanse of old blooms spoke, or the right - hand, or those of the slope in front; but it was the single person of something else speaking through each at once. [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly, on the barrow, there mingled with all this wild rhetoric of night a sound which modulated so naturally into the rest that its beginning and ending were hardly to be distinguished. [/]
[Narrator] The bluffs, and the bushes, and the heather - bells had broken silence; at last, so did the woman; and her articulation was but as another phrase of the same discourse as theirs. [/]
[Narrator] Thrown out on the winds it became twined in with them, and with them it flew away. [/]
[Narrator] What she uttered was a lengthened sighing, apparently at something in her mind which had led to her presence here. [/]
[Narrator] There was a spasmodic abandonment about it as if, in allowing herself to utter the sound the woman ’s brain had authorized what it could not regulate. [/]
[Narrator] One point was evident in this; that she had been existing in a suppressed state, and not in one of languor, or stagnation. [/]
[Narrator] Far away down the valley the faint shine from the window of the inn still lasted on; and a few additional moments proved that the window, or what was within it, had more to do with the woman ’s sigh than had either her own actions or the scene immediately around. [/]
[Narrator] She lifted her left hand, which held a closed telescope. [/]
[Narrator] This she rapidly extended, as if she were well accustomed to the operation, and raising it to her eye directed it towards the light beaming from the inn. [/]
[Narrator] The handkerchief which had hooded her head was now a little thrown back, her face being somewhat elevated. [/]
[Narrator] A profile was visible against the dull monochrome of cloud around her; and it was as though side shadows from the features of Sappho and Mrs. Siddons had converged upwards from the tomb to form an image like neither but suggesting both. [/]
[Narrator] This, however, was mere superficiality. [/]
[Narrator] In respect of character a face may make certain admissions by its outline; but it fully confesses only in its changes. [/]
[Narrator] So much is this the case that what is called the play of the features often helps more in understanding a man or woman than the earnest labours of all the other members together. [/]
[Narrator] Thus the night revealed little of her whose form it was embracing, for the mobile parts of her countenance could not be seen. [/]
[Narrator] At last she gave up her spying attitude, closed the telescope, and turned to the decaying embers. [/]
[Narrator] From these no appreciable beams now radiated, except when a more than usually smart gust brushed over their faces and raised a fitful glow which came and went like the blush of a girl. [/]
[Narrator] She stooped over the silent circle, and selecting from the brands a piece of stick which bore the largest live coal at its end, brought it to where she had been standing before. [/]
[Narrator] She held the brand to the ground, blowing the red coal with her mouth at the same time; till it faintly illuminated the sod, and revealed a small object, which turned out to be an hourglass, though she wore a watch. [/]
[Narrator] She blew long enough to show that the sand had all slipped through. [/]
[ACloselyWrappedFemaleFigure] “ Ah! ” [/]
[Narrator] she said, as if surprised. [/]
[Narrator] The light raised by her breath had been very fitful, and a momentary irradiation of flesh was all that it had disclosed of her face. [/]
[Narrator] That consisted of two matchless lips and a cheek only, her head being still enveloped. [/]
[Narrator] She threw away the stick, took the glass in her hand, the telescope under her arm, and moved on. [/]
[Narrator] Along the ridge ran a faint foot - track, which the lady followed. [/]
[Narrator] Those who knew it well called it a path; and, while a mere visitor would have passed it unnoticed even by day, the regular haunters of the heath were at no loss for it at midnight. [/]
[Narrator] The whole secret of following these incipient paths, when there was not light enough in the atmosphere to show a turnpike road, lay in the development of the sense of touch in the feet, which comes with years of night - rambling in little - trodden spots. [/]
[Narrator] To a walker practised in such places a difference between impact on maiden herbage, and on the crippled stalks of a slight footway, is perceptible through the thickest boot or shoe. [/]
[Narrator] The solitary figure who walked this beat took no notice of the windy tune still played on the dead heathbells. [/]
[Narrator] She did not turn her head to look at a group of dark creatures further on, who fled from her presence as she skirted a ravine where they fed. [/]
[Narrator] They were about a score of the small wild ponies known as heath - croppers. [/]
[Narrator] They roamed at large on the undulations of Egdon, but in numbers too few to detract much from the solitude. [/]
[Narrator] The pedestrian noticed nothing just now, and a clue to her abstraction was afforded by a trivial incident. [/]
[Narrator] A bramble caught hold of her skirt, and checked her progress. [/]
[Narrator] Instead of putting it off and hastening along, she yielded herself up to the pull, and stood passively still. [/]
[Narrator] When she began to extricate herself it was by turning round and round, and so unwinding the prickly switch. [/]
[Narrator] She was in a desponding reverie. [/]
[Narrator] Her course was in the direction of the small undying fire which had drawn the attention of the men on Rainbarrow and of Wildeve in the valley below. [/]
[Narrator] A faint illumination from its rays began to glow upon her face, and the fire soon revealed itself to be lit, not on the level ground, but on a salient corner or redan of earth, at the junction of two converging bank fences. [/]
[Narrator] Outside was a ditch, dry except immediately under the fire, where there was a large pool, bearded all round by heather and rushes. [/]
[Narrator] In the smooth water of the pool the fire appeared upside down. [/]
[Narrator] The banks meeting behind were bare of a hedge, save such as was formed by disconnected tufts of furze, standing upon stems along the top, like impaled heads above a city wall. [/]
[Narrator] A white mast, fitted up with spars and other nautical tackle, could be seen rising against the dark clouds whenever the flames played brightly enough to reach it. [/]
[Narrator] Altogether the scene had much the appearance of a fortification upon which had been kindled a beacon fire. [/]
[Narrator] Nobody was visible; but ever and anon a whitish something moved above the bank from behind, and vanished again. [/]
[Narrator] This was a small human hand, in the act of lifting pieces of fuel into the fire, but for all that could be seen the hand, like that which troubled Belshazzar, was there alone. [/]
[Narrator] Occasionally an ember rolled off the bank, and dropped with a hiss into the pool. [/]
[Narrator] At one side of the pool rough steps built of clods enabled everyone who wished to do so to mount the bank; which the woman did. [/]
[Narrator] Within was a paddock in an uncultivated state, though bearing evidence of having once been tilled; but the heath and fern had insidiously crept in, and were reasserting their old supremacy. [/]
[Narrator] Further ahead were dimly visible an irregular dwelling - house, garden, and outbuildings, backed by a clump of firs. [/]
[Narrator] The young lady — for youth had revealed its presence in her buoyant bound up the bank — walked along the top instead of descending inside, and came to the corner where the fire was burning. [/]
[Narrator] One reason for the permanence of the blaze was now manifest: the fuel consisted of hard pieces of wood, cleft and sawn — the knotty boles of old thorn trees which grew in twos and threes about the hillsides. [/]
[Narrator] A yet unconsumed pile of these lay in the inner angle of the bank; and from this corner the upturned face of a little boy greeted her eyes. [/]
[Narrator] He was dilatorily throwing up a piece of wood into the fire every now and then, a business which seemed to have engaged him a considerable part of the evening, for his face was somewhat weary. [/]
[ALittleBoy] “ I am glad you have come, Miss Eustacia, ” he said, with a sigh of relief. [/]

[AnOldMan] “ Nonsense. [/]

[Narrator] The little slave went on feeding the fire as before. [/]

[Narrator] It was to the full as lonely a place as Rainbarrow, though at rather a lower level; and it was more sheltered from wind and weather on account of the few firs to the north. [/]
[Narrator] The bank which enclosed the homestead, and protected it from the lawless state of the world without, was formed of thick square clods, dug from the ditch on the outside, and built up with a slight batter or incline, which forms no slight defense where hedges will not grow because of the wind and the wilderness, and where wall materials are unattainable. [/]
[Narrator] Otherwise the situation was quite open, commanding the whole length of the valley which reached to the river behind Wildeve ’s house. [/]
[Narrator] High above this to the right, and much nearer thitherward than the Quiet Woman Inn, the blurred contour of Rainbarrow obstructed the sky. [/]

[Narrator] The words were not without emotion, and retained their level tone as if by a careful equipoise between imminent extremes. [/]
[Narrator] At this unexpectedly repressing manner in her lover the girl seemed to repress herself also. [/]

[Narrator] he said touchily. [/]

[Narrator] Eustacia sighed — it was no fragile maiden sigh, but a sigh which shook her like a shiver. [/]

[Narrator] She knew that he trifled with her; but she loved on. [/]
[Narrator] She scattered the half - burnt brands, went indoors immediately, and up to her bedroom without a light. [/]

[Narrator] VII. [/]
[Narrator] Queen of Night Eustacia Vye was the raw material of a divinity. [/]
[Narrator] On Olympus she would have done well with a little preparation. [/]
[Narrator] She had the passions and instincts which make a model goddess, that is, those which make not quite a model woman. [/]
[Narrator] Had it been possible for the earth and mankind to be entirely in her grasp for a while, she had handled the distaff, the spindle, and the shears at her own free will, few in the world would have noticed the change of government. [/]
[Narrator] There would have been the same inequality of lot, the same heaping up of favours here, of contumely there, the same generosity before justice, the same perpetual dilemmas, the same captious alteration of caresses and blows that we endure now. [/]
[Narrator] She was in person full - limbed and somewhat heavy; without ruddiness, as without pallor; and soft to the touch as a cloud. [/]
[Narrator] To see her hair was to fancy that a whole winter did not contain darkness enough to form its shadow — it closed over her forehead like nightfall extinguishing the western glow. [/]
[Narrator] Her nerves extended into those tresses, and her temper could always be softened by stroking them down. [/]
[Narrator] When her hair was brushed she would instantly sink into stillness and look like the Sphinx. [/]
[Narrator] If, in passing under one of the Egdon banks, any of its thick skeins were caught, as they sometimes were, by a prickly tuft of the large _ Ulex Europæus_—which will act as a sort of hairbrush — she would go back a few steps, and pass against it a second time. [/]
[Narrator] She had pagan eyes, full of nocturnal mysteries, and their light, as it came and went, and came again, was partially hampered by their oppressive lids and lashes; and of these the under lid was much fuller than it usually is with English women. [/]
[Narrator] This enabled her to indulge in reverie without seeming to do so — she might have been believed capable of sleeping without closing them up. [/]
[Narrator] Assuming that the souls of men and women were visible essences, you could fancy the colour of Eustacia ’s soul to be flamelike. [/]
[Narrator] The sparks from it that rose into her dark pupils gave the same impression. [/]
[Narrator] The mouth seemed formed less to speak than to quiver, less to quiver than to kiss. [/]
[Narrator] Some might have added, less to kiss than to curl. [/]
[Narrator] Viewed sideways, the closing - line of her lips formed, with almost geometric precision, the curve so well known in the arts of design as the cima - recta, or ogee. [/]
[Narrator] The sight of such a flexible bend as that on grim Egdon was quite an apparition. [/]
[Narrator] It was felt at once that the mouth did not come over from Sleswig with a band of Saxon pirates whose lips met like the two halves of a muffin. [/]
[Narrator] One had fancied that such lip - curves were mostly lurking underground in the South as fragments of forgotten marbles. [/]
[Narrator] So fine were the lines of her lips that, though full, each corner of her mouth was as clearly cut as the point of a spear. [/]
[Narrator] This keenness of corner was only blunted when she was given over to sudden fits of gloom, one of the phases of the night - side of sentiment which she knew too well for her years. [/]
[Narrator] Her presence brought memories of such things as Bourbon roses, rubies, and tropical midnight; her moods recalled lotus - eaters and the march in Athalie; her motions, the ebb and flow of the sea; her voice, the viola. [/]
[Narrator] In a dim light, and with a slight rearrangement of her hair, her general figure might have stood for that of either of the higher female deities. [/]
[Narrator] The new moon behind her head, an old helmet upon it, a diadem of accidental dewdrops round her brow, would have been adjuncts sufficient to strike the note of Artemis, Athena, or Hera respectively, with as close an approximation to the antique as that which passes muster on many respected canvases. [/]
[Narrator] But celestial imperiousness, love, wrath, and fervour had proved to be somewhat thrown away on netherward Egdon. [/]
[Narrator] Her power was limited, and the consciousness of this limitation had biassed her development. [/]
[Narrator] Egdon was her Hades, and since coming there she had imbibed much of what was dark in its tone, though inwardly and eternally unreconciled thereto. [/]
[Narrator] Her appearance accorded well with this smouldering rebelliousness, and the shady splendour of her beauty was the real surface of the sad and stifled warmth within her. [/]
[Narrator] A true Tartarean dignity sat upon her brow, and not factitiously or with marks of constraint, for it had grown in her with years. [/]
[Narrator] Across the upper part of her head she wore a thin fillet of black velvet, restraining the luxuriance of her shady hair, in a way which added much to this class of majesty by irregularly clouding her forehead. [/]
[Richter] “ Nothing can embellish a beautiful face more than a narrow band drawn over the brow, ” says Richter. [/]
[Narrator] Some of the neighbouring girls wore coloured ribbon for the same purpose, and sported metallic ornaments elsewhere; but if anyone suggested coloured ribbon and metallic ornaments to Eustacia Vye she laughed and went on. [/]
[Narrator] Why did a woman of this sort live on Egdon Heath? [/]
[Narrator] Budmouth was her native place, a fashionable seaside resort at that date. [/]
[Narrator] She was the daughter of the bandmaster of a regiment which had been quartered there — a Corfiote by birth, and a fine musician — who met his future wife during her trip thither with her father the captain, a man of good family. [/]
[Narrator] The marriage was scarcely in accord with the old man ’s wishes, for the bandmaster ’s pockets were as light as his occupation. [/]
[Narrator] But the musician did his best; adopted his wife ’s name, made England permanently his home, took great trouble with his child ’s education, the expenses of which were defrayed by the grandfather, and throve as the chief local musician till her mother ’s death, when he left off thriving, drank, and died also. [/]
[Narrator] The girl was left to the care of her grandfather, who, since three of his ribs became broken in a shipwreck, had lived in this airy perch on Egdon, a spot which had taken his fancy because the house was to be had for next to nothing, and because a remote blue tinge on the horizon between the hills, visible from the cottage door, was traditionally believed to be the English Channel. [/]
[Narrator] She hated the change; she felt like one banished; but here she was forced to abide. [/]
[Narrator] Thus it happened that in Eustacia ’s brain were juxtaposed the strangest assortment of ideas, from old time and from new. [/]
[Narrator] There was no middle distance in her perspective — romantic recollections of sunny afternoons on an esplanade, with military bands, officers, and gallants around, stood like gilded letters upon the dark tablet of surrounding Egdon. [/]
[Narrator] Every bizarre effect that could result from the random intertwining of watering - place glitter with the grand solemnity of a heath, was to be found in her. [/]
[Narrator] Seeing nothing of human life now, she imagined all the more of what she had seen. [/]
[Narrator] Where did her dignity come from? [/]
[Narrator] By a latent vein from Alcinous ’ line, her father hailing from Phæacia ’s isle? —or from Fitzalan and De Vere, her maternal grandfather having had a cousin in the peerage? [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps it was the gift of Heaven — a happy convergence of natural laws. [/]
[Narrator] Among other things opportunity had of late years been denied her of learning to be undignified, for she lived lonely. [/]
[Narrator] Isolation on a heath renders vulgarity well - nigh impossible. [/]
[Narrator] It would have been as easy for the heath - ponies, bats, and snakes to be vulgar as for her. [/]
[Narrator] A narrow life in Budmouth might have completely demeaned her. [/]
[Narrator] The only way to look queenly without realms or hearts to queen it over is to look as if you had lost them; and Eustacia did that to a triumph. [/]
[Narrator] In the captain ’s cottage she could suggest mansions she had never seen. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps that was because she frequented a vaster mansion than any of them, the open hills. [/]
[TheGirl] Like the summer condition of the place around her, she was an embodiment of the phrase “ a populous solitude”—apparently so listless, void, and quiet, she was really busy and full. [/]
[Narrator] To be loved to madness — such was her great desire. [/]
[Narrator] Love was to her the one cordial which could drive away the eating loneliness of her days. [/]
[Narrator] And she seemed to long for the abstraction called passionate love more than for any particular lover. [/]
[Narrator] She could show a most reproachful look at times, but it was directed less against human beings than against certain creatures of her mind, the chief of these being Destiny, through whose interference she dimly fancied it arose that love alighted only on gliding youth — that any love she might win would sink simultaneously with the sand in the glass. [/]
[Narrator] She thought of it with an ever - growing consciousness of cruelty, which tended to breed actions of reckless unconventionality, framed to snatch a year ’s, a week ’s, even an hour ’s passion from anywhere while it could be won. [/]
[Narrator] Through want of it she had sung without being merry, possessed without enjoying, outshone without triumphing. [/]
[Narrator] Her loneliness deepened her desire. [/]
[Narrator] On Egdon, coldest and meanest kisses were at famine prices, and where was a mouth matching hers to be found? [/]
[Narrator] Fidelity in love for fidelity ’s sake had less attraction for her than for most women; fidelity because of love ’s grip had much. [/]
[Narrator] A blaze of love, and extinction, was better than a lantern glimmer of the same which should last long years. [/]
[Narrator] On this head she knew by prevision what most women learn only by experience — she had mentally walked round love, told the towers thereof, considered its palaces, and concluded that love was but a doleful joy. [/]
[Narrator] Yet she desired it, as one in a desert would be thankful for brackish water. [/]
[Narrator] She often repeated her prayers; not at particular times, but, like the unaffectedly devout, when she desired to pray. [/]
[TheGirl] Her prayer was always spontaneous, and often ran thus, “ O deliver my heart from this fearful gloom and loneliness; send me great love from somewhere, else I shall die. ” [/]
[Narrator] Her high gods were William the Conqueror, Strafford, and Napoleon Buonaparte, as they had appeared in the Lady ’s History used at the establishment in which she was educated. [/]
[Narrator] Had she been a mother she would have christened her boys such names as Saul or Sisera in preference to Jacob or David, neither of whom she admired. [/]
[Narrator] At school she had used to side with the Philistines in several battles, and had wondered if Pontius Pilate were as handsome as he was frank and fair. [/]
[Narrator] Thus she was a girl of some forwardness of mind, indeed, weighed in relation to her situation among the very rearward of thinkers, very original. [/]
[Narrator] Her instincts towards social non - comformity were at the root of this. [/]
[Narrator] In the matter of holidays, her mood was that of horses who, when turned out to grass, enjoy looking upon their kind at work on the highway. [/]
[Narrator] She only valued rest to herself when it came in the midst of other people ’s labour. [/]
[Narrator] Hence she hated Sundays when all was at rest, and often said they would be the death of her. [/]
[Narrator] To see the heathmen in their Sunday condition, that is, with their hands in their pockets, their boots newly oiled, and not laced up (a particularly Sunday sign), walking leisurely among the turves and furze - faggots they had cut during the week, and kicking them critically as if their use were unknown, was a fearful heaviness to her. [/]
[Narrator] To relieve the tedium of this untimely day she would overhaul the cupboards containing her grandfather ’s old charts and other rubbish, humming Saturday - night ballads of the country people the while. [/]
[Narrator] But on Saturday nights she would frequently sing a psalm, and it was always on a weekday that she read the Bible, that she might be unoppressed with a sense of doing her duty. [/]
[Narrator] Such views of life were to some extent the natural begettings of her situation upon her nature. [/]
[Narrator] To dwell on a heath without studying its meanings was like wedding a foreigner without learning his tongue. [/]
[Narrator] The subtle beauties of the heath were lost to Eustacia; she only caught its vapours. [/]
[Narrator] An environment which would have made a contented woman a poet, a suffering woman a devotee, a pious woman a psalmist, even a giddy woman thoughtful, made a rebellious woman saturnine. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia had got beyond the vision of some marriage of inexpressible glory; yet, though her emotions were in full vigour, she cared for no meaner union. [/]
[Narrator] Thus we see her in a strange state of isolation. [/]
[Narrator] To have lost the godlike conceit that we may do what we will, and not to have acquired a homely zest for doing what we can, shows a grandeur of temper which can not be objected to in the abstract, for it denotes a mind that, though disappointed, forswears compromise. [/]
[Narrator] But, if congenial to philosophy, it is apt to be dangerous to the commonwealth. [/]
[Narrator] In a world where doing means marrying, and the commonwealth is one of hearts and hands, the same peril attends the condition. [/]
[Narrator] And so we see our Eustacia — for at times she was not altogether unlovable — arriving at that stage of enlightenment which feels that nothing is worth while, and filling up the spare hours of her existence by idealizing Wildeve for want of a better object. [/]
[Narrator] This was the sole reason of his ascendency: she knew it herself. [/]
[Narrator] At moments her pride rebelled against her passion for him, and she even had longed to be free. [/]
[Narrator] But there was only one circumstance which could dislodge him, and that was the advent of a greater man. [/]
[Narrator] For the rest, she suffered much from depression of spirits, and took slow walks to recover them, in which she carried her grandfather ’s telescope and her grandmother ’s hourglass — the latter because of a peculiar pleasure she derived from watching a material representation of time ’s gradual glide away. [/]
[Narrator] She seldom schemed, but when she did scheme, her plans showed rather the comprehensive strategy of a general than the small arts called womanish, though she could utter oracles of Delphian ambiguity when she did not choose to be direct. [/]
[Narrator] In heaven she will probably sit between the Héloïses and the Cleopatras. VIII. [/]
[Narrator] Those Who Are Found Where There Is Said to Be Nobody As soon as the sad little boy had withdrawn from the fire he clasped the money tight in the palm of his hand, as if thereby to fortify his courage, and began to run. [/]
[Narrator] There was really little danger in allowing a child to go home alone on this part of Egdon Heath. [/]
[Narrator] The distance to the boy ’s house was not more than three - eighths of a mile, his father ’s cottage, and one other a few yards further on, forming part of the small hamlet of Mistover Knap: the third and only remaining house was that of Captain Vye and Eustacia, which stood quite away from the small cottages and was the loneliest of lonely houses on these thinly populated slopes. [/]
[Narrator] He ran until he was out of breath, and then, becoming more courageous, walked leisurely along, singing in an old voice a little song about a sailor - boy and a fair one, and bright gold in store. [/]
[Narrator] In the middle of this the child stopped — from a pit under the hill ahead of him shone a light, whence proceeded a cloud of floating dust and a smacking noise. [/]
[Narrator] Only unusual sights and sounds frightened the boy. [/]
[Narrator] The shrivelled voice of the heath did not alarm him, for that was familiar. [/]
[Narrator] The thornbushes which arose in his path from time to time were less satisfactory, for they whistled gloomily, and had a ghastly habit after dark of putting on the shapes of jumping madmen, sprawling giants, and hideous cripples. [/]
[Narrator] Lights were not uncommon this evening, but the nature of all of them was different from this. [/]
[Narrator] Discretion rather than terror prompted the boy to turn back instead of passing the light, with a view of asking Miss Eustacia Vye to let her servant accompany him home. [/]
[Narrator] When the boy had reascended to the top of the valley he found the fire to be still burning on the bank, though lower than before. [/]
[Narrator] Beside it, instead of Eustacia ’s solitary form, he saw two persons, the second being a man. [/]
[Narrator] The boy crept along under the bank to ascertain from the nature of the proceedings if it would be prudent to interrupt so splendid a creature as Miss Eustacia on his poor trivial account. [/]
[Narrator] After listening under the bank for some minutes to the talk he turned in a perplexed and doubting manner and began to withdraw as silently as he had come. [/]
[Narrator] That he did not, upon the whole, think it advisable to interrupt her conversation with Wildeve, without being prepared to bear the whole weight of her displeasure, was obvious. [/]
[Narrator] Here was a Scyllæo - Charybdean position for a poor boy. [/]
[Narrator] Pausing when again safe from discovery, he finally decided to face the pit phenomenon as the lesser evil. [/]
[Narrator] With a heavy sigh he retraced the slope, and followed the path he had followed before. [/]
[Narrator] The light had gone, the rising dust had disappeared — he hoped for ever. [/]
[Narrator] He marched resolutely along, and found nothing to alarm him till, coming within a few yards of the sandpit, he heard a slight noise in front, which led him to halt. [/]
[Narrator] The halt was but momentary, for the noise resolved itself into the steady bites of two animals grazing. [/]
[TheBoy] “ Two he’th - croppers down here, ” he said aloud. [/]
[TheBoy] “ I have never known ’em come down so far afore. ” [/]
[Narrator] The animals were in the direct line of his path, but that the child thought little of; he had played round the fetlocks of horses from his infancy. [/]
[Narrator] On coming nearer, however, the boy was somewhat surprised to find that the little creatures did not run off, and that each wore a clog, to prevent his going astray; this signified that they had been broken in. [/]
[Narrator] He could now see the interior of the pit, which, being in the side of the hill, had a level entrance. [/]
[Narrator] In the innermost corner the square outline of a van appeared, with its back towards him. [/]
[Narrator] A light came from the interior, and threw a moving shadow upon the vertical face of gravel at the further side of the pit into which the vehicle faced. [/]
[Narrator] The child assumed that this was the cart of a gipsy, and his dread of those wanderers reached but to that mild pitch which titillates rather than pains. [/]
[Narrator] Only a few inches of mud wall kept him and his family from being gipsies themselves. [/]

[Narrator] The boy knew too well for his peace of mind upon whose lair he had lighted. [/]
[Narrator] Uglier persons than gipsies were known to cross Egdon at times, and a reddleman was one of them. [/]
