[Narrator] IX. [/]
[Narrator] Love Leads a Shrewd Man into Strategy Reddlemen of the old school are now but seldom seen. [/]
[Narrator] Since the introduction of railways Wessex farmers have managed to do without these Mephistophelian visitants, and the bright pigment so largely used by shepherds in preparing sheep for the fair is obtained by other routes. [/]
[Narrator] Even those who yet survive are losing the poetry of existence which characterized them when the pursuit of the trade meant periodical journeys to the pit whence the material was dug, a regular camping out from month to month, except in the depth of winter, a peregrination among farms which could be counted by the hundred, and in spite of this Arab existence the preservation of that respectability which is insured by the never - failing production of a well - lined purse. [/]
[Narrator] Reddle spreads its lively hues over everything it lights on, and stamps unmistakably, as with the mark of Cain, any person who has handled it half an hour. [/]
[Narrator] A child ’s first sight of a reddleman was an epoch in his life. [/]
[Narrator] That blood - coloured figure was a sublimation of all the horrid dreams which had afflicted the juvenile spirit since imagination began. [/]
[AReddleman] “ The reddleman is coming for you! ” had been the formulated threat of Wessex mothers for many generations. [/]
[Narrator] He was successfully supplanted for a while, at the beginning of the present century, by Buonaparte; but as process of time rendered the latter personage stale and ineffective the older phrase resumed its early prominence. [/]
[Narrator] And now the reddleman has in his turn followed Buonaparte to the land of worn - out bogeys, and his place is filled by modern inventions. [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman lived like a gipsy; but gipsies he scorned. [/]
[Narrator] He was about as thriving as travelling basket and mat makers; but he had nothing to do with them. [/]
[Narrator] He was more decently born and brought up than the cattledrovers who passed and repassed him in his wanderings; but they merely nodded to him. [/]
[Narrator] His stock was more valuable than that of pedlars; but they did not think so, and passed his cart with eyes straight ahead. [/]
[Narrator] He was such an unnatural colour to look at that the men of roundabouts and waxwork shows seemed gentlemen beside him; but he considered them low company, and remained aloof. [/]
[Narrator] Among all these squatters and folks of the road the reddleman continually found himself; yet he was not of them. [/]
[Narrator] His occupation tended to isolate him, and isolated he was mostly seen to be. [/]
[Narrator] It was sometimes suggested that reddlemen were criminals for whose misdeeds other men wrongfully suffered — that in escaping the law they had not escaped their own consciences, and had taken to the trade as a lifelong penance. [/]
[Narrator] Else why should they have chosen it? [/]
[Narrator] In the present case such a question would have been particularly apposite. [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman who had entered Egdon that afternoon was an instance of the pleasing being wasted to form the ground - work of the singular, when an ugly foundation would have done just as well for that purpose. [/]
[Narrator] The one point that was forbidding about this reddleman was his colour. [/]
[Narrator] Freed from that he would have been as agreeable a specimen of rustic manhood as one would often see. [/]
[Narrator] A keen observer might have been inclined to think — which was, indeed, partly the truth — that he had relinquished his proper station in life for want of interest in it. [/]
[Narrator] Moreover, after looking at him one would have hazarded the guess that good nature, and an acuteness as extreme as it could be without verging on craft, formed the framework of his character. [/]
[Narrator] While he darned the stocking his face became rigid with thought. [/]
[Narrator] Softer expressions followed this, and then again recurred the tender sadness which had sat upon him during his drive along the highway that afternoon. [/]
[Narrator] Presently his needle stopped. [/]
[Narrator] He laid down the stocking, arose from his seat, and took a leathern pouch from a hook in the corner of the van. [/]
[Narrator] This contained among other articles a brown - paper packet, which, to judge from the hinge - like character of its worn folds, seemed to have been carefully opened and closed a good many times. [/]
[Narrator] He sat down on a three - legged milking stool that formed the only seat in the van, and, examining his packet by the light of a candle, took thence an old letter and spread it open. [/]
[Narrator] The writing had originally been traced on white paper, but the letter had now assumed a pale red tinge from the accident of its situation; and the black strokes of writing thereon looked like the twigs of a winter hedge against a vermilion sunset. [/]
[I] The letter bore a date some two years previous to that time, and was signed “ Thomasin Yeobright. ” [/]
[Narrator] It ran as follows: — DEAR DIGGORY VENN, —The question you put when you overtook me coming home from Pond - close gave me such a surprise that I am afraid I did not make you exactly understand what I meant. [/]
[Narrator] Of course, if my aunt had not met me I could have explained all then at once, but as it was there was no chance. [/]
[Narrator] I have been quite uneasy since, as you know I do not wish to pain you, yet I fear I shall be doing so now in contradicting what I seemed to say then. [/]
[Narrator] I can not, Diggory, marry you, or think of letting you call me your sweetheart. [/]
[Narrator] I could not, indeed, Diggory. [/]
[Narrator] I hope you will not much mind my saying this, and feel in a great pain. [/]
[Narrator] It makes me very sad when I think it may, for I like you very much, and I always put you next to my cousin Clym in my mind. [/]
[Narrator] There are so many reasons why we can not be married that I can hardly name them all in a letter. [/]
[Narrator] I did not in the least expect that you were going to speak on such a thing when you followed me, because I had never thought of you in the sense of a lover at all. [/]
[Narrator] You must not becall me for laughing when you spoke; you mistook when you thought I laughed at you as a foolish man. [/]
[Narrator] I laughed because the idea was so odd, and not at you at all. [/]
[Narrator] The great reason with my own personal self for not letting you court me is, that I do not feel the things a woman ought to feel who consents to walk with you with the meaning of being your wife. [/]
[Narrator] It is not as you think, that I have another in my mind, for I do not encourage anybody, and never have in my life. [/]
[Narrator] Another reason is my aunt. [/]
[Narrator] She would not, I know, agree to it, even if I wished to have you. [/]
[Narrator] She likes you very well, but she will want me to look a little higher than a small dairy - farmer, and marry a professional man. [/]
[Narrator] I hope you will not set your heart against me for writing plainly, but I felt you might try to see me again, and it is better that we should not meet. [/]
[Narrator] I shall always think of you as a good man, and be anxious for your well - doing. [/]
[Narrator] I send this by Jane Orchard ’s little maid, —And remain Diggory, your faithful friend, THOMASIN YEOBRIGHT. [/]
[Narrator] To Mr. VENN, Dairy - farmer. [/]
[Narrator] Since the arrival of that letter, on a certain autumn morning long ago, the reddleman and Thomasin had not met till today. [/]
[Narrator] During the interval he had shifted his position even further from hers than it had originally been, by adopting the reddle trade; though he was really in very good circumstances still. [/]
[Narrator] Indeed, seeing that his expenditure was only one - fourth of his income, he might have been called a prosperous man. [/]
[Narrator] Rejected suitors take to roaming as naturally as unhived bees; and the business to which he had cynically devoted himself was in many ways congenial to Venn. [/]
[Narrator] But his wanderings, by mere stress of old emotions, had frequently taken an Egdon direction, though he never intruded upon her who attracted him thither. [/]

[Narrator] He had occupied his time in moving with his ponies and load to a new point in the heath, eastward to his previous station; and here he selected a nook with a careful eye to shelter from wind and rain, which seemed to mean that his stay there was to be a comparatively extended one. [/]
[Narrator] After this he returned on foot some part of the way that he had come; and, it being now dark, he diverged to the left till he stood behind a holly bush on the edge of a pit not twenty yards from Rainbarrow. [/]

[Narrator] But the loss of his labour produced little effect upon the reddleman. [/]

[Narrator] “ ’ Tis my cross, my shame, and will be my death! ” “ I abhor it too, ” said he. [/]
[Wildeve] “ How mournfully the wind blows round us now! ” [/]
[Narrator] She did not answer. [/]
[Narrator] Its tone was indeed solemn and pervasive. [/]
[Narrator] Compound utterances addressed themselves to their senses, and it was possible to view by ear the features of the neighbourhood. [/]
[Narrator] Acoustic pictures were returned from the darkened scenery; they could hear where the tracts of heather began and ended; where the furze was growing stalky and tall; where it had been recently cut; in what direction the fir - clump lay, and how near was the pit in which the hollies grew; for these differing features had their voices no less than their shapes and colours. [/]
[Wildeve] “ God, how lonely it is! ” [/]
[Narrator] resumed Wildeve. [/]
[Wildeve] “ What are picturesque ravines and mists to us who see nothing else? [/]
[Wildeve] Why should we stay here? [/]
[Wildeve] Will you go with me to America? [/]
[Wildeve] I have kindred in Wisconsin. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ That wants consideration. ” “ It seems impossible to do well here, unless one were a wild bird or a landscape - painter. [/]
[Narrator] Well? ” “ Give me time, ” she softly said, taking his hand. [/]
[She] “ America is so far away. [/]
[She] Are you going to walk with me a little way? ” [/]
[Narrator] As Eustacia uttered the latter words she retired from the base of the barrow, and Wildeve followed her, so that the reddleman could hear no more. [/]
[Narrator] He lifted the turves and arose. [/]
[Narrator] Their black figures sank and disappeared from against the sky. [/]
[Narrator] They were as two horns which the sluggish heath had put forth from its crown, like a mollusc, and had now again drawn in. [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman ’s walk across the vale, and over into the next where his cart lay, was not sprightly for a slim young fellow of twenty - four. [/]
[Narrator] His spirit was perturbed to aching. [/]
[Narrator] The breezes that blew around his mouth in that walk carried off upon them the accents of a commination. [/]
[Narrator] He entered the van, where there was a fire in a stove. [/]
[Narrator] Without lighting his candle he sat down at once on the three - legged stool, and pondered on what he had seen and heard touching that still - loved one of his. [/]
[Narrator] He uttered a sound which was neither sigh nor sob, but was even more indicative than either of a troubled mind. [/]
[Wildeve] “ My Tamsie, ” he whispered heavily. [/]
[Wildeve] “ What can be done? [/]
[Wildeve] Yes, I will see that Eustacia Vye. ” X. A Desperate Attempt at Persuasion The next morning, at the time when the height of the sun appeared very insignificant from any part of the heath as compared with the altitude of Rainbarrow, and when all the little hills in the lower levels were like an archipelago in a fog - formed Ægean, the reddleman came from the brambled nook which he had adopted as his quarters and ascended the slopes of Mistover Knap. [/]
[Narrator] Though these shaggy hills were apparently so solitary, several keen round eyes were always ready on such a wintry morning as this to converge upon a passer - by. [/]
[Narrator] Feathered species sojourned here in hiding which would have created wonder if found elsewhere. [/]
[Narrator] A bustard haunted the spot, and not many years before this five and twenty might have been seen in Egdon at one time. [/]
[Narrator] Marsh - harriers looked up from the valley by Wildeve ’s. [/]
[Narrator] A cream - coloured courser had used to visit this hill, a bird so rare that not more than a dozen have ever been seen in England; but a barbarian rested neither night nor day till he had shot the African truant, and after that event cream - coloured coursers thought fit to enter Egdon no more. [/]
[Narrator] A traveller who should walk and observe any of these visitants as Venn observed them now could feel himself to be in direct communication with regions unknown to man. [/]
[Narrator] Here in front of him was a wild mallard — just arrived from the home of the north wind. [/]
[Narrator] The creature brought within him an amplitude of Northern knowledge. [/]
[Narrator] Glacial catastrophes, snowstorm episodes, glittering auroral effects, Polaris in the zenith, Franklin underfoot — the category of his commonplaces was wonderful. [/]
[Narrator] But the bird, like many other philosophers, seemed as he looked at the reddleman to think that a present moment of comfortable reality was worth a decade of memories. [/]
[Narrator] Venn passed on through these towards the house of the isolated beauty who lived up among them and despised them. [/]
[Narrator] The day was Sunday; but as going to church, except to be married or buried, was exceptional at Egdon, this made little difference. [/]
[Narrator] He had determined upon the bold stroke of asking for an interview with Miss Vye — to attack her position as Thomasin ’s rival either by art or by storm, showing therein, somewhat too conspicuously, the want of gallantry characteristic of a certain astute sort of men, from clowns to kings. [/]
[Narrator] The great Frederick making war on the beautiful Archduchess, Napoleon refusing terms to the beautiful Queen of Prussia, were not more dead to difference of sex than the reddleman was, in his peculiar way, in planning the displacement of Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] To call at the captain ’s cottage was always more or less an undertaking for the inferior inhabitants. [/]
[Narrator] Though occasionally chatty, his moods were erratic, and nobody could be certain how he would behave at any particular moment. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia was reserved, and lived very much to herself. [/]
[Narrator] Except the daughter of one of the cotters, who was their servant, and a lad who worked in the garden and stable, scarcely anyone but themselves ever entered the house. [/]
[Narrator] They were the only genteel people of the district except the Yeobrights, and though far from rich, they did not feel that necessity for preserving a friendly face towards every man, bird, and beast which influenced their poorer neighbours. [/]
[Narrator] When the reddleman entered the garden the old man was looking through his glass at the stain of blue sea in the distant landscape, the little anchors on his buttons twinkling in the sun. [/]
[Venn] He recognized Venn as his companion on the highway, but made no remark on that circumstance, merely saying, “ Ah, reddleman — you here? [/]
[Venn] Have a glass of grog? ” Venn declined, on the plea of it being too early, and stated that his business was with Miss Vye. [/]
[Narrator] The captain surveyed him from cap to waistcoat and from waistcoat to leggings for a few moments, and finally asked him to go indoors. [/]
[Narrator] Miss Vye was not to be seen by anybody just then; and the reddleman waited in the window - bench of the kitchen, his hands hanging across his divergent knees, and his cap hanging from his hands. [/]
[TheReddleman] “ I suppose the young lady is not up yet? ” [/]
[Narrator] he presently said to the servant. [/]
[TheReddleman] “ Not quite yet. [/]
[TheReddleman] Folks never call upon ladies at this time of day. ” [/]
[Venn] “ Then I ’ll step outside, ” said Venn. [/]
[Venn] “ If she is willing to see me, will she please send out word, and I ’ll come in. ” [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman left the house and loitered on the hill adjoining. [/]
[Narrator] A considerable time elapsed, and no request for his presence was brought. [/]
[Narrator] He was beginning to think that his scheme had failed, when he beheld the form of Eustacia herself coming leisurely towards him. [/]
[Narrator] A sense of novelty in giving audience to that singular figure had been sufficient to draw her forth. [/]
[Narrator] She seemed to feel, after a bare look at Diggory Venn, that the man had come on a strange errand, and that he was not so mean as she had thought him; for her close approach did not cause him to writhe uneasily, or shift his feet, or show any of those little signs which escape an ingenuous rustic at the advent of the uncommon in womankind. [/]
[Eustacia] On his inquiring if he might have a conversation with her she replied, “ Yes, walk beside me, ” and continued to move on. [/]
[Narrator] Before they had gone far it occurred to the perspicacious reddleman that he would have acted more wisely by appearing less unimpressionable, and he resolved to correct the error as soon as he could find opportunity. [/]
[TheMan] “ I have made so bold, miss, as to step across and tell you some strange news which has come to my ears about that man. ” “ Ah! [/]
[TheMan] what man? ” He jerked his elbow to the southeast — the direction of the Quiet Woman. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia turned quickly to him. [/]

[TheMan] It is that he may refuse to marry Thomasin Yeobright after all. ” [/]

[/]
[Eustacia] Although we live only two miles apart I have never been inside her aunt ’s house in my life. ” [/]

[Narrator] He inwardly sighed and felt it necessary to unmask his second argument. [/]

[Narrator] She had advanced to the secret recesses of sensuousness, yet had hardly crossed the threshold of conventionality. XI. [/]
[Narrator] The Dishonesty of an Honest Woman The reddleman had left Eustacia ’s presence with desponding views on Thomasin ’s future happiness; but he was awakened to the fact that one other channel remained untried by seeing, as he followed the way to his van, the form of Mrs. Yeobright slowly walking towards the Quiet Woman. [/]
[Narrator] He went across to her; and could almost perceive in her anxious face that this journey of hers to Wildeve was undertaken with the same object as his own to Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] She did not conceal the fact. [/]
[Narrator] “ Then, ” said the reddleman, “ you may as well leave it alone, Mrs. Yeobright. ” “ I half think so myself, ” she said. [/]
[Narrator] “ But nothing else remains to be done besides pressing the question upon him. ” “ I should like to say a word first, ” said Venn firmly. [/]
[Venn] “ Mr. Wildeve is not the only man who has asked Thomasin to marry him; and why should not another have a chance? [/]
[Venn] Mrs. Yeobright, I should be glad to marry your niece and would have done it any time these last two years. [/]
[Venn] There, now it is out, and I have never told anybody before but herself. ” [/]

[TheReddleman] “ Looks are not everything, ” said the reddleman, noticing the glance. [/]

[MrsYeobright] “ I am much obliged to you for your interest in my niece; but I fear there would be objections. [/]

[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright shook her head. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ Thomasin thinks, and I think with her, that she ought to be Wildeve ’s wife, if she means to appear before the world without a slur upon her name. [/]

[HerGrandfather] ” BOOK SECOND — THE ARRIVAL I. Tidings of the Comer On the fine days at this time of the year, and earlier, certain ephemeral operations were apt to disturb, in their trifling way, the majestic calm of Egdon Heath. [/]
[Narrator] They were activities which, beside those of a town, a village, or even a farm, would have appeared as the ferment of stagnation merely, a creeping of the flesh of somnolence. [/]
[Narrator] But here, away from comparisons, shut in by the stable hills, among which mere walking had the novelty of pageantry, and where any man could imagine himself to be Adam without the least difficulty, they attracted the attention of every bird within eyeshot, every reptile not yet asleep, and set the surrounding rabbits curiously watching from hillocks at a safe distance. [/]
[Narrator] The performance was that of bringing together and building into a stack the furze faggots which Humphrey had been cutting for the captain ’s use during the foregoing fine days. [/]
[Narrator] The stack was at the end of the dwelling, and the men engaged in building it were Humphrey and Sam, the old man looking on. [/]
[Narrator] It was a fine and quiet afternoon, about three o’clock; but the winter solstice having stealthily come on, the lowness of the sun caused the hour to seem later than it actually was, there being little here to remind an inhabitant that he must unlearn his summer experience of the sky as a dial. [/]
[Narrator] In the course of many days and weeks sunrise had advanced its quarters from northeast to southeast, sunset had receded from northwest to southwest; but Egdon had hardly heeded the change. [/]

[Narrator] While the furze - gatherers had desultorily conversed thus Eustacia ’s face gradually bent to the hearth in a profound reverie, her toe unconsciously tapping the dry turf which lay burning at her feet. [/]

[Narrator] A young and clever man was coming into that lonely heath from, of all contrasting places in the world, Paris. [/]
[Narrator] It was like a man coming from heaven. [/]

[Narrator] The scene of the daydream is sufficient for a pilgrimage at nineteen. [/]

[Narrator] Strange that such a piece of idling should have seemed an important errand. [/]
