[Narrator] This brought her to a spot in which the green bottom of the dale began to widen, the furze bushes to recede yet further from the path on each side, till they were diminished to an isolated one here and there by the increasing fertility of the soil. [/]
[Narrator] Beyond the irregular carpet of grass was a row of white palings, which marked the verge of the heath in this latitude. [/]
[Narrator] They showed upon the dusky scene that they bordered as distinctly as white lace on velvet. [/]
[Narrator] Behind the white palings was a little garden; behind the garden an old, irregular, thatched house, facing the heath, and commanding a full view of the valley. [/]
[Narrator] This was the obscure, removed spot to which was about to return a man whose latter life had been passed in the French capital — the centre and vortex of the fashionable world. II. [/]
[Narrator] The People at Blooms - End Make Ready All that afternoon the expected arrival of the subject of Eustacia ’s ruminations created a bustle of preparation at Blooms - End. [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin had been persuaded by her aunt, and by an instinctive impulse of loyalty towards her cousin Clym, to bestir herself on his account with an alacrity unusual in her during these most sorrowful days of her life. [/]
[Narrator] At the time that Eustacia was listening to the rick - makers ’ conversation on Clym ’s return, Thomasin was climbing into a loft over her aunt ’s fuelhouse, where the store - apples were kept, to search out the best and largest of them for the coming holiday - time. [/]
[Narrator] The loft was lighted by a semicircular hole, through which the pigeons crept to their lodgings in the same high quarters of the premises; and from this hole the sun shone in a bright yellow patch upon the figure of the maiden as she knelt and plunged her naked arms into the soft brown fern, which, from its abundance, was used on Egdon in packing away stores of all kinds. [/]
[Narrator] The pigeons were flying about her head with the greatest unconcern, and the face of her aunt was just visible above the floor of the loft, lit by a few stray motes of light, as she stood halfway up the ladder, looking at a spot into which she was not climber enough to venture. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Now a few russets, Tamsin. [/]
[Thomasin] He used to like them almost as well as ribstones. ” [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin turned and rolled aside the fern from another nook, where more mellow fruit greeted her with its ripe smell. [/]
[Narrator] Before picking them out she stopped a moment. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Dear Clym, I wonder how your face looks now? ” [/]
[Narrator] she said, gazing abstractedly at the pigeon - hole, which admitted the sunlight so directly upon her brown hair and transparent tissues that it almost seemed to shine through her. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ If he could have been dear to you in another way, ” said Mrs. Yeobright from the ladder, “ this might have been a happy meeting. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ Is there any use in saying what can do no good, Aunt? ” [/]
[HerAunt] “ Yes, ” said her aunt, with some warmth. [/]
[HerAunt] “ To thoroughly fill the air with the past misfortune, so that other girls may take warning and keep clear of it. ” Thomasin lowered her face to the apples again. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I am a warning to others, just as thieves and drunkards and gamblers are, ” she said in a low voice. [/]
[Thomasin] “ What a class to belong to! [/]
[Thomasin] Do I really belong to them? ’ [/]
[Thomasin] Tis absurd! [/]
[Thomasin] Yet why, Aunt, does everybody keep on making me think that I do, by the way they behave towards me? [/]
[Thomasin] Why do n’t people judge me by my acts? [/]
[Thomasin] Now, look at me as I kneel here, picking up these apples — do I look like a lost woman? .. . [/]
[Thomasin] I wish all good women were as good as I! ” [/]
[Narrator] she added vehemently. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ Strangers do n’t see you as I do, ” said Mrs. Yeobright; “ they judge from false report. [/]
[MrsYeobright] Well, it is a silly job, and I am partly to blame. ” [/]
[TheGirl] “ How quickly a rash thing can be done! ” [/]
[Narrator] replied the girl. [/]
[Narrator] Her lips were quivering, and tears so crowded themselves into her eyes that she could hardly distinguish apples from fern as she continued industriously searching to hide her weakness. [/]
[HerAunt] “ As soon as you have finished getting the apples, ” her aunt said, descending the ladder, “ come down, and we ’ll go for the holly. [/]
[HerAunt] There is nobody on the heath this afternoon, and you need not fear being stared at. [/]
[HerAunt] We must get some berries, or Clym will never believe in our preparations. ” [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin came down when the apples were collected, and together they went through the white palings to the heath beyond. [/]
[Narrator] The open hills were airy and clear, and the remote atmosphere appeared, as it often appears on a fine winter day, in distinct planes of illumination independently toned, the rays which lit the nearer tracts of landscape streaming visibly across those further off; a stratum of ensaffroned light was imposed on a stratum of deep blue, and behind these lay still remoter scenes wrapped in frigid grey. [/]
[Narrator] They reached the place where the hollies grew, which was in a conical pit, so that the tops of the trees were not much above the general level of the ground. [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin stepped up into a fork of one of the bushes, as she had done under happier circumstances on many similar occasions, and with a small chopper that they had brought she began to lop off the heavily berried boughs. [/]
[HerAunt] “ Do n’t scratch your face, ” said her aunt, who stood at the edge of the pit, regarding the girl as she held on amid the glistening green and scarlet masses of the tree. [/]
[Narrator] “ Will you walk with me to meet him this evening? ” “ I should like to. [/]
[Thomasin] Else it would seem as if I had forgotten him, ” said Thomasin, tossing out a bough. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Not that that would matter much; I belong to one man; nothing can alter that. [/]
[Narrator] And that man I must marry, for my pride ’s sake. ” “ I am afraid — ” began Mrs. Yeobright. [/]
[TheGirl] “ Ah, you think, ‘ That weak girl — how is she going to get a man to marry her when she chooses? ’ [/]
[TheGirl] But let me tell you one thing, Aunt: Mr. Wildeve is not a profligate man, any more than I am an improper woman. [/]
[TheGirl] He has an unfortunate manner, and does n’t try to make people like him if they do n’t wish to do it of their own accord. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ Thomasin, ” said Mrs. Yeobright quietly, fixing her eye upon her niece, “ do you think you deceive me in your defence of Mr. Wildeve? ” “ How do you mean? ” “ I have long had a suspicion that your love for him has changed its colour since you have found him not to be the saint you thought him, and that you act a part to me. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ He wished to marry me, and I wish to marry him. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ Now, I put it to you: would you at this present moment agree to be his wife if that had not happened to entangle you with him? ” Thomasin looked into the tree and appeared much disturbed. [/]
[Narrator] “ Aunt, ” she said presently, “ I have, I think, a right to refuse to answer that question. ” “ Yes, you have. ” “ You may think what you choose. [/]
[Thomasin] I have never implied to you by word or deed that I have grown to think otherwise of him, and I never will. [/]
[Thomasin] And I shall marry him. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ Well, wait till he repeats his offer. [/]
[MrsYeobright] I think he may do it, now that he knows — something I told him. [/]
[MrsYeobright] I do n’t for a moment dispute that it is the most proper thing for you to marry him. [/]
[MrsYeobright] Much as I have objected to him in bygone days, I agree with you now, you may be sure. [/]
[MrsYeobright] It is the only way out of a false position, and a very galling one. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ What did you tell him? ” “ That he was standing in the way of another lover of yours. ” “ Aunt, ” said Thomasin, with round eyes, “ what _ do _ you mean? ” “ Do n’t be alarmed; it was my duty. [/]
[MrsYeobright] I can say no more about it now, but when it is over I will tell you exactly what I said, and why I said it. ” Thomasin was perforce content. [/]
[Thomasin] “ And you will keep the secret of my would - be marriage from Clym for the present? ” [/]
[Narrator] she next asked. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ I have given my word to. [/]
[MrsYeobright] But what is the use of it? [/]
[MrsYeobright] He must soon know what has happened. [/]
[MrsYeobright] A mere look at your face will show him that something is wrong. ” [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin turned and regarded her aunt from the tree. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Now, hearken to me, ” she said, her delicate voice expanding into firmness by a force which was other than physical. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Tell him nothing. [/]
[Thomasin] If he finds out that I am not worthy to be his cousin, let him. [/]
[Thomasin] But, since he loved me once, we will not pain him by telling him my trouble too soon. [/]
[Thomasin] The air is full of the story, I know; but gossips will not dare to speak of it to him for the first few days. [/]
[Thomasin] His closeness to me is the very thing that will hinder the tale from reaching him early. [/]
[Thomasin] If I am not made safe from sneers in a week or two I will tell him myself. ” [/]
[Narrator] The earnestness with which Thomasin spoke prevented further objections. [/]
[HerAunt] Her aunt simply said, “ Very well. [/]
[HerAunt] He should by rights have been told at the time that the wedding was going to be. [/]
[HerAunt] He will never forgive you for your secrecy. ” [/]
[HerAunt] “ Yes, he will, when he knows it was because I wished to spare him, and that I did not expect him home so soon. [/]
[HerAunt] And you must not let me stand in the way of your Christmas party. [/]
[HerAunt] Putting it off would only make matters worse. ” [/]
[HerAunt] “ Of course I shall not. [/]
[HerAunt] I do not wish to show myself beaten before all Egdon, and the sport of a man like Wildeve. [/]
[HerAunt] We have enough berries now, I think, and we had better take them home. [/]
[HerAunt] By the time we have decked the house with this and hung up the mistletoe, we must think of starting to meet him. ” [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin came out of the tree, shook from her hair and dress the loose berries which had fallen thereon, and went down the hill with her aunt, each woman bearing half the gathered boughs. [/]
[Narrator] It was now nearly four o’clock, and the sunlight was leaving the vales. [/]
[Narrator] When the west grew red the two relatives came again from the house and plunged into the heath in a different direction from the first, towards a point in the distant highway along which the expected man was to return. [/]
[Narrator] III. [/]
[Narrator] How a Little Sound Produced a Great Dream Eustacia stood just within the heath, straining her eyes in the direction of Mrs. Yeobright ’s house and premises. [/]
[Narrator] No light, sound, or movement was perceptible there. [/]
[Narrator] The evening was chilly; the spot was dark and lonely. [/]
[Narrator] She inferred that the guest had not yet come; and after lingering ten or fifteen minutes she turned again towards home. [/]
[Narrator] She had not far retraced her steps when sounds in front of her betokened the approach of persons in conversation along the same path. [/]
[Narrator] Soon their heads became visible against the sky. [/]
[Narrator] They were walking slowly; and though it was too dark for much discovery of character from aspect, the gait of them showed that they were not workers on the heath. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia stepped a little out of the foot - track to let them pass. [/]
[Narrator] They were two women and a man; and the voices of the women were those of Mrs. Yeobright and Thomasin. [/]
[Narrator] They went by her, and at the moment of passing appeared to discern her dusky form. [/]
[PersonsInConversation] There came to her ears in a masculine voice, “ Good night! ” [/]
[Narrator] She murmured a reply, glided by them, and turned round. [/]
[Narrator] She could not, for a moment, believe that chance, unrequested, had brought into her presence the soul of the house she had gone to inspect, the man without whom her inspection would not have been thought of. [/]
[Narrator] She strained her eyes to see them, but was unable. [/]
[Narrator] Such was her intentness, however, that it seemed as if her ears were performing the functions of seeing as well as hearing. [/]
[Narrator] This extension of power can almost be believed in at such moments. [/]
[Narrator] The deaf Dr. Kitto was probably under the influence of a parallel fancy when he described his body as having become, by long endeavour, so sensitive to vibrations that he had gained the power of perceiving by it as by ears. [/]
[Narrator] She could follow every word that the ramblers uttered. [/]
[Narrator] They were talking no secrets. [/]
[Narrator] They were merely indulging in the ordinary vivacious chat of relatives who have long been parted in person though not in soul. [/]
[Narrator] But it was not to the words that Eustacia listened; she could not even have recalled, a few minutes later, what the words were. [/]
[Narrator] It was to the alternating voice that gave out about one - tenth of them — the voice that had wished her good night. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes this throat uttered Yes, sometimes it uttered No; sometimes it made inquiries about a time - worn denizen of the place. [/]
[Narrator] Once it surprised her notions by remarking upon the friendliness and geniality written in the faces of the hills around. [/]
[Narrator] The three voices passed on, and decayed and died out upon her ear. [/]
[Narrator] Thus much had been granted her; and all besides withheld. [/]
[Narrator] No event could have been more exciting. [/]
[Narrator] During the greater part of the afternoon she had been entrancing herself by imagining the fascination which must attend a man come direct from beautiful Paris — laden with its atmosphere, familiar with its charms. [/]
[Narrator] And this man had greeted her. [/]
[Narrator] With the departure of the figures the profuse articulations of the women wasted away from her memory; but the accents of the other stayed on. [/]
[Narrator] Was there anything in the voice of Mrs. Yeobright ’s son — for Clym it was — startling as a sound? [/]
[Narrator] No; it was simply comprehensive. [/]
[TheSpeaker] All emotional things were possible to the speaker of that “ good night. ” [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia ’s imagination supplied the rest — except the solution to one riddle. [/]
[Narrator] What _ could _ the tastes of that man be who saw friendliness and geniality in these shaggy hills? [/]
[Narrator] On such occasions as this a thousand ideas pass through a highly charged woman ’s head; and they indicate themselves on her face; but the changes, though actual, are minute. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia ’s features went through a rhythmical succession of them. [/]
[Narrator] She glowed; remembering the mendacity of the imagination, she flagged; then she freshened; then she fired; then she cooled again. [/]
[Narrator] It was a cycle of aspects, produced by a cycle of visions. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia entered her own house; she was excited. [/]
[Narrator] Her grandfather was enjoying himself over the fire, raking about the ashes and exposing the red - hot surface of the turves, so that their lurid glare irradiated the chimney - corner with the hues of a furnace. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Why is it that we are never friendly with the Yeobrights? ” she said, coming forward and stretching her soft hands over the warmth. [/]
[Eustacia] “ I wish we were. [/]
[Narrator] They seem to be very nice people. ” “ Be hanged if I know why, ” said the captain. [/]
[TheCaptain] “ I liked the old man well enough, though he was as rough as a hedge. [/]
[TheCaptain] But you would never have cared to go there, even if you might have, I am well sure. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ Why should n’t I? ” “ Your town tastes would find them far too countrified. [/]
[TheCaptain] They sit in the kitchen, drink mead and elder - wine, and sand the floor to keep it clean. [/]
[Narrator] A sensible way of life; but how would you like it? ” “ I thought Mrs. Yeobright was a ladylike woman? [/]
[Narrator] A curate ’s daughter, was she not? ” “ Yes; but she was obliged to live as her husband did; and I suppose she has taken kindly to it by this time. [/]
[TheCaptain] Ah, I recollect that I once accidentally offended her, and I have never seen her since. ” [/]
[Narrator] That night was an eventful one to Eustacia ’s brain, and one which she hardly ever forgot. [/]
[Narrator] She dreamt a dream; and few human beings, from Nebuchadnezzar to the Swaffham tinker, ever dreamt a more remarkable one. [/]
[Narrator] Such an elaborately developed, perplexing, exciting dream was certainly never dreamed by a girl in Eustacia ’s situation before. [/]
[Narrator] It had as many ramifications as the Cretan labyrinth, as many fluctuations as the northern lights, as much colour as a parterre in June, and was as crowded with figures as a coronation. [/]
[Narrator] To Queen Scheherazade the dream might have seemed not far removed from commonplace; and to a girl just returned from all the courts of Europe it might have seemed not more than interesting. [/]
[Narrator] But amid the circumstances of Eustacia ’s life it was as wonderful as a dream could be. [/]
[Narrator] There was, however, gradually evolved from its transformation scenes a less extravagant episode, in which the heath dimly appeared behind the general brilliancy of the action. [/]
[Narrator] She was dancing to wondrous music, and her partner was the man in silver armour who had accompanied her through the previous fantastic changes, the visor of his helmet being closed. [/]
[Narrator] The mazes of the dance were ecstatic. [/]
[Narrator] Soft whispering came into her ear from under the radiant helmet, and she felt like a woman in Paradise. [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly these two wheeled out from the mass of dancers, dived into one of the pools of the heath, and came out somewhere into an iridescent hollow, arched with rainbows. [/]
[TheManInSilverArmourWhoHadAccompaniedHerThroughThePreviousFantasticChangesTheVisorOfHisHelmetBeingClosed] “ It must be here, ” said the voice by her side, and blushingly looking up she saw him removing his casque to kiss her. [/]
[Narrator] At that moment there was a cracking noise, and his figure fell into fragments like a pack of cards. [/]
[Narrator] She cried aloud. [/]
[Eustacia] “ O that I had seen his face! ” [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia awoke. [/]
[Narrator] The cracking had been that of the window shutter downstairs, which the maid - servant was opening to let in the day, now slowly increasing to Nature ’s meagre allowance at this sickly time of the year. [/]
[Eustacia] “ O that I had seen his face! ” [/]
[Narrator] she said again. [/]
[Eustacia] “ ’ Twas meant for Mr. Yeobright! ” [/]
[Narrator] When she became cooler she perceived that many of the phases of the dream had naturally arisen out of the images and fancies of the day before. [/]
[Narrator] But this detracted little from its interest, which lay in the excellent fuel it provided for newly kindled fervour. [/]
[Eustacia] She was at the modulating point between indifference and love, at the stage called “ having a fancy for. ” [/]
[Narrator] It occurs once in the history of the most gigantic passions, and it is a period when they are in the hands of the weakest will. [/]
[Narrator] The perfervid woman was by this time half in love with a vision. [/]
[Narrator] The fantastic nature of her passion, which lowered her as an intellect, raised her as a soul. [/]
[Narrator] If she had had a little more self - control she would have attenuated the emotion to nothing by sheer reasoning, and so have killed it off. [/]
[Narrator] If she had had a little less pride she might have gone and circumambulated the Yeobrights ’ premises at Blooms - End at any maidenly sacrifice until she had seen him. [/]
[Narrator] But Eustacia did neither of these things. [/]
[Narrator] She acted as the most exemplary might have acted, being so influenced; she took an airing twice or thrice a day upon the Egdon hills, and kept her eyes employed. [/]
[Narrator] The first occasion passed, and he did not come that way. [/]
[Narrator] She promenaded a second time, and was again the sole wanderer there. [/]
[Narrator] The third time there was a dense fog; she looked around, but without much hope. [/]
[Narrator] Even if he had been walking within twenty yards of her she could not have seen him. [/]
[Narrator] At the fourth attempt to encounter him it began to rain in torrents, and she turned back. [/]
[Narrator] The fifth sally was in the afternoon; it was fine, and she remained out long, walking to the very top of the valley in which Blooms - End lay. [/]
[Narrator] She saw the white paling about half a mile off; but he did not appear. [/]
[Narrator] It was almost with heart - sickness that she came home and with a sense of shame at her weakness. [/]
[Narrator] She resolved to look for the man from Paris no more. [/]
[Narrator] But Providence is nothing if not coquettish; and no sooner had Eustacia formed this resolve than the opportunity came which, while sought, had been entirely withholden. [/]
[Narrator] IV. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia Is Led on to an Adventure In the evening of this last day of expectation, which was the twenty - third of December, Eustacia was at home alone. [/]
[Narrator] She had passed the recent hour in lamenting over a rumour newly come to her ears — that Yeobright ’s visit to his mother was to be of short duration, and would end some time the next week. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Naturally, ” she said to herself. [/]
[Narrator] A man in the full swing of his activities in a gay city could not afford to linger long on Egdon Heath. [/]
[Narrator] That she would behold face to face the owner of the awakening voice within the limits of such a holiday was most unlikely, unless she were to haunt the environs of his mother ’s house like a robin, to do which was difficult and unseemly. [/]
[Narrator] The customary expedient of provincial girls and men in such circumstances is churchgoing. [/]
[Narrator] In an ordinary village or country town one can safely calculate that, either on Christmas day or the Sunday contiguous, any native home for the holidays, who has not through age or ennui lost the appetite for seeing and being seen, will turn up in some pew or other, shining with hope, self - consciousness, and new clothes. [/]
[Narrator] Thus the congregation on Christmas morning is mostly a Tussaud collection of celebrities who have been born in the neighbourhood. [/]
[Narrator] Hither the mistress, left neglected at home all the year, can steal and observe the development of the returned lover who has forgotten her, and think as she watches him over her prayer book that he may throb with a renewed fidelity when novelties have lost their charm. [/]
[Narrator] And hither a comparatively recent settler like Eustacia may betake herself to scrutinize the person of a native son who left home before her advent upon the scene, and consider if the friendship of his parents be worth cultivating during his next absence in order to secure a knowledge of him on his next return. [/]
[Narrator] But these tender schemes were not feasible among the scattered inhabitants of Egdon Heath. [/]
[Narrator] In name they were parishioners, but virtually they belonged to no parish at all. [/]
[Narrator] People who came to these few isolated houses to keep Christmas with their friends remained in their friends ’ chimney - corners drinking mead and other comforting liquors till they left again for good and all. [/]
[Narrator] Rain, snow, ice, mud everywhere around, they did not care to trudge two or three miles to sit wet - footed and splashed to the nape of their necks among those who, though in some measure neighbours, lived close to the church, and entered it clean and dry. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia knew it was ten to one that Clym Yeobright would go to no church at all during his few days of leave, and that it would be a waste of labour for her to go driving the pony and gig over a bad road in hope to see him there. [/]
[Narrator] It was dusk, and she was sitting by the fire in the dining - room or hall, which they occupied at this time of the year in preference to the parlour, because of its large hearth, constructed for turf - fires, a fuel the captain was partial to in the winter season. [/]
[Narrator] The only visible articles in the room were those on the window - sill, which showed their shapes against the low sky, the middle article being the old hourglass, and the other two a pair of ancient British urns which had been dug from a barrow near, and were used as flowerpots for two razor - leaved cactuses. [/]
[Narrator] Somebody knocked at the door. [/]
[Narrator] The servant was out; so was her grandfather. [/]
[Narrator] The person, after waiting a minute, came in and tapped at the door of the room. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Who ’s there? ” said Eustacia. [/]
[ThePerson] “ Please, Cap’n Vye, will you let us — — ” Eustacia arose and went to the door. [/]
[Eustacia] “ I can not allow you to come in so boldly. [/]
[Eustacia] You should have waited. ” [/]
[ALad] “ The cap’n said I might come in without any fuss, ” was answered in a lad ’s pleasant voice. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Oh, did he? ” said Eustacia more gently. [/]
[Eustacia] “ What do you want, Charley? ” “ Please will your grandfather lend us his fuelhouse to try over our parts in, tonight at seven o’clock? ” “ What, are you one of the Egdon mummers for this year? ” [/]
[Eustacia] “ Yes, miss. [/]
[Eustacia] The cap’n used to let the old mummers practise here. ” “ I know it. [/]
[Eustacia] Yes, you may use the fuelhouse if you like, ” said Eustacia languidly. [/]
[Narrator] The choice of Captain Vye ’s fuelhouse as the scene of rehearsal was dictated by the fact that his dwelling was nearly in the centre of the heath. [/]
[Narrator] The fuelhouse was as roomy as a barn, and was a most desirable place for such a purpose. [/]
[Narrator] The lads who formed the company of players lived at different scattered points around, and by meeting in this spot the distances to be traversed by all the comers would be about equally proportioned. [/]
[Narrator] For mummers and mumming Eustacia had the greatest contempt. [/]
[Narrator] The mummers themselves were not afflicted with any such feeling for their art, though at the same time they were not enthusiastic. [/]
[Narrator] A traditional pastime is to be distinguished from a mere revival in no more striking feature than in this, that while in the revival all is excitement and fervour, the survival is carried on with a stolidity and absence of stir which sets one wondering why a thing that is done so perfunctorily should be kept up at all. [/]
[Narrator] Like Balaam and other unwilling prophets, the agents seem moved by an inner compulsion to say and do their allotted parts whether they will or no. [/]
[Narrator] This unweeting manner of performance is the true ring by which, in this refurbishing age, a fossilized survival may be known from a spurious reproduction. [/]
[Narrator] The piece was the well - known play of Saint George, and all who were behind the scenes assisted in the preparations, including the women of each household. [/]
[Narrator] Without the co - operation of sisters and sweethearts the dresses were likely to be a failure; but on the other hand, this class of assistance was not without its drawbacks. [/]
[Narrator] The girls could never be brought to respect tradition in designing and decorating the armour; they insisted on attaching loops and bows of silk and velvet in any situation pleasing to their taste. [/]
[Narrator] Gorget, gusset, basinet, cuirass, gauntlet, sleeve, all alike in the view of these feminine eyes were practicable spaces whereon to sew scraps of fluttering colour. [/]
[Narrator] It might be that Joe, who fought on the side of Christendom, had a sweetheart, and that Jim, who fought on the side of the Moslem, had one likewise. [/]
[Narrator] During the making of the costumes it would come to the knowledge of Joe ’s sweetheart that Jim ’s was putting brilliant silk scallops at the bottom of her lover ’s surcoat, in addition to the ribbons of the visor, the bars of which, being invariably formed of coloured strips about half an inch wide hanging before the face, were mostly of that material. [/]
[Narrator] Joe ’s sweetheart straight - way placed brilliant silk on the scallops of the hem in question, and, going a little further, added ribbon tufts to the shoulder pieces. [/]
[Narrator] Jim ’s, not to be outdone, would affix bows and rosettes everywhere. [/]
[Narrator] The result was that in the end the Valiant Soldier, of the Christian army, was distinguished by no peculiarity of accoutrement from the Turkish Knight; and what was worse, on a casual view Saint George himself might be mistaken for his deadly enemy, the Saracen. [/]
[Narrator] The guisers themselves, though inwardly regretting this confusion of persons, could not afford to offend those by whose assistance they so largely profited, and the innovations were allowed to stand. [/]
[Narrator] There was, it is true, a limit to this tendency to uniformity. [/]
[Narrator] The Leech or Doctor preserved his character intact — his darker habiliments, peculiar hat, and the bottle of physic slung under his arm, could never be mistaken. [/]
[Narrator] And the same might be said of the conventional figure of Father Christmas, with his gigantic club, an older man, who accompanied the band as general protector in long night journeys from parish to parish, and was bearer of the purse. [/]
[Narrator] Seven o’clock, the hour of the rehearsal, came round, and in a short time Eustacia could hear voices in the fuelhouse. [/]
[Eustacia] To dissipate in some trifling measure her abiding sense of the murkiness of human life she went to the “ linhay ” or lean - to shed, which formed the root - store of their dwelling and abutted on the fuelhouse. [/]
[Narrator] Here was a small rough hole in the mud wall, originally made for pigeons, through which the interior of the next shed could be viewed. [/]
[Narrator] A light came from it now; and Eustacia stepped upon a stool to look in upon the scene. [/]
[Narrator] On a ledge in the fuelhouse stood three tall rushlights and by the light of them seven or eight lads were marching about, haranguing, and confusing each other, in endeavours to perfect themselves in the play. [/]
[Narrator] Humphrey and Sam, the furze- and turf - cutters, were there looking on, so also was Timothy Fairway, who leant against the wall and prompted the boys from memory, interspersing among the set words remarks and anecdotes of the superior days when he and others were the Egdon mummers - elect that these lads were now. [/]
[Fairway] “ Well, ye be as well up to it as ever ye will be, ” he said. [/]
[Fairway] “ Not that such mumming would have passed in our time. [/]
[Fairway] Harry as the Saracen should strut a bit more, and John need n’t holler his inside out. [/]
[Fairway] Beyond that perhaps you ’ll do. [/]
[Fairway] Have you got all your clothes ready? ” “ We shall by Monday. ” “ Your first outing will be Monday night, I suppose? ” “ Yes. [/]
[Fairway] At Mrs. Yeobright ’s. ” “ Oh, Mrs. Yeobright ’s. [/]
[Fairway] What makes her want to see ye? [/]
[Fairway] I should think a middle - aged woman was tired of mumming. ” [/]
[Fairway] “ She ’s got up a bit of a party, because ’ tis the first Christmas that her son Clym has been home for a long time. ” “ To be sure, to be sure — her party! [/]
[Fairway] I am going myself. [/]
[Fairway] I almost forgot it, upon my life. ” [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia ’s face flagged. [/]
[Narrator] There was to be a party at the Yeobrights ’; she, naturally, had nothing to do with it. [/]
[Narrator] She was a stranger to all such local gatherings, and had always held them as scarcely appertaining to her sphere. [/]
[Narrator] But had she been going, what an opportunity would have been afforded her of seeing the man whose influence was penetrating her like summer sun! [/]
[Narrator] To increase that influence was coveted excitement; to cast it off might be to regain serenity; to leave it as it stood was tantalizing. [/]
[Narrator] The lads and men prepared to leave the premises, and Eustacia returned to her fireside. [/]
[Narrator] She was immersed in thought, but not for long. [/]
[Narrator] In a few minutes the lad Charley, who had come to ask permission to use the place, returned with the key to the kitchen. [/]
[Eustacia] Eustacia heard him, and opening the door into the passage said, “ Charley, come here. ” [/]
[Narrator] The lad was surprised. [/]
[Narrator] He entered the front room not without blushing; for he, like many, had felt the power of this girl ’s face and form. [/]
[Narrator] She pointed to a seat by the fire, and entered the other side of the chimney - corner herself. [/]
[Narrator] It could be seen in her face that whatever motive she might have had in asking the youth indoors would soon appear. [/]
[TheBeauty] “ Which part do you play, Charley — the Turkish Knight, do you not? ” inquired the beauty, looking across the smoke of the fire to him on the other side. [/]
[TheLad] “ Yes, miss, the Turkish Knight, ” he replied diffidently. [/]
[TheLad] “ Is yours a long part? ” “ Nine speeches, about. ” [/]
[TheLad] “ Can you repeat them to me? [/]
[TheLad] If so I should like to hear them. ” [/]
[TheLad] The lad smiled into the glowing turf and began — “ Here come I, a Turkish Knight, Who learnt in Turkish land to fight, ” continuing the discourse throughout the scenes to the concluding catastrophe of his fall by the hand of Saint George. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia had occasionally heard the part recited before. [/]
[Narrator] When the lad ended she began, precisely in the same words, and ranted on without hitch or divergence till she too reached the end. [/]
[Narrator] It was the same thing, yet how different. [/]
[Narrator] Like in form, it had the added softness and finish of a Raffaelle after Perugino, which, while faithfully reproducing the original subject, entirely distances the original art. [/]
[Narrator] Charley ’s eyes rounded with surprise. [/]
[Charley] “ Well, you be a clever lady! ” [/]
[Narrator] he said, in admiration. [/]
[Narrator] “ I ’ve been three weeks learning mine. ” “ I have heard it before, ” she quietly observed. [/]
[Narrator] “ Now, would you do anything to please me, Charley? ” “ I ’d do a good deal, miss. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ Would you let me play your part for one night? ” “ Oh, miss! [/]
[Narrator] But your woman ’s gown — you could n’t. ” “ I can get boy ’s clothes — at least all that would be wanted besides the mumming dress. [/]
[Eustacia] What should I have to give you to lend me your things, to let me take your place for an hour or two on Monday night, and on no account to say a word about who or what I am? [/]
[Eustacia] You would, of course, have to excuse yourself from playing that night, and to say that somebody — a cousin of Miss Vye’s — would act for you. [/]
[Eustacia] The other mummers have never spoken to me in their lives so that it would be safe enough; and if it were not, I should not mind. [/]
[Eustacia] Now, what must I give you to agree to this? [/]
[Eustacia] Half a crown? ” [/]
[TheYouth] The youth shook his head “ Five shillings? ” [/]
[Narrator] He shook his head again. [/]
[TheYouth] “ Money wo n’t do it, ” he said, brushing the iron head of the firedog with the hollow of his hand. [/]
[Eustacia] “ What will, then, Charley? ” said Eustacia in a disappointed tone. [/]
[TheLad] “ You know what you forbade me at the Maypoling, miss, ” murmured the lad, without looking at her, and still stroking the firedog ’s head. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Yes, ” said Eustacia, with a little more hauteur. [/]
[Narrator] “ You wanted to join hands with me in the ring, if I recollect? ” “ Half an hour of that, and I ’ll agree, miss. ” [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia regarded the youth steadfastly. [/]
[Narrator] He was three years younger than herself, but apparently not backward for his age. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Half an hour of what? ” [/]
[Narrator] she said, though she guessed what. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Holding your hand in mine. ” [/]
[Narrator] She was silent. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Make it a quarter of an hour, ” she said “ Yes, Miss Eustacia — I will, if I may kiss it too. [/]
[Eustacia] A quarter of an hour. [/]
[Eustacia] And I ’ll swear to do the best I can to let you take my place without anybody knowing. [/]
[Eustacia] Do n’t you think somebody might know your tongue, miss? ” [/]
[Eustacia] “ It is possible. [/]
[Eustacia] But I will put a pebble in my mouth to make is less likely. [/]
[Eustacia] Very well; you shall be allowed to have my hand as soon as you bring the dress and your sword and staff. [/]
[Eustacia] I do n’t want you any longer now. ” [/]
[Narrator] Charley departed, and Eustacia felt more and more interest in life. [/]
[Narrator] Here was something to do: here was some one to see, and a charmingly adventurous way to see him. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Ah, ” she said to herself, “ want of an object to live for — that ’s all is the matter with me! ” Eustacia ’s manner was as a rule of a slumberous sort, her passions being of the massive rather than the vivacious kind. [/]
[Narrator] But when aroused she would make a dash which, just for the time, was not unlike the move of a naturally lively person. [/]
[Narrator] On the question of recognition she was somewhat indifferent. [/]
[Narrator] By the acting lads themselves she was not likely to be known. [/]
[Narrator] With the guests who might be assembled she was hardly so secure. [/]
[Narrator] Yet detection, after all, would be no such dreadful thing. [/]
[Narrator] The fact only could be detected, her true motive never. [/]
[Narrator] It would be instantly set down as the passing freak of a girl whose ways were already considered singular. [/]
[Narrator] That she was doing for an earnest reason what would most naturally be done in jest was at any rate a safe secret. [/]
[Narrator] The next evening Eustacia stood punctually at the fuelhouse door, waiting for the dusk which was to bring Charley with the trappings. [/]
[Narrator] Her grandfather was at home tonight, and she would be unable to ask her confederate indoors. [/]
[Narrator] He appeared on the dark ridge of heathland, like a fly on a Negro, bearing the articles with him, and came up breathless with his walk. [/]
[Charley] “ Here are the things, ” he whispered, placing them upon the threshold. [/]
[Narrator] “ And now, Miss Eustacia — ” “ The payment. [/]
[Eustacia] It is quite ready. [/]
[Eustacia] I am as good as my word. ” [/]
[Narrator] She leant against the door - post, and gave him her hand. [/]
[Narrator] Charley took it in both his own with a tenderness beyond description, unless it was like that of a child holding a captured sparrow. [/]
[Charley] “ Why, there ’s a glove on it! ” [/]
[Narrator] he said in a deprecating way. [/]
[Eustacia] “ I have been walking, ” she observed. [/]
[Charley] “ But, miss! ” [/]
[Eustacia] “ Well — it is hardly fair. ” [/]
[Narrator] She pulled off the glove, and gave him her bare hand. [/]
[Narrator] They stood together minute after minute, without further speech, each looking at the blackening scene, and each thinking his and her own thoughts. [/]
[Charley] “ I think I wo n’t use it all up tonight, ” said Charley devotedly, when six or eight minutes had been passed by him caressing her hand. [/]
[Narrator] “ May I have the other few minutes another time? ” “ As you like, ” said she without the least emotion. [/]
[Eustacia] “ But it must be over in a week. [/]
[Eustacia] Now, there is only one thing I want you to do — to wait while I put on the dress, and then to see if I do my part properly. [/]
[Eustacia] But let me look first indoors. ” [/]
[Narrator] She vanished for a minute or two, and went in. [/]
[Narrator] Her grandfather was safely asleep in his chair. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Now, then, ” she said, on returning, “ walk down the garden a little way, and when I am ready I ’ll call you. ” [/]
[Narrator] Charley walked and waited, and presently heard a soft whistle. [/]
[Narrator] He returned to the fuelhouse door. [/]
[Charley] “ Did you whistle, Miss Vye? ” “ Yes; come in, ” reached him in Eustacia ’s voice from a back quarter. [/]
[Charley] “ I must not strike a light till the door is shut, or it may be seen shining. [/]
[Charley] Push your hat into the hole through to the wash - house, if you can feel your way across. ” [/]
[Narrator] Charley did as commanded, and she struck the light revealing herself to be changed in sex, brilliant in colours, and armed from top to toe. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps she quailed a little under Charley ’s vigorous gaze, but whether any shyness at her male attire appeared upon her countenance could not be seen by reason of the strips of ribbon which used to cover the face in mumming costumes, representing the barred visor of the mediæval helmet. [/]
[Eustacia] “ It fits pretty well, ” she said, looking down at the white overalls, “ except that the tunic, or whatever you call it, is long in the sleeve. [/]
[Eustacia] The bottom of the overalls I can turn up inside. [/]
[Eustacia] Now pay attention. ” [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia then proceeded in her delivery, striking the sword against the staff or lance at the minatory phrases, in the orthodox mumming manner, and strutting up and down. [/]
[Narrator] Charley seasoned his admiration with criticism of the gentlest kind, for the touch of Eustacia ’s hand yet remained with him. [/]
[Eustacia] “ And now for your excuse to the others, ” she said. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Where do you meet before you go to Mrs. Yeobright ’s? ” “ We thought of meeting here, miss, if you have nothing to say against it. [/]
[Eustacia] At eight o’clock, so as to get there by nine. ” “ Yes. [/]
[Eustacia] Well, you of course must not appear. [/]
[Eustacia] I will march in about five minutes late, ready - dressed, and tell them that you ca n’t come. [/]
[Eustacia] I have decided that the best plan will be for you to be sent somewhere by me, to make a real thing of the excuse. [/]
[Eustacia] Our two heath - croppers are in the habit of straying into the meads, and tomorrow evening you can go and see if they are gone there. [/]
[Eustacia] I ’ll manage the rest. [/]
[Eustacia] Now you may leave me. ” [/]
[Eustacia] “ Yes, miss. [/]
[Eustacia] But I think I ’ll have one minute more of what I am owed, if you do n’t mind. ” [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia gave him her hand as before. [/]
[Eustacia] “ One minute, ” she said, and counted on till she reached seven or eight minutes. [/]
[Narrator] Hand and person she then withdrew to a distance of several feet, and recovered some of her old dignity. [/]
[Narrator] The contract completed, she raised between them a barrier impenetrable as a wall. [/]
[Him] “ There, ’ tis all gone; and I did n’t mean quite all, ” he said, with a sigh. [/]
[Eustacia] “ You had good measure, ” said she, turning away. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Yes, miss. [/]
[Eustacia] Well, ’ tis over, and now I ’ll get home - along. ” [/]
[Narrator] V. Through the Moonlight The next evening the mummers were assembled in the same spot, awaiting the entrance of the Turkish Knight. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Twenty minutes after eight by the Quiet Woman, and Charley not come. ” [/]
[Eustacia] “ Ten minutes past by Blooms - End. ” “ It wants ten minutes to, by Grandfer Cantle ’s watch. ” [/]
[Eustacia] “ And ’ tis five minutes past by the captain ’s clock. ” [/]
[Narrator] On Egdon there was no absolute hour of the day. [/]
[Narrator] The time at any moment was a number of varying doctrines professed by the different hamlets, some of them having originally grown up from a common root, and then become divided by secession, some having been alien from the beginning. [/]
[Narrator] West Egdon believed in Blooms - End time, East Egdon in the time of the Quiet Woman Inn. [/]
[Narrator] Grandfer Cantle ’s watch had numbered many followers in years gone by, but since he had grown older faiths were shaken. [/]
[Narrator] Thus, the mummers having gathered hither from scattered points each came with his own tenets on early and late; and they waited a little longer as a compromise. [/]
[Eustacia] Eustacia had watched the assemblage through the hole; and seeing that now was the proper moment to enter, she went from the “ linhay ” and boldly pulled the bobbin of the fuelhouse door. [/]
[Narrator] Her grandfather was safe at the Quiet Woman. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Here ’s Charley at last! [/]
[Narrator] How late you be, Charley. ” “ ’ Tis not Charley, ” said the Turkish Knight from within his visor. [/]
[TheTurkishKnight] “ ’ Tis a cousin of Miss Vye ’s, come to take Charley ’s place from curiosity. [/]
[TheTurkishKnight] He was obliged to go and look for the heath - croppers that have got into the meads, and I agreed to take his place, as he knew he could n’t come back here again tonight. [/]
[TheTurkishKnight] I know the part as well as he. ” [/]
[Narrator] Her graceful gait, elegant figure, and dignified manner in general won the mummers to the opinion that they had gained by the exchange, if the newcomer were perfect in his part. [/]
[SaintGeorge] “ It do n’t matter — if you be not too young, ” said Saint George. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia ’s voice had sounded somewhat more juvenile and fluty than Charley ’s. [/]
[Eustacia] “ I know every word of it, I tell you, ” said Eustacia decisively. [/]
[Narrator] Dash being all that was required to carry her triumphantly through, she adopted as much as was necessary. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Go ahead, lads, with the try - over. [/]
[Eustacia] I ’ll challenge any of you to find a mistake in me. ” [/]
[Narrator] The play was hastily rehearsed, whereupon the other mummers were delighted with the new knight. [/]
[Narrator] They extinguished the candles at half - past eight, and set out upon the heath in the direction of Mrs. Yeobright ’s house at Bloom’s - End. [/]
[Narrator] There was a slight hoarfrost that night, and the moon, though not more than half full, threw a spirited and enticing brightness upon the fantastic figures of the mumming band, whose plumes and ribbons rustled in their walk like autumn leaves. [/]
[Narrator] Their path was not over Rainbarrow now, but down a valley which left that ancient elevation a little to the east. [/]
[Narrator] The bottom of the vale was green to a width of ten yards or thereabouts, and the shining facets of frost upon the blades of grass seemed to move on with the shadows of those they surrounded. [/]
[Narrator] The masses of furze and heath to the right and left were dark as ever; a mere half - moon was powerless to silver such sable features as theirs. [/]
[Narrator] Half - an - hour of walking and talking brought them to the spot in the valley where the grass riband widened and led down to the front of the house. [/]
[Narrator] At sight of the place Eustacia who had felt a few passing doubts during her walk with the youths, again was glad that the adventure had been undertaken. [/]
[Narrator] She had come out to see a man who might possibly have the power to deliver her soul from a most deadly oppression. [/]
[Narrator] What was Wildeve? [/]
[Narrator] Interesting, but inadequate. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps she would see a sufficient hero tonight. [/]
[Narrator] As they drew nearer to the front of the house the mummers became aware that music and dancing were briskly flourishing within. [/]
[Narrator] Every now and then a long low note from the serpent, which was the chief wind instrument played at these times, advanced further into the heath than the thin treble part, and reached their ears alone; and next a more than usual loud tread from a dancer would come the same way. [/]
[AManWhoMightPossiblyHaveThePowerToDeliverHerSoulFromAMostDeadlyOppression] With nearer approach these fragmentary sounds became pieced together, and were found to be the salient points of the tune called “ Nancy ’s Fancy. ” [/]
[Narrator] He was there, of course. [/]
[Narrator] Who was she that he danced with? [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps some unknown woman, far beneath herself in culture, was by the most subtle of lures sealing his fate this very instant. [/]
[Narrator] To dance with a man is to concentrate a twelvemonth ’s regulation fire upon him in the fragment of an hour. [/]
[Narrator] To pass to courtship without acquaintance, to pass to marriage without courtship, is a skipping of terms reserved for those alone who tread this royal road. [/]
[Narrator] She would see how his heart lay by keen observation of them all. [/]
[Narrator] The enterprising lady followed the mumming company through the gate in the white paling, and stood before the open porch. [/]
[Narrator] The house was encrusted with heavy thatchings, which dropped between the upper windows; the front, upon which the moonbeams directly played, had originally been white; but a huge pyracanth now darkened the greater portion. [/]
[Narrator] It became at once evident that the dance was proceeding immediately within the surface of the door, no apartment intervening. [/]
[Narrator] The brushing of skirts and elbows, sometimes the bumping of shoulders, could be heard against the very panels. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia, though living within two miles of the place, had never seen the interior of this quaint old habitation. [/]
[Narrator] Between Captain Vye and the Yeobrights there had never existed much acquaintance, the former having come as a stranger and purchased the long - empty house at Mistover Knap not long before the death of Mrs. Yeobright ’s husband; and with that event and the departure of her son such friendship as had grown up became quite broken off. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Is there no passage inside the door, then? ” asked Eustacia as they stood within the porch. [/]
[TheLadWhoPlayedTheSaracen] “ No, ” said the lad who played the Saracen. [/]
[TheLadWhoPlayedTheSaracen] “ The door opens right upon the front sitting - room, where the spree ’s going on. ” [/]
[Eustacia] “ So that we can not open the door without stopping the dance. ” [/]
[TheLadWhoPlayedTheSaracen] “ That ’s it. [/]
[Narrator] Here we must bide till they have done, for they always bolt the back door after dark. ” “ They wo n’t be much longer, ” said Father Christmas. [/]
[Narrator] This assertion, however, was hardly borne out by the event. [/]
[Narrator] Again the instruments ended the tune; again they recommenced with as much fire and pathos as if it were the first strain. [/]
[TheseOutsidersUnderTheMoon] The air was now that one without any particular beginning, middle, or end, which perhaps, among all the dances which throng an inspired fiddler ’s fancy, best conveys the idea of the interminable — the celebrated “ Devil ’s Dream. ” [/]
[Narrator] The fury of personal movement that was kindled by the fury of the notes could be approximately imagined by these outsiders under the moon, from the occasional kicks of toes and heels against the door, whenever the whirl round had been of more than customary velocity. [/]
[Narrator] The first five minutes of listening was interesting enough to the mummers. [/]
[Eustacia] The five minutes extended to ten minutes, and these to a quarter of an hour; but no signs of ceasing were audible in the lively “ Dream. ” [/]
[Narrator] The bumping against the door, the laughter, the stamping, were all as vigorous as ever, and the pleasure in being outside lessened considerably. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Why does Mrs. Yeobright give parties of this sort? ” [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia asked, a little surprised to hear merriment so pronounced. [/]
[TheTurkishKnight] “ It is not one of her bettermost parlour - parties. [/]
[TheTurkishKnight] She ’s asked the plain neighbours and workpeople without drawing any lines, just to give ’em a good supper and such like. [/]
[TheTurkishKnight] Her son and she wait upon the folks. ” [/]
[Eustacia] “ I see, ” said Eustacia. [/]
[SaintGeorge] “ ’ Tis the last strain, I think, ” said Saint George, with his ear to the panel. [/]
[Narrator] “ A young man and woman have just swung into this corner, and he ’s saying to her, ‘ Ah, the pity; ’ tis over for us this time, my own. ’ ” “ Thank God, ” said the Turkish Knight, stamping, and taking from the wall the conventional lance that each of the mummers carried. [/]
[Narrator] Her boots being thinner than those of the young men, the hoar had damped her feet and made them cold. [/]
[TheValiantSoldier] “ Upon my song ’ tis another ten minutes for us, ” said the Valiant Soldier, looking through the keyhole as the tune modulated into another without stopping. [/]
[Narrator] “ Grandfer Cantle is standing in this corner, waiting his turn. ” “ ’ Twon’t be long; ’ tis a six - handed reel, ” said the Doctor. [/]
[TheSaracen] “ Why not go in, dancing or no? [/]
[TheSaracen] They sent for us, ” said the Saracen. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Certainly not, ” said Eustacia authoritatively, as she paced smartly up and down from door to gate to warm herself. [/]
[Narrator] “ We should burst into the middle of them and stop the dance, and that would be unmannerly. ” “ He thinks himself somebody because he has had a bit more schooling than we, ” said the Doctor. [/]
[Eustacia] “ You may go to the deuce! ” said Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] There was a whispered conversation between three or four of them, and one turned to her. [/]
[One] “ Will you tell us one thing? ” [/]
[Narrator] he said, not without gentleness. [/]
[One] “ Be you Miss Vye? [/]
[Narrator] We think you must be. ” “ You may think what you like, ” said Eustacia slowly. [/]
[Eustacia] “ But honourable lads will not tell tales upon a lady. ” [/]
[One] “ We ’ll say nothing, miss. [/]
[Narrator] That ’s upon our honour. ” “ Thank you, ” she replied. [/]
[Narrator] At this moment the fiddles finished off with a screech, and the serpent emitted a last note that nearly lifted the roof. [/]
[Narrator] When, from the comparative quiet within, the mummers judged that the dancers had taken their seats, Father Christmas advanced, lifted the latch, and put his head inside the door. [/]
[SeveralGuests] “ Ah, the mummers, the mummers! ” cried several guests at once. [/]
[Narrator] “ Clear a space for the mummers. ” Humpbacked Father Christmas then made a complete entry, swinging his huge club, and in a general way clearing the stage for the actors proper, while he informed the company in smart verse that he was come, welcome or welcome not; concluding his speech with “ Make room, make room, my gallant boys, And give us space to rhyme; We ’ve come to show Saint George ’s play, Upon this Christmas time. ” [/]
[Narrator] The guests were now arranging themselves at one end of the room, the fiddler was mending a string, the serpent - player was emptying his mouthpiece, and the play began. [/]
[FirstOfThoseOutsideTheValiantSoldier] First of those outside the Valiant Soldier entered, in the interest of Saint George — “ Here come I, the Valiant Soldier; Slasher is my name ”; and so on. [/]
[Narrator] This speech concluded with a challenge to the infidel, at the end of which it was Eustacia ’s duty to enter as the Turkish Knight. [/]
[Narrator] She, with the rest who were not yet on, had hitherto remained in the moonlight which streamed under the porch. [/]
[Eustacia] With no apparent effort or backwardness she came in, beginning — “ Here come I, a Turkish Knight, Who learnt in Turkish land to fight; I ’ll fight this man with courage bold: If his blood ’s hot I ’ll make it cold! ” [/]
[Narrator] During her declamation Eustacia held her head erect, and spoke as roughly as she could, feeling pretty secure from observation. [/]
[Narrator] But the concentration upon her part necessary to prevent discovery, the newness of the scene, the shine of the candles, and the confusing effect upon her vision of the ribboned visor which hid her features, left her absolutely unable to perceive who were present as spectators. [/]
[Narrator] On the further side of a table bearing candles she could faintly discern faces, and that was all. [/]
[Jim] Meanwhile Jim Starks as the Valiant Soldier had come forward, and, with a glare upon the Turk, replied — “ If, then, thou art that Turkish Knight, Draw out thy sword, and let us fight! ” [/]
[Narrator] And fight they did; the issue of the combat being that the Valiant Soldier was slain by a preternaturally inadequate thrust from Eustacia, Jim, in his ardour for genuine histrionic art, coming down like a log upon the stone floor with force enough to dislocate his shoulder. [/]
[SaintGeorge] Then, after more words from the Turkish Knight, rather too faintly delivered, and statements that he ’d fight Saint George and all his crew, Saint George himself magnificently entered with the well - known flourish — “ Here come I, Saint George, the valiant man, With naked sword and spear in hand, Who fought the dragon and brought him to the slaughter, [/]
[SaintGeorge] And by this won fair Sabra, the King of Egypt ’s daughter; [/]
[SaintGeorge] What mortal man would dare to stand Before me with my sword in hand? ” [/]
[Narrator] This was the lad who had first recognized Eustacia; and when she now, as the Turk, replied with suitable defiance, and at once began the combat, the young fellow took especial care to use his sword as gently as possible. [/]
[Narrator] Being wounded, the Knight fell upon one knee, according to the direction. [/]
[Narrator] The Doctor now entered, restored the Knight by giving him a draught from the bottle which he carried, and the fight was again resumed, the Turk sinking by degrees until quite overcome — dying as hard in this venerable drama as he is said to do at the present day. [/]
[Narrator] This gradual sinking to the earth was, in fact, one reason why Eustacia had thought that the part of the Turkish Knight, though not the shortest, would suit her best. [/]
[Narrator] A direct fall from upright to horizontal, which was the end of the other fighting characters, was not an elegant or decorous part for a girl. [/]
[Narrator] But it was easy to die like a Turk, by a dogged decline. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia was now among the number of the slain, though not on the floor, for she had managed to sink into a sloping position against the clock - case, so that her head was well elevated. [/]
[Narrator] The play proceeded between Saint George, the Saracen, the Doctor, and Father Christmas; and Eustacia, having no more to do, for the first time found leisure to observe the scene round, and to search for the form that had drawn her hither. VI. [/]
[Narrator] The Two Stand Face to Face The room had been arranged with a view to the dancing, the large oak table having been moved back till it stood as a breastwork to the fireplace. [/]
[Narrator] At each end, behind, and in the chimney - corner were grouped the guests, many of them being warm - faced and panting, among whom Eustacia cursorily recognized some well - to - do persons from beyond the heath. [/]

[Narrator] A nose, chin, hands, knees, and toes projected from the seat within the chimney opening, which members she found to unite in the person of Grandfer Cantle, Mrs. Yeobright ’s occasional assistant in the garden, and therefore one of the invited. [/]
[Narrator] The smoke went up from an Etna of peat in front of him, played round the notches of the chimney - crook, struck against the salt - box, and got lost among the flitches. [/]
[Narrator] Another part of the room soon riveted her gaze. [/]
[Narrator] At the other side of the chimney stood the settle, which is the necessary supplement to a fire so open that nothing less than a strong breeze will carry up the smoke. [/]
[Narrator] It is, to the hearths of old - fashioned cavernous fireplaces, what the east belt of trees is to the exposed country estate, or the north wall to the garden. [/]
[Narrator] Outside the settle candles gutter, locks of hair wave, young women shiver, and old men sneeze. [/]
[Narrator] Inside is Paradise. [/]
[Narrator] Not a symptom of a draught disturbs the air; the sitters ’ backs are as warm as their faces, and songs and old tales are drawn from the occupants by the comfortable heat, like fruit from melon plants in a frame. [/]
[Narrator] It was, however, not with those who sat in the settle that Eustacia was concerned. [/]
[Narrator] A face showed itself with marked distinctness against the dark - tanned wood of the upper part. [/]
[Narrator] The owner, who was leaning against the settle ’s outer end, was Clement Yeobright, or Clym, as he was called here; she knew it could be nobody else. [/]
[Narrator] The spectacle constituted an area of two feet in Rembrandt ’s intensest manner. [/]
[Narrator] A strange power in the lounger ’s appearance lay in the fact that, though his whole figure was visible, the observer ’s eye was only aware of his face. [/]
[Narrator] To one of middle age the countenance was that of a young man, though a youth might hardly have seen any necessity for the term of immaturity. [/]
[Narrator] But it was really one of those faces which convey less the idea of so many years as its age than of so much experience as its store. [/]
[Narrator] The number of their years may have adequately summed up Jared, Mahalaleel, and the rest of the antediluvians, but the age of a modern man is to be measured by the intensity of his history. [/]
[Narrator] The face was well shaped, even excellently. [/]
[Narrator] But the mind within was beginning to use it as a mere waste tablet whereon to trace its idiosyncrasies as they developed themselves. [/]
[Narrator] The beauty here visible would in no long time be ruthlessly over - run by its parasite, thought, which might just as well have fed upon a plainer exterior where there was nothing it could harm. [/]
[People] Had Heaven preserved Yeobright from a wearing habit of meditation, people would have said, “ A handsome man. ” [/]
[People] Had his brain unfolded under sharper contours they would have said, “ A thoughtful man. ” [/]
[Narrator] But an inner strenuousness was preying upon an outer symmetry, and they rated his look as singular. [/]
[Narrator] Hence people who began by beholding him ended by perusing him. [/]
[Narrator] His countenance was overlaid with legible meanings. [/]
[Narrator] Without being thought - worn he yet had certain marks derived from a perception of his surroundings, such as are not unfrequently found on men at the end of the four or five years of endeavour which follow the close of placid pupilage. [/]
[Narrator] He already showed that thought is a disease of flesh, and indirectly bore evidence that ideal physical beauty is incompatible with emotional development and a full recognition of the coil of things. [/]
[Narrator] Mental luminousness must be fed with the oil of life, even though there is already a physical need for it; and the pitiful sight of two demands on one supply was just showing itself here. [/]
[Narrator] When standing before certain men the philosopher regrets that thinkers are but perishable tissue, the artist that perishable tissue has to think. [/]
[Narrator] Thus to deplore, each from his point of view, the mutually destructive interdependence of spirit and flesh would have been instinctive with these in critically observing Yeobright. [/]
[Narrator] As for his look, it was a natural cheerfulness striving against depression from without, and not quite succeeding. [/]
[Narrator] The look suggested isolation, but it revealed something more. [/]
[Narrator] As is usual with bright natures, the deity that lies ignominiously chained within an ephemeral human carcase shone out of him like a ray. [/]
[Narrator] The effect upon Eustacia was palpable. [/]
[Narrator] The extraordinary pitch of excitement that she had reached beforehand would, indeed, have caused her to be influenced by the most commonplace man. [/]
[Narrator] She was troubled at Yeobright ’s presence. [/]

[Narrator] At moments during this performance Eustacia was half in doubt about the security of her position; yet it had a fearful joy. [/]

[/]
[Narrator] Should there be a classic period to art hereafter, its Pheidias may produce such faces. [/]
[Narrator] The view of life as a thing to be put up with, replacing that zest for existence which was so intense in early civilizations, must ultimately enter so thoroughly into the constitution of the advanced races that its facial expression will become accepted as a new artistic departure. [/]
[Narrator] People already feel that a man who lives without disturbing a curve of feature, or setting a mark of mental concern anywhere upon himself, is too far removed from modern perceptiveness to be a modern type. [/]
[Narrator] Physically beautiful men — the glory of the race when it was young — are almost an anachronism now; and we may wonder whether, at some time or other, physically beautiful women may not be an anachronism likewise. [/]
[Narrator] The truth seems to be that a long line of disillusive centuries has permanently displaced the Hellenic idea of life, or whatever it may be called. [/]
[Narrator] What the Greeks only suspected we know well; what their Æschylus imagined our nursery children feel. [/]
[Narrator] That old - fashioned revelling in the general situation grows less and less possible as we uncover the defects of natural laws, and see the quandary that man is in by their operation. [/]
[Narrator] The lineaments which will get embodied in ideals based upon this new recognition will probably be akin to those of Yeobright. [/]
[Narrator] The observer ’s eye was arrested, not by his face as a picture, but by his face as a page; not by what it was, but by what it recorded. [/]
[Narrator] His features were attractive in the light of symbols, as sounds intrinsically common become attractive in language, and as shapes intrinsically simple become interesting in writing. [/]
[Narrator] He had been a lad of whom something was expected. [/]
[Narrator] Beyond this all had been chaos. [/]
[Narrator] That he would be successful in an original way, or that he would go to the dogs in an original way, seemed equally probable. [/]
[Narrator] The only absolute certainty about him was that he would not stand still in the circumstances amid which he was born. [/]
[TheListener] Hence, when his name was casually mentioned by neighbouring yeomen, the listener said, “ Ah, Clym Yeobright — what is he doing now? ” [/]
[Narrator] When the instinctive question about a person is, What is he doing? [/]
[Narrator] it is felt that he will be found to be, like most of us, doing nothing in particular. [/]
[Narrator] There is an indefinite sense that he must be invading some region of singularity, good or bad. [/]
[Narrator] The devout hope is that he is doing well. [/]
[Narrator] The secret faith is that he is making a mess of it. [/]
[Narrator] Half a dozen comfortable market - men, who were habitual callers at the Quiet Woman as they passed by in their carts, were partial to the topic. [/]
[Narrator] In fact, though they were not Egdon men, they could hardly avoid it while they sucked their long clay tubes and regarded the heath through the window. [/]
[Narrator] Clym had been so inwoven with the heath in his boyhood that hardly anybody could look upon it without thinking of him. [/]
