[Narrator] The New Course Causes Disappointment Yeobright loved his kind. [/]
[Narrator] He had a conviction that the want of most men was knowledge of a sort which brings wisdom rather than affluence. [/]
[Narrator] He wished to raise the class at the expense of individuals rather than individuals at the expense of the class. [/]
[Narrator] What was more, he was ready at once to be the first unit sacrificed. [/]
[Narrator] In passing from the bucolic to the intellectual life the intermediate stages are usually two at least, frequently many more; and one of those stages is almost sure to be worldly advanced. [/]
[Narrator] We can hardly imagine bucolic placidity quickening to intellectual aims without imagining social aims as the transitional phase. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright ’s local peculiarity was that in striving at high thinking he still cleaved to plain living — nay, wild and meagre living in many respects, and brotherliness with clowns. [/]
[Narrator] He was a John the Baptist who took ennoblement rather than repentance for his text. [/]
[Narrator] Mentally he was in a provincial future, that is, he was in many points abreast with the central town thinkers of his date. [/]
[Narrator] Much of this development he may have owed to his studious life in Paris, where he had become acquainted with ethical systems popular at the time. [/]
[Narrator] In consequence of this relatively advanced position, Yeobright might have been called unfortunate. [/]
[Narrator] The rural world was not ripe for him. [/]
[Narrator] A man should be only partially before his time — to be completely to the vanward in aspirations is fatal to fame. [/]
[Narrator] Had Philip ’s warlike son been intellectually so far ahead as to have attempted civilization without bloodshed, he would have been twice the godlike hero that he seemed, but nobody would have heard of an Alexander. [/]
[Narrator] In the interests of renown the forwardness should lie chiefly in the capacity to handle things. [/]
[Narrator] Successful propagandists have succeeded because the doctrine they bring into form is that which their listeners have for some time felt without being able to shape. [/]
[Narrator] A man who advocates æsthetic effort and deprecates social effort is only likely to be understood by a class to which social effort has become a stale matter. [/]
[Narrator] To argue upon the possibility of culture before luxury to the bucolic world may be to argue truly, but it is an attempt to disturb a sequence to which humanity has been long accustomed. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright preaching to the Egdon eremites that they might rise to a serene comprehensiveness without going through the process of enriching themselves was not unlike arguing to ancient Chaldeans that in ascending from earth to the pure empyrean it was not necessary to pass first into the intervening heaven of ether. [/]
[Narrator] Was Yeobright ’s mind well - proportioned? [/]
[Narrator] No. [/]
[Narrator] A well proportioned mind is one which shows no particular bias; one of which we may safely say that it will never cause its owner to be confined as a madman, tortured as a heretic, or crucified as a blasphemer. [/]
[Narrator] Also, on the other hand, that it will never cause him to be applauded as a prophet, revered as a priest, or exalted as a king. [/]
[Narrator] Its usual blessings are happiness and mediocrity. [/]
[Narrator] It produces the poetry of Rogers, the paintings of West, the statecraft of North, the spiritual guidance of Tomline; enabling its possessors to find their way to wealth, to wind up well, to step with dignity off the stage, to die comfortably in their beds, and to get the decent monument which, in many cases, they deserve. [/]
[Narrator] It never would have allowed Yeobright to do such a ridiculous thing as throw up his business to benefit his fellow - creatures. [/]
[Narrator] He walked along towards home without attending to paths. [/]
[Narrator] If anyone knew the heath [/]
[Narrator] well it was Clym. [/]
[Narrator] He was permeated with its scenes, with its substance, and with its odours. [/]
[Narrator] He might be said to be its product. [/]
[ItsOwner] His eyes had first opened thereon; with its appearance all the first images of his memory were mingled, his estimate of life had been coloured by it: his toys had been the flint knives and arrow - heads which he found there, wondering why stones should “ grow ” to such odd shapes; his flowers, the purple bells and yellow furze: his animal kingdom, the snakes and croppers; his society, its human haunters. [/]
[Narrator] Take all the varying hates felt by Eustacia Vye towards the heath, and translate them into loves, and you have the heart of Clym. [/]
[Narrator] He gazed upon the wide prospect as he walked, and was glad. [/]
[Narrator] To many persons this Egdon was a place which had slipped out of its century generations ago, to intrude as an uncouth object into this. [/]
[Narrator] It was an obsolete thing, and few cared to study it. [/]
[Narrator] How could this be otherwise in the days of square fields, plashed hedges, and meadows watered on a plan so rectangular that on a fine day they looked like silver gridirons? [/]
[Narrator] The farmer, in his ride, who could smile at artificial grasses, look with solicitude at the coming corn, and sigh with sadness at the fly - eaten turnips, bestowed upon the distant upland of heath nothing better than a frown. [/]
[Narrator] But as for Yeobright, when he looked from the heights on his way he could not help indulging in a barbarous satisfaction at observing that, in some of the attempts at reclamation from the waste, tillage, after holding on for a year or two, had receded again in despair, the ferns and furze - tufts stubbornly reasserting themselves. [/]
[Narrator] He descended into the valley, and soon reached his home at Blooms - End. [/]
[Narrator] His mother was snipping dead leaves from the window - plants. [/]
[Narrator] She looked up at him as if she did not understand the meaning of his long stay with her; her face had worn that look for several days. [/]
[Narrator] He could perceive that the curiosity which had been shown by the hair - cutting group amounted in his mother to concern. [/]
[Narrator] But she had asked no question with her lips, even when the arrival of his trunk suggested that he was not going to leave her soon. [/]
[Narrator] Her silence besought an explanation of him more loudly than words. [/]
[ItsOwner] “ I am not going back to Paris again, Mother, ” he said. [/]
[ItsOwner] “ At least, in my old capacity. [/]
[ItsOwner] I have given up the business. ” [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright turned in pained surprise. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ I thought something was amiss, because of the boxes. [/]
[MrsYeobright] I wonder you did not tell me sooner. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ I ought to have done it. [/]
[MrsYeobright] But I have been in doubt whether you would be pleased with my plan. [/]
[MrsYeobright] I was not quite clear on a few points myself. [/]
[MrsYeobright] I am going to take an entirely new course. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ I am astonished, Clym. [/]
[MrsYeobright] How can you want to do better than you ’ve been doing? ” “ Very easily. [/]
[MrsYeobright] But I shall not do better in the way you mean; I suppose it will be called doing worse. [/]
[MrsYeobright] But I hate that business of mine, and I want to do some worthy thing before I die. [/]
[MrsYeobright] As a schoolmaster I think to do it — a school - master to the poor and ignorant, to teach them what nobody else will. ” “ After all the trouble that has been taken to give you a start, and when there is nothing to do but to keep straight on towards affluence, you say you will be a poor man ’s schoolmaster. [/]
[MrsYeobright] Your fancies will be your ruin, Clym. ” Mrs. Yeobright spoke calmly, but the force of feeling behind the words was but too apparent to one who knew her as well as her son did. [/]
[Narrator] He did not answer. [/]
[Narrator] There was in his face that hopelessness of being understood which comes when the objector is constitutionally beyond the reach of a logic that, even under favouring conditions, is almost too coarse a vehicle for the subtlety of the argument. [/]
[Narrator] No more was said on the subject till the end of dinner. [/]
[Narrator] His mother then began, as if there had been no interval since the morning. [/]
[HisMother] “ It disturbs me, Clym, to find that you have come home with such thoughts as those. [/]
[HisMother] I had n’t the least idea that you meant to go backward in the world by your own free choice. [/]
[HisMother] Of course, I have always supposed you were going to push straight on, as other men do — all who deserve the name — when they have been put in a good way of doing well. ” [/]
[Clym] “ I can not help it, ” said Clym, in a troubled tone. [/]
[Clym] “ Mother, I hate the flashy business. [/]
[Clym] Talk about men who deserve the name, can any man deserving the name waste his time in that effeminate way, when he sees half the world going to ruin for want of somebody to buckle to and teach them how to breast the misery they are born to? [/]
[Clym] I get up every morning and see the whole creation groaning and travailing in pain, as St. Paul says, and yet there am I, trafficking in glittering splendours with wealthy women and titled libertines, and pandering to the meanest vanities — I, who have health and strength enough for anything. [/]
[Clym] I have been troubled in my mind about it all the year, and the end is that I can not do it any more. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ Why ca n’t you do it as well as others? ” “ I do n’t know, except that there are many things other people care for which I do n’t; and that ’s partly why I think I ought to do this. [/]
[Clym] For one thing, my body does not require much of me. [/]
[Clym] I can not enjoy delicacies; good things are wasted upon me. [/]
[Clym] Well, I ought to turn that defect to advantage, and by being able to do without what other people require I can spend what such things cost upon anybody else. ” [/]
[Narrator] Now, Yeobright, having inherited some of these very instincts from the woman before him, could not fail to awaken a reciprocity in her through her feelings, if not by arguments, disguise it as she might for his good. [/]
[Narrator] She spoke with less assurance. [/]
[TheWomanBeforeHim] “ And yet you might have been a wealthy man if you had only persevered. [/]
[TheWomanBeforeHim] Manager to that large diamond establishment — what better can a man wish for? [/]
[TheWomanBeforeHim] What a post of trust and respect! [/]
[Narrator] I suppose you will be like your father; like him, you are getting weary of doing well. ” “ No, ” said her son, “ I am not weary of that, though I am weary of what you mean by it. [/]
[Narrator] Mother, what is doing well? ” Mrs. Yeobright was far too thoughtful a woman to be content with ready definitions, and, like the “ What is wisdom? ” of Plato ’s Socrates, and the “ What is truth? ” of Pontius Pilate, Yeobright ’s burning question received no answer. [/]
[Narrator] The silence was broken by the clash of the garden gate, a tap at the door, and its opening. [/]
[Narrator] Christian Cantle appeared in the room in his Sunday clothes. [/]
[Narrator] It was the custom on Egdon to begin the preface to a story before absolutely entering the house, so as to be well in for the body of the narrative by the time visitor and visited stood face to face. [/]
[Narrator] Christian had been saying to them while the door was leaving its latch, “ To think that I, who go from home but once in a while, and hardly then, should have been there this morning! ” “ ’ Tis news you have brought us, then, Christian? ” said Mrs. Yeobright. [/]
[Christian] “ Ay, sure, about a witch, and ye must overlook my time o ’ day; for, says I, ‘ I must go and tell ’em, though they wo n’t have half done dinner. ’ [/]
[Christian] I assure ye it made me shake like a driven leaf. [/]
[Christian] Do ye think any harm will come o’t? ” “ Well — what? ” “ This morning at church we was all standing up, and the pa’son said, ‘ Let us pray. ’ [/]
[Christian] ‘ Well, ’ thinks I, ‘ one may as well kneel as stand ’; so down I went; and, more than that, all the rest were as willing to oblige the man as I. [/]
[Christian] We had n’t been hard at it for more than a minute when a most terrible screech sounded through church, as if somebody had just gied up their heart ’s blood. [/]
[Christian] All the folk jumped up and then we found that Susan Nunsuch had pricked Miss Vye with a long stocking - needle, as she had threatened to do as soon as ever she could get the young lady to church, where she do n’t come very often. [/]
[Christian] She’ve waited for this chance for weeks, so as to draw her blood and put an end to the bewitching of Susan ’s children that has been carried on so long. [/]
[Christian] Sue followed her into church, sat next to her, and as soon as she could find a chance in went the stocking - needle into my lady ’s arm. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ Good heaven, how horrid! ” said Mrs. Yeobright. [/]
[Christian] “ Sue pricked her that deep that the maid fainted away; and as I was afeard there might be some tumult among us, I got behind the bass viol and did n’t see no more. [/]
[Christian] But they carried her out into the air, ’ tis said; but when they looked round for Sue she was gone. [/]
[Christian] What a scream that girl gied, poor thing! [/]
[Christian] There were the pa’son in his surplice holding up his hand and saying, ‘ Sit down, my good people, sit down! ’ [/]
[Christian] But the deuce a bit would they sit down. [/]
[Christian] O, and what d’ye think I found out, Mrs. Yeobright? [/]
[Narrator] The pa’son wears a suit of clothes under his surplice! —I could see his black sleeves when he held up his arm. ” “ ’ Tis a cruel thing, ” said Yeobright. [/]
[HisMother] “ Yes, ” said his mother. [/]
[Christian] “ The nation ought to look into it, ” said Christian. [/]
[Christian] “ Here ’s Humphrey coming, I think. ” [/]
[Narrator] In came Humphrey. [/]
[Humphrey] “ Well, have ye heard the news? [/]
[Humphrey] But I see you have. [/]
[Humphrey] ’ Tis a very strange thing that whenever one of Egdon folk goes to church some rum job or other is sure to be doing. [/]
[Humphrey] The last time one of us was there was when neighbour Fairway went in the fall; and that was the day you forbad the banns, Mrs. Yeobright. ” [/]
[Clym] “ Has this cruelly treated girl been able to walk home? ” said Clym. [/]
[Humphrey] “ They say she got better, and went home very well. [/]
[Humphrey] And now I ’ve told it I must be moving homeward myself. ” [/]
[Humphrey] “ And I, ” said Humphrey. [/]
[Narrator] “ Truly now we shall see if there ’s anything in what folks say about her. ” When they were gone into the heath again Yeobright said quietly to his mother, “ Do you think I have turned teacher too soon? ” “ It is right that there should be schoolmasters, and missionaries, and all such men, ” she replied. [/]
[HisMother] “ But it is right, too, that I should try to lift you out of this life into something richer, and that you should not come back again, and be as if I had not tried at all. ” [/]
[Narrator] Later in the day Sam, the turf - cutter, entered. [/]
[Sam] “ I ’ve come a - borrowing, Mrs. Yeobright. [/]
[Narrator] I suppose you have heard what ’s been happening to the beauty on the hill? ” “ Yes, Sam: half a dozen have been telling us. ” “ Beauty? ” said Clym. [/]
[Sam] “ Yes, tolerably well - favoured, ” Sam replied. [/]
[Sam] “ Lord! [/]
[Sam] all the country owns that ’ tis one of the strangest things in the world that such a woman should have come to live up there. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ Dark or fair? ” “ Now, though I ’ve seen her twenty times, that ’s a thing I can not call to mind. ” “ Darker than Tamsin, ” murmured Mrs. Yeobright. [/]
[Narrator] “ A woman who seems to care for nothing at all, as you may say. ” “ She is melancholy, then? ” inquired Clym. [/]
[Sam] “ She mopes about by herself, and do n’t mix in with the people. ” [/]
[Sam] “ Is she a young lady inclined for adventures? ” “ Not to my knowledge. ” “ Does n’t join in with the lads in their games, to get some sort of excitement in this lonely place? ” “ No. ” “ Mumming, for instance? ” “ No. [/]
[Sam] Her notions be different. [/]
[Sam] I should rather say her thoughts were far away from here, with lords and ladies she ’ll never know, and mansions she ’ll never see again. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] Observing that Clym appeared singularly interested Mrs. Yeobright said rather uneasily to Sam, “ You see more in her than most of us do. [/]
[MrsYeobright] Miss Vye is to my mind too idle to be charming. [/]
[MrsYeobright] I have never heard that she is of any use to herself or to other people. [/]
[MrsYeobright] Good girls do n’t get treated as witches even on Egdon. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ Nonsense — that proves nothing either way, ” said Yeobright. [/]
[Sam] “ Well, of course I do n’t understand such niceties, ” said Sam, withdrawing from a possibly unpleasant argument; “ and what she is we must wait for time to tell us. [/]
[Sam] The business that I have really called about is this, to borrow the longest and strongest rope you have. [/]
[Sam] The captain ’s bucket has dropped into the well, and they are in want of water; and as all the chaps are at home today we think we can get it out for him. [/]
[Sam] We have three cart - ropes already, but they wo n’t reach to the bottom. ” [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright told him that he might have whatever ropes he could find in the outhouse, and Sam went out to search. [/]
[Narrator] When he passed by the door Clym joined him, and accompanied him to the gate. [/]
[Sam] “ Is this young witch - lady going to stay long at Mistover? ” he asked. [/]
[Clym] “ I should say so. ” [/]
[Clym] “ What a cruel shame to ill - use her, She must have suffered greatly — more in mind than in body. ” “ ’ Twas a graceless trick — such a handsome girl, too. [/]
[Clym] You ought to see her, Mr. Yeobright, being a young man come from far, and with a little more to show for your years than most of us. ” [/]
[Clym] “ Do you think she would like to teach children? ” said Clym. [/]
[Narrator] Sam shook his head. [/]
[Sam] “ Quite a different sort of body from that, I reckon. ” [/]
[Clym] “ O, it was merely something which occurred to me. [/]
[Narrator] It would of course be necessary to see her and talk it over — not an easy thing, by the way, for my family and hers are not very friendly. ” “ I ’ll tell you how you mid see her, Mr. Yeobright, ” said Sam. [/]
[Sam] “ We are going to grapple for the bucket at six o’clock tonight at her house, and you could lend a hand. [/]
[Sam] There ’s five or six coming, but the well is deep, and another might be useful, if you do n’t mind appearing in that shape. [/]
[Narrator] She ’s sure to be walking round. ” “ I ’ll think of it, ” said Yeobright; and they parted. [/]
[Narrator] He thought of it a good deal; but nothing more was said about Eustacia inside the house at that time. [/]
[Narrator] Whether this romantic martyr to superstition and the melancholy mummer he had conversed with under the full moon were one and the same person remained as yet a problem. [/]
[Narrator] III. [/]
[Narrator] The First Act in a Timeworn Drama The afternoon was fine, and Yeobright walked on the heath for an hour with his mother. [/]
[Narrator] When they reached the lofty ridge which divided the valley of Blooms - End from the adjoining valley they stood still and looked round. [/]
[Narrator] The Quiet Woman Inn was visible on the low margin of the heath in one direction, and afar on the other hand rose Mistover Knap. [/]
[He] “ You mean to call on Thomasin? ” he inquired. [/]
[HisMother] “ Yes. [/]
[HisMother] But you need not come this time, ” said his mother. [/]
[HisMother] “ In that case I ’ll branch off here, Mother. [/]
[HisMother] I am going to Mistover. ” [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright turned to him inquiringly. [/]
[He] “ I am going to help them get the bucket out of the captain ’s well, ” he continued. [/]
[He] “ As it is so very deep I may be useful. [/]
[He] And I should like to see this Miss Vye — not so much for her good looks as for another reason. ” [/]
[HisMother] “ Must you go? ” [/]
[Narrator] his mother asked. [/]
[He] “ I thought to. ” [/]
[Narrator] And they parted. [/]
[He] “ There is no help for it, ” murmured Clym ’s mother gloomily as he withdrew. [/]
[He] “ They are sure to see each other. [/]
[He] I wish Sam would carry his news to other houses than mine. ” Clym ’s retreating figure got smaller and smaller as it rose and fell over the hillocks on his way. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ He is tender - hearted, ” said Mrs. Yeobright to herself while she watched him; “ otherwise it would matter little. [/]
[MrsYeobright] How he ’s going on! ” [/]
[Narrator] He was, indeed, walking with a will over the furze, as straight as a line, as if his life depended upon it. [/]
[Narrator] His mother drew a long breath, and, abandoning the visit to Thomasin, turned back. [/]
[Narrator] The evening films began to make nebulous pictures of the valleys, but the high lands still were raked by the declining rays of the winter sun, which glanced on Clym as he walked forward, eyed by every rabbit and field - fare around, a long shadow advancing in front of him. [/]
[Narrator] On drawing near to the furze - covered bank and ditch which fortified the captain ’s dwelling he could hear voices within, signifying that operations had been already begun. [/]
[Narrator] At the side - entrance gate he stopped and looked over. [/]
[Narrator] Half a dozen able - bodied men were standing in a line from the well - mouth, holding a rope which passed over the well - roller into the depths below. [/]
[Narrator] Fairway, with a piece of smaller rope round his body, made fast to one of the standards, to guard against accidents, was leaning over the opening, his right hand clasping the vertical rope that descended into the well. [/]
[Fairway] “ Now, silence, folks, ” said Fairway. [/]
[Narrator] The talking ceased, and Fairway gave a circular motion to the rope, as if he were stirring batter. [/]
[Narrator] At the end of a minute a dull splashing reverberated from the bottom of the well; the helical twist he had imparted to the rope had reached the grapnel below. [/]
[Fairway] “ Haul! ” said Fairway; and the men who held the rope began to gather it over the wheel. [/]
[OneOfTheHaulersIn] “ I think we ’ve got sommat, ” said one of the haulers - in. [/]
[Fairway] “ Then pull steady, ” said Fairway. [/]
[Narrator] They gathered up more and more, till a regular dripping into the well could be heard below. [/]
[Narrator] It grew smarter with the increasing height of the bucket, and presently a hundred and fifty feet of rope had been pulled in. [/]
[Narrator] Fairway then lit a lantern, tied it to another cord, and began lowering it into the well beside the first: Clym came forward and looked down. [/]
[Narrator] Strange humid leaves, which knew nothing of the seasons of the year, and quaint - natured mosses were revealed on the wellside as the lantern descended; till its rays fell upon a confused mass of rope and bucket dangling in the dank, dark air. [/]
[Fairway] “ We ’ve only got en by the edge of the hoop — steady, for God ’s sake! ” said Fairway. [/]
[Narrator] They pulled with the greatest gentleness, till the wet bucket appeared about two yards below them, like a dead friend come to earth again. [/]
[Narrator] Three or four hands were stretched out, then jerk went the rope, whizz went the wheel, the two foremost haulers fell backward, the beating of a falling body was heard, receding down the sides of the well, and a thunderous uproar arose at the bottom. [/]
[Narrator] The bucket was gone again. [/]
[Fairway] “ Damn the bucket! ” said Fairway. [/]
[Sam] “ Lower again, ” said Sam. [/]
[Fairway] “ I ’m as stiff as a ram ’s horn stooping so long, ” said Fairway, standing up and stretching himself till his joints creaked. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ Rest a few minutes, Timothy, ” said Yeobright. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ I ’ll take your place. ” [/]
[Narrator] The grapnel was again lowered. [/]
[Narrator] Its smart impact upon the distant water reached their ears like a kiss, whereupon Yeobright knelt down, and leaning over the well began dragging the grapnel round and round as Fairway had done. [/]
[TheSpeaker] “ Tie a rope round him — it is dangerous! ” [/]
[Narrator] cried a soft and anxious voice somewhere above them. [/]
[Narrator] Everybody turned. [/]
[Narrator] The speaker was a woman, gazing down upon the group from an upper window, whose panes blazed in the ruddy glare from the west. [/]
[Narrator] Her lips were parted and she appeared for the moment to forget where she was. [/]
[Narrator] The rope was accordingly tied round his waist, and the work proceeded. [/]
[Narrator] At the next haul the weight was not heavy, and it was discovered that they had only secured a coil of the rope detached from the bucket. [/]
[Narrator] The tangled mass was thrown into the background. [/]
[Narrator] Humphrey took Yeobright ’s place, and the grapnel was lowered again. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright retired to the heap of recovered rope in a meditative mood. [/]
[Narrator] Of the identity between the lady ’s voice and that of the melancholy mummer he had not a moment ’s doubt. [/]
[He] “ How thoughtful of her! ” [/]
[Narrator] he said to himself. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia, who had reddened when she perceived the effect of her exclamation upon the group below, was no longer to be seen at the window, though Yeobright scanned it wistfully. [/]
[Narrator] While he stood there the men at the well succeeded in getting up the bucket without a mishap. [/]
[Narrator] One of them went to inquire for the captain, to learn what orders he wished to give for mending the well - tackle. [/]
[Narrator] The captain proved to be away from home, and Eustacia appeared at the door and came out. [/]
[Narrator] She had lapsed into an easy and dignified calm, far removed from the intensity of life in her words of solicitude for Clym ’s safety. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Will it be possible to draw water here tonight? ” [/]
[Narrator] she inquired. [/]
[Eustacia] “ No, miss; the bottom of the bucket is clean knocked out. [/]
[Eustacia] And as we can do no more now we ’ll leave off, and come again tomorrow morning. ” [/]
[Eustacia] “ No water, ” she murmured, turning away. [/]
[Clym] “ I can send you up some from Blooms - End, ” said Clym, coming forward and raising his hat as the men retired. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright and Eustacia looked at each other for one instant, as if each had in mind those few moments during which a certain moonlight scene was common to both. [/]
[Narrator] With the glance the calm fixity of her features sublimed itself to an expression of refinement and warmth; it was like garish noon rising to the dignity of sunset in a couple of seconds. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Thank you; it will hardly be necessary, ” she replied. [/]
[Eustacia] “ But if you have no water? ” “ Well, it is what I call no water, ” she said, blushing, and lifting her long - lashed eyelids as if to lift them were a work requiring consideration. [/]
[Eustacia] “ But my grandfather calls it water enough. [/]
[Eustacia] I ’ll show you what I mean. ” [/]
[Narrator] She moved away a few yards, and Clym followed. [/]
[Narrator] When she reached the corner of the enclosure, where the steps were formed for mounting the boundary bank, she sprang up with a lightness which seemed strange after her listless movement towards the well. [/]
[Narrator] It incidentally showed that her apparent languor did not arise from lack of force. [/]
[Narrator] Clym ascended behind her, and noticed a circular burnt patch at the top of the bank. [/]
[Clym] “ Ashes? ” he said. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Yes, ” said Eustacia. [/]
[Eustacia] “ We had a little bonfire here last Fifth of November, and those are the marks of it. ” [/]
[Narrator] On that spot had stood the fire she had kindled to attract Wildeve. [/]

[Narrator] An Hour of Bliss and Many Hours of Sadness The next day was gloomy enough at Blooms - End. [/]

[Narrator] He had often come up here without stating his purpose to his mother; but this was the first time that he had been ostensibly frank as to his purpose while really concealing it. [/]
[Narrator] It was a moral situation which, three months earlier, he could hardly have credited of himself. [/]
[Narrator] In returning to labour in this sequestered spot he had anticipated an escape from the chafing of social necessities; yet behold they were here also. [/]
[Narrator] More than ever he longed to be in some world where personal ambition was not the only recognized form of progress — such, perhaps, as might have been the case at some time or other in the silvery globe then shining upon him. [/]
[Narrator] His eye travelled over the length and breadth of that distant country — over the Bay of Rainbows, the sombre Sea of Crises, the Ocean of Storms, the Lake of Dreams, the vast Walled Plains, and the wondrous Ring Mountains — till he almost felt himself to be voyaging bodily through its wild scenes, standing on its hollow hills, traversing its deserts, descending its vales and old sea bottoms, or mounting to the edges of its craters. [/]
[Narrator] While he watched the far - removed landscape a tawny stain grew into being on the lower verge — the eclipse had begun. [/]

[Narrator] And as he walked further and further from the charmed atmosphere of his Olympian girl his face grew sad with a new sort of sadness. [/]
[Narrator] A perception of the dilemma in which his love had placed him came back in full force. [/]
[Narrator] In spite of Eustacia ’s apparent willingness to wait through the period of an unpromising engagement, till he should be established in his new pursuit, he could not but perceive at moments that she loved him rather as a visitant from a gay world to which she rightly belonged than as a man with a purpose opposed to that recent past of his which so interested her. [/]
[Narrator] It meant that, though she made no conditions as to his return to the French capital, this was what she secretly longed for in the event of marriage; and it robbed him of many an otherwise pleasant hour. [/]
[Narrator] Along with that came the widening breach between himself and his mother. [/]
[Narrator] Whenever any little occurrence had brought into more prominence than usual the disappointment that he was causing her it had sent him on lone and moody walks; or he was kept awake a great part of the night by the turmoil of spirit which such a recognition created. [/]
[Narrator] If Mrs. Yeobright could only have been led to see what a sound and worthy purpose this purpose of his was and how little it was being affected by his devotions to Eustacia, how differently would she regard him! [/]
[Narrator] Thus as his sight grew accustomed to the first blinding halo kindled about him by love and beauty, Yeobright began to perceive what a strait he was in. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes he wished that he had never known Eustacia, immediately to retract the wish as brutal. [/]
[Narrator] Three antagonistic growths had to be kept alive: his mother ’s trust in him, his plan for becoming a teacher, and Eustacia ’s happiness. [/]
[Narrator] His fervid nature could not afford to relinquish one of these, though two of the three were as many as he could hope to preserve. [/]
[Narrator] Though his love was as chaste as that of Petrarch for his Laura, it had made fetters of what previously was only a difficulty. [/]
[Narrator] A position which was not too simple when he stood whole - hearted had become indescribably complicated by the addition of Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] Just when his mother was beginning to tolerate one scheme he had introduced another still bitterer than the first, and the combination was more than she could bear. [/]
[Narrator] V. Sharp Words Are Spoken, and a Crisis Ensues When Yeobright was not with Eustacia he was sitting slavishly over his books; when he was not reading he was meeting her. [/]
[Narrator] These meetings were carried on with the greatest secrecy. [/]
[Narrator] One afternoon his mother came home from a morning visit to Thomasin. [/]
[Narrator] He could see from a disturbance in the lines of her face that something had happened. [/]
[HisMother] “ I have been told an incomprehensible thing, ” she said mournfully. [/]
[Narrator] “ The captain has let out at the Woman that you and Eustacia Vye are engaged to be married. ” “ We are, ” said Yeobright. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ But it may not be yet for a very long time. ” [/]
[HisMother] “ I should hardly think it _ would _ be yet for a very long time! [/]

[Narrator] It was a sunny afternoon at the beginning of summer, and the moist hollows of the heath had passed from their brown to their green stage. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright walked to the edge of the basin which extended down from Mistover and Rainbarrow. [/]
[Narrator] By this time he was calm, and he looked over the landscape. [/]
[Narrator] In the minor valleys, between the hillocks which diversified the contour of the vale, the fresh young ferns were luxuriantly growing up, ultimately to reach a height of five or six feet. [/]
[Narrator] He descended a little way, flung himself down in a spot where a path emerged from one of the small hollows, and waited. [/]
[Narrator] Hither it was that he had promised Eustacia to bring his mother this afternoon, that they might meet and be friends. [/]
[Narrator] His attempt had utterly failed. [/]
[Narrator] He was in a nest of vivid green. [/]
[Narrator] The ferny vegetation round him, though so abundant, was quite uniform — it was a grove of machine - made foliage, a world of green triangles with saw - edges, and not a single flower. [/]
[Narrator] The air was warm with a vaporous warmth, and the stillness was unbroken. [/]
[Narrator] Lizards, grasshoppers, and ants were the only living things to be beheld. [/]
[Narrator] The scene seemed to belong to the ancient world of the carboniferous period, when the forms of plants were few, and of the fern kind; when there was neither bud nor blossom, nothing but a monotonous extent of leafage, amid which no bird sang. [/]
[Narrator] When he had reclined for some considerable time, gloomily pondering, he discerned above the ferns a drawn bonnet of white silk approaching from the left, and Yeobright knew directly that it covered the head of her he loved. [/]
[Clym] His heart awoke from its apathy to a warm excitement, and, jumping to his feet, he said aloud, “ I knew she was sure to come. ” [/]
[Narrator] She vanished in a hollow for a few moments, and then her whole form unfolded itself from the brake. [/]
[HisMother] “ Only you here? ” [/]

[HisMother] Pleasure not known beforehand is half wasted; to anticipate it is to double it. [/]
[HisMother] I have not thought once today of having you all to myself this afternoon, and the actual moment of a thing is so soon gone. ” [/]
[HisMother] “ It is indeed. ” “ Poor Clym! ” [/]

[HisMother] Something has happened at your home. [/]

[HisMother] I love you none the less in telling you; it is only right you should know. ” [/]
[I] “ I have feared my bliss, ” she said, with the merest motion of her lips. [/]
[I] “ It has been too intense and consuming. ” [/]
[HisMother] “ There is hope yet. [/]
[HisMother] There are forty years of work in me yet, and why should you despair? [/]
[HisMother] I am only at an awkward turning. [/]
[HisMother] I wish people would n’t be so ready to think that there is no progress without uniformity. ” [/]
[I] “ Ah — your mind runs off to the philosophical side of it. [/]
[I] Well, these sad and hopeless obstacles are welcome in one sense, for they enable us to look with indifference upon the cruel satires that Fate loves to indulge in. [/]
[I] I have heard of people, who, upon coming suddenly into happiness, have died from anxiety lest they should not live to enjoy it. [/]
[I] I felt myself in that whimsical state of uneasiness lately; but I shall be spared it now. [/]
[I] Let us walk on. ” [/]
[Narrator] Clym took the hand which was already bared for him — it was a favourite way with them to walk bare hand in bare hand — and led her through the ferns. [/]
[Narrator] They formed a very comely picture of love at full flush, as they walked along the valley that late afternoon, the sun sloping down on their right, and throwing their thin spectral shadows, tall as poplar trees, far out across the furze and fern. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia went with her head thrown back fancifully, a certain glad and voluptuous air of triumph pervading her eyes at having won by her own unaided self a man who was her perfect complement in attainment, appearance, and age. [/]
[Narrator] On the young man ’s part, the paleness of face which he had brought with him from Paris, and the incipient marks of time and thought, were less perceptible than when he returned, the healthful and energetic sturdiness which was his by nature having partially recovered its original proportions. [/]
[Narrator] They wandered onward till they reached the nether margin of the heath, where it became marshy and merged in moorland. [/]
[Eustacia] “ I must part from you here, Clym, ” said Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] They stood still and prepared to bid each other farewell. [/]
[Narrator] Everything before them was on a perfect level. [/]
[Narrator] The sun, resting on the horizon line, streamed across the ground from between copper - coloured and lilac clouds, stretched out in flats beneath a sky of pale soft green. [/]
[Narrator] All dark objects on the earth that lay towards the sun were overspread by a purple haze, against which groups of wailing gnats shone out, rising upwards and dancing about like sparks of fire. [/]
[Eustacia] “ O! [/]
[Eustacia] this leaving you is too hard to bear! ” [/]

[Narrator] “ Your mother will influence you too much; I shall not be judged fairly, it will get afloat that I am not a good girl, and the witch story will be added to make me blacker! ” “ They can not. [/]
[Clym] Nobody dares to speak disrespectfully of you or of me. ” [/]

[Clym] At the end of that time I shall have finished my reading — yes, we will do it, and this heart - aching will be over. [/]
[Clym] We shall, of course, live in absolute seclusion, and our married life will only begin to outward view when we take the house in Budmouth, where I have already addressed a letter on the matter. [/]
[Clym] Would your grandfather allow you? ” “ I think he would — on the understanding that it should not last longer than six months. ” “ I will guarantee that, if no misfortune happens. ” [/]
[Her] “ If no misfortune happens, ” she repeated slowly. [/]
[Clym] “ Which is not likely. [/]
[Clym] Dearest, fix the exact day. ” [/]
[Narrator] And then they consulted on the question, and the day was chosen. [/]
[Narrator] It was to be a fortnight from that time. [/]
[Narrator] This was the end of their talk, and Eustacia left him. [/]
[Narrator] Clym watched her as she retired towards the sun. [/]
[Narrator] The luminous rays wrapped her up with her increasing distance, and the rustle of her dress over the sprouting sedge and grass died away. [/]
[Narrator] As he watched, the dead flat of the scenery overpowered him, though he was fully alive to the beauty of that untarnished early summer green which was worn for the nonce by the poorest blade. [/]
[Narrator] There was something in its oppressive horizontality which too much reminded him of the arena of life; it gave him a sense of bare equality with, and no superiority to, a single living thing under the sun. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia was now no longer the goddess but the woman to him, a being to fight for, support, help, be maligned for. [/]
[Narrator] Now that he had reached a cooler moment he would have preferred a less hasty marriage; but the card was laid, and he determined to abide by the game. [/]
[Narrator] Whether Eustacia was to add one other to the list of those who love too hotly to love long and well, the forthcoming event was certainly a ready way of proving. VI. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright Goes, and the Breach Is Complete All that evening smart sounds denoting an active packing up came from Yeobright ’s room to the ears of his mother downstairs. [/]
[Narrator] Next morning he departed from the house and again proceeded across the heath. [/]
[Narrator] A long day ’s march was before him, his object being to secure a dwelling to which he might take Eustacia when she became his wife. [/]
[Narrator] Such a house, small, secluded, and with its windows boarded up, he had casually observed a month earlier, about two miles beyond the village of East Egdon, and six miles distant altogether; and thither he directed his steps today. [/]
[Narrator] The weather was far different from that of the evening before. [/]
[Narrator] The yellow and vapoury sunset which had wrapped up Eustacia from his parting gaze had presaged change. [/]
[Narrator] It was one of those not infrequent days of an English June which are as wet and boisterous as November. [/]
[Narrator] The cold clouds hastened on in a body, as if painted on a moving slide. [/]
[Narrator] Vapours from other continents arrived upon the wind, which curled and parted round him as he walked on. [/]
[Narrator] At length Clym reached the margin of a fir and beech plantation that had been enclosed from heath - land in the year of his birth. [/]
[Narrator] Here the trees, laden heavily with their new and humid leaves, were now suffering more damage than during the highest winds of winter, when the boughs are especially disencumbered to do battle with the storm. [/]
[Narrator] The wet young beeches were undergoing amputations, bruises, cripplings, and harsh lacerations, from which the wasting sap would bleed for many a day to come, and which would leave scars visible till the day of their burning. [/]
[Narrator] Each stem was wrenched at the root, where it moved like a bone in its socket, and at every onset of the gale convulsive sounds came from the branches, as if pain were felt. [/]
[Narrator] In a neighbouring brake a finch was trying to sing; but the wind blew under his feathers till they stood on end, twisted round his little tail, and made him give up his song. [/]
[Narrator] Yet a few yards to Yeobright ’s left, on the open heath, how ineffectively gnashed the storm! [/]
[Narrator] Those gusts which tore the trees merely waved the furze and heather in a light caress. [/]
[Narrator] Egdon was made for such times as these. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright reached the empty house about midday. [/]
[Narrator] It was almost as lonely as that of Eustacia ’s grandfather, but the fact that it stood near a heath was disguised by a belt of firs which almost enclosed the premises. [/]
[Narrator] He journeyed on about a mile further to the village in which the owner lived, and, returning with him to the house, arrangements were completed, and the man undertook that one room at least should be ready for occupation the next day. [/]
[Narrator] Clym ’s intention was to live there alone until Eustacia should join him on their wedding - day. [/]
[Narrator] Then he turned to pursue his way homeward through the drizzle that had so greatly transformed the scene. [/]
[Narrator] The ferns, among which he had lain in comfort yesterday, were dripping moisture from every frond, wetting his legs through as he brushed past; and the fur of the rabbits leaping before him was clotted into dark locks by the same watery surrounding. [/]
[Narrator] He reached home damp and weary enough after his ten - mile walk. [/]
[Narrator] It had hardly been a propitious beginning, but he had chosen his course, and would show no swerving. [/]
[Narrator] The evening and the following morning were spent in concluding arrangements for his departure. [/]
[Narrator] To stay at home a minute longer than necessary after having once come to his determination would be, he felt, only to give new pain to his mother by some word, look, or deed. [/]
[Narrator] He had hired a conveyance and sent off his goods by two o’clock that day. [/]
[Narrator] The next step was to get some furniture, which, after serving for temporary use in the cottage, would be available for the house at Budmouth when increased by goods of a better description. [/]
[Narrator] A mart extensive enough for the purpose existed at Anglebury, some miles beyond the spot chosen for his residence, and there he resolved to pass the coming night. [/]
[Narrator] It now only remained to wish his mother good - bye. [/]
[Narrator] She was sitting by the window as usual when he came downstairs. [/]
[Clym] “ Mother, I am going to leave you, ” he said, holding out his hand. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ I thought you were, by your packing, ” replied Mrs. Yeobright in a voice from which every particle of emotion was painfully excluded. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ And you will part friends with me? ” “ Certainly, Clym. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ I am going to be married on the twenty - fifth. ” “ I thought you were going to be married. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ And then — and then you must come and see us. [/]
[MrsYeobright] You will understand me better after that, and our situation will not be so wretched as it is now. ” [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ I do not think it likely I shall come to see you. ” “ Then it will not be my fault or Eustacia ’s, Mother. [/]
[MrsYeobright] Good - bye! ” [/]
[Narrator] He kissed her cheek, and departed in great misery, which was several hours in lessening itself to a controllable level. [/]
[Narrator] The position had been such that nothing more could be said without, in the first place, breaking down a barrier; and that was not to be done. [/]
[Narrator] No sooner had Yeobright gone from his mother ’s house than her face changed its rigid aspect for one of blank despair. [/]
[Narrator] After a while she wept, and her tears brought some relief. [/]
[Narrator] During the rest of the day she did nothing but walk up and down the garden path in a state bordering on stupefaction. [/]
[Narrator] Night came, and with it but little rest. [/]
[Narrator] The next day, with an instinct to do something which should reduce prostration to mournfulness, she went to her son ’s room, and with her own hands arranged it in order, for an imaginary time when he should return again. [/]

[Narrator] There was as much variety in her motions as in their flight. [/]
[Narrator] When she was musing she was a kestrel, which hangs in the air by an invisible motion of its wings. [/]
[Narrator] When she was in a high wind her light body was blown against trees and banks like a heron ’s. [/]
[Narrator] When she was frightened she darted noiselessly like a kingfisher. [/]
[Narrator] When she was serene she skimmed like a swallow, and that is how she was moving now. [/]

[Narrator] This is the true mark of the man of sentiment. [/]
[Narrator] Though Wildeve ’s fevered feeling had not been elaborated to real poetical compass, it was of the standard sort. [/]

[Narrator] The Morning and the Evening of a Day The wedding morning came. [/]
[Narrator] Nobody would have imagined from appearances that Blooms - End had any interest in Mistover that day. [/]
[Narrator] A solemn stillness prevailed around the house of Clym ’s mother, and there was no more animation indoors. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright, who had declined to attend the ceremony, sat by the breakfast table in the old room which communicated immediately with the porch, her eyes listlessly directed towards the open door. [/]
[Narrator] It was the room in which, six months earlier, the merry Christmas party had met, to which Eustacia came secretly and as a stranger. [/]
[Narrator] The only living thing that entered now was a sparrow; and seeing no movements to cause alarm, he hopped boldly round the room, endeavoured to go out by the window, and fluttered among the pot - flowers. [/]
[Narrator] This roused the lonely sitter, who got up, released the bird, and went to the door. [/]
[Narrator] She was expecting Thomasin, who had written the night before to state that the time had come when she would wish to have the money and that she would if possible call this day. [/]
[Narrator] Yet Thomasin occupied Mrs. Yeobright ’s thoughts but slightly as she looked up the valley of the heath, alive with butterflies, and with grasshoppers whose husky noises on every side formed a whispered chorus. [/]
[Narrator] A domestic drama, for which the preparations were now being made a mile or two off, was but little less vividly present to her eyes than if enacted before her. [/]
[Narrator] She tried to dismiss the vision, and walked about the garden plot; but her eyes ever and anon sought out the direction of the parish church to which Mistover belonged, and her excited fancy clove the hills which divided the building from her eyes. [/]
[Narrator] The morning wore away. [/]
[Narrator] Eleven o’clock struck — could it be that the wedding was then in progress? [/]
[Narrator] It must be so. [/]

[MrsYeobright] Come back here to supper, if it is not too late. ” [/]
[Narrator] It was nearly nine o’clock when he began to ascend the vale towards Mistover; but the long days of summer being at their climax, the first obscurity of evening had only just begun to tan the landscape. [/]
[Narrator] At this point of his journey Christian heard voices, and found that they proceeded from a company of men and women who were traversing a hollow ahead of him, the tops only of their heads being visible. [/]
[Narrator] He paused and thought of the money he carried. [/]
[Narrator] It was almost too early even for Christian seriously to fear robbery; nevertheless he took a precaution which ever since his boyhood he had adopted whenever he carried more than two or three shillings upon his person — a precaution somewhat like that of the owner of the Pitt Diamond when filled with similar misgivings. [/]
[Narrator] He took off his boots, untied the guineas, and emptied the contents of one little bag into the right boot, and of the other into the left, spreading them as flatly as possible over the bottom of each, which was really a spacious coffer by no means limited to the size of the foot. [/]
[Narrator] Pulling them on again and lacing them to the very top, he proceeded on his way, more easy in his head than under his soles. [/]
[Narrator] His path converged towards that of the noisy company, and on coming nearer he found to his relief that they were several Egdon people whom he knew very well, while with them walked Fairway, of Blooms - End. [/]
[Fairway] “ What! [/]

[Narrator] When they entered the large common room of the inn they found assembled there about ten men from among the neighbouring population, and the group was increased by the new contingent to double that number. [/]
[Narrator] Most of them were sitting round the room in seats divided by wooden elbows like those of crude cathedral stalls, which were carved with the initials of many an illustrious drunkard of former times who had passed his days and his nights between them, and now lay as an alcoholic cinder in the nearest churchyard. [/]
[Narrator] Among the cups on the long table before the sitters lay an open parcel of light drapery — the gown - piece, as it was called — which was to be raffled for. [/]

[Narrator] A New Force Disturbs the Current Wildeve stared. [/]

[Narrator] Play was again resumed. [/]

[Wildeve] We can continue the game. ” [/]

[Narrator] It happened to be that season of the year at which glowworms put forth their greatest brilliancy, and the light they yielded was more than ample for the purpose, since it is possible on such nights to read the handwriting of a letter by the light of two or three. [/]

[Narrator] Wildeve forgot the loss of the money at the sight of his lost love, whose preciousness in his eyes was increasing in geometrical progression with each new incident that reminded him of their hopeless division. [/]

[Narrator] Thus Venn, in his anxiety to rectify matters, had placed in Thomasin ’s hands not only the fifty guineas which rightly belonged to her, but also the fifty intended for her cousin Clym. [/]
[Narrator] His mistake had been based upon Wildeve ’s words at the opening of the game, when he indignantly denied that the guinea was not his own. [/]
[Narrator] It had not been comprehended by the reddleman that at halfway through the performance the game was continued with the money of another person; and it was an error which afterwards helped to cause more misfortune than treble the loss in money value could have done. [/]
[Narrator] The night was now somewhat advanced; and Venn plunged deeper into the heath, till he came to a ravine where his van was standing — a spot not more than two hundred yards from the site of the gambling bout. [/]
[Narrator] He entered this movable home of his, lit his lantern, and, before closing his door for the night, stood reflecting on the circumstances of the preceding hours. [/]
[Narrator] While he stood the dawn grew visible in the northeast quarter of the heavens, which, the clouds having cleared off, was bright with a soft sheen at this midsummer time, though it was only between one and two o’clock. [/]
[Narrator] Venn, thoroughly weary, then shut his door and flung himself down to sleep. [/]
[Narrator] BOOK FOURTH — THE CLOSED DOOR I. The Rencounter by the Pool The July sun shone over Egdon and fired its crimson heather to scarlet. [/]
[Narrator] It was the one season of the year, and the one weather of the season, in which the heath was gorgeous. [/]
[Narrator] This flowering period represented the second or noontide division in the cycle of those superficial changes which alone were possible here; it followed the green or young - fern period, representing the morn, and preceded the brown period, when the heathbells and ferns would wear the russet tinges of evening; to be in turn displaced by the dark hue of the winter period, representing night. [/]
[Narrator] Clym and Eustacia, in their little house at Alderworth, beyond East Egdon, were living on with a monotony which was delightful to them. [/]
[Narrator] The heath and changes of weather were quite blotted out from their eyes for the present. [/]
[Narrator] They were enclosed in a sort of luminous mist, which hid from them surroundings of any inharmonious colour, and gave to all things the character of light. [/]
[Narrator] When it rained they were charmed, because they could remain indoors together all day with such a show of reason; when it was fine they were charmed, because they could sit together on the hills. [/]
[Narrator] They were like those double stars which revolve round and round each other, and from a distance appear to be one. [/]
[Narrator] The absolute solitude in which they lived intensified their reciprocal thoughts; yet some might have said that it had the disadvantage of consuming their mutual affections at a fearfully prodigal rate. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright did not fear for his own part; but recollection of Eustacia ’s old speech about the evanescence of love, now apparently forgotten by her, sometimes caused him to ask himself a question; and he recoiled at the thought that the quality of finiteness was not foreign to Eden. [/]
[Narrator] When three or four weeks had been passed thus, Yeobright resumed his reading in earnest. [/]
[Narrator] To make up for lost time he studied indefatigably, for he wished to enter his new profession with the least possible delay. [/]
[Narrator] Now, Eustacia ’s dream had always been that, once married to Clym, she would have the power of inducing him to return to Paris. [/]
[Narrator] He had carefully withheld all promise to do so; but would he be proof against her coaxing and argument? [/]
[Narrator] She had calculated to such a degree on the probability of success that she had represented Paris, and not Budmouth, to her grandfather as in all likelihood their future home. [/]
[Narrator] Her hopes were bound up in this dream. [/]
[Narrator] In the quiet days since their marriage, when Yeobright had been poring over her lips, her eyes, and the lines of her face, she had mused and mused on the subject, even while in the act of returning his gaze; and now the sight of the books, indicating a future which was antagonistic to her dream, struck her with a positively painful jar. [/]
[Narrator] She was hoping for the time when, as the mistress of some pretty establishment, however small, near a Parisian Boulevard, she would be passing her days on the skirts at least of the gay world, and catching stray wafts from those town pleasures she was so well fitted to enjoy. [/]
[Narrator] Yet Yeobright was as firm in the contrary intention as if the tendency of marriage were rather to develop the fantasies of young philanthropy than to sweep them away. [/]
[Narrator] Her anxiety reached a high pitch; but there was something in Clym ’s undeviating manner which made her hesitate before sounding him on the subject. [/]
[Narrator] At this point in their experience, however, an incident helped her. [/]
[Narrator] It occurred one evening about six weeks after their union, and arose entirely out of the unconscious misapplication of Venn of the fifty guineas intended for Yeobright. [/]
[Narrator] A day or two after the receipt of the money Thomasin had sent a note to her aunt to thank her. [/]
[Narrator] She had been surprised at the largeness of the amount; but as no sum had ever been mentioned she set that down to her late uncle ’s generosity. [/]
[Narrator] She had been strictly charged by her aunt to say nothing to her husband of this gift; and Wildeve, as was natural enough, had not brought himself to mention to his wife a single particular of the midnight scene in the heath. [/]
[Narrator] Christian ’s terror, in like manner, had tied his tongue on the share he took in that proceeding; and hoping that by some means or other the money had gone to its proper destination, he simply asserted as much, without giving details. [/]
[Narrator] Therefore, when a week or two had passed away, Mrs. Yeobright began to wonder why she never heard from her son of the receipt of the present; and to add gloom to her perplexity came the possibility that resentment might be the cause of his silence. [/]
[Narrator] She could hardly believe as much, but why did he not write? [/]
[Narrator] She questioned Christian, and the confusion in his answers would at once have led her to believe that something was wrong, had not one - half of his story been corroborated by Thomasin ’s note. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright was in this state of uncertainty when she was informed one morning that her son ’s wife was visiting her grandfather at Mistover. [/]
[Narrator] She determined to walk up the hill, see Eustacia, and ascertain from her daughter - in - law ’s lips whether the family guineas, which were to Mrs. Yeobright what family jewels are to wealthier dowagers, had miscarried or not. [/]
[Narrator] When Christian learnt where she was going his concern reached its height. [/]
[Narrator] At the moment of her departure he could prevaricate no longer, and, confessing to the gambling, told her the truth as far as he knew it — that the guineas had been won by Wildeve. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ What, is he going to keep them? ” [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright cried. [/]
[Christian] “ I hope and trust not! ” moaned Christian. [/]
[Christian] “ He ’s a good man, and perhaps will do right things. [/]
[Christian] He said you ought to have gied Mr. Clym ’s share to Eustacia, and that ’s perhaps what he ’ll do himself. ” [/]
[Narrator] To Mrs. Yeobright, as soon as she could calmly reflect, there was much likelihood in this, for she could hardly believe that Wildeve would really appropriate money belonging to her son. [/]
[Narrator] The intermediate course of giving it to Eustacia was the sort of thing to please Wildeve ’s fancy. [/]
[Narrator] But it filled the mother with anger none the less. [/]

[/]

[Her] I ’ll be bitterly merry, and ironically gay, and I ’ll laugh in derision. [/]
[Her] And I ’ll begin by going to this dance on the green. ” [/]
[Narrator] She ascended to her bedroom and dressed herself with scrupulous care. [/]
[Narrator] To an onlooker her beauty would have made her feelings almost seem reasonable. [/]
[Narrator] The gloomy corner into which accident as much as indiscretion had brought this woman might have led even a moderate partisan to feel that she had cogent reasons for asking the Supreme Power by what right a being of such exquisite finish had been placed in circumstances calculated to make of her charms a curse rather than a blessing. [/]
[Narrator] It was five in the afternoon when she came out from the house ready for her walk. [/]
[Narrator] There was material enough in the picture for twenty new conquests. [/]
[Narrator] The rebellious sadness that was rather too apparent when she sat indoors without a bonnet was cloaked and softened by her outdoor attire, which always had a sort of nebulousness about it, devoid of harsh edges anywhere; so that her face looked from its environment as from a cloud, with no noticeable lines of demarcation between flesh and clothes. [/]
[Narrator] The heat of the day had scarcely declined as yet, and she went along the sunny hills at a leisurely pace, there being ample time for her idle expedition. [/]
[Narrator] Tall ferns buried her in their leafage whenever her path lay through them, which now formed miniature forests, though not one stem of them would remain to bud the next year. [/]
[Narrator] The site chosen for the village festivity was one of the lawnlike oases which were occasionally, yet not often, met with on the plateaux of the heath district. [/]
[Narrator] The brakes of furze and fern terminated abruptly round the margin, and the grass was unbroken. [/]
[Narrator] A green cattletrack skirted the spot, without, however, emerging from the screen of fern, and this path Eustacia followed, in order to reconnoitre the group before joining it. [/]
[Narrator] The lusty notes of the East Egdon band had directed her unerringly, and she now beheld the musicians themselves, sitting in a blue wagon with red wheels scrubbed as bright as new, and arched with sticks, to which boughs and flowers were tied. [/]
[Narrator] In front of this was the grand central dance of fifteen or twenty couples, flanked by minor dances of inferior individuals whose gyrations were not always in strict keeping with the tune. [/]
[Narrator] The young men wore blue and white rosettes, and with a flush on their faces footed it to the girls, who, with the excitement and the exercise, blushed deeper than the pink of their numerous ribbons. [/]
[Narrator] Fair ones with long curls, fair ones with short curls, fair ones with lovelocks, fair ones with braids, flew round and round; and a beholder might well have wondered how such a prepossessing set of young women of like size, age, and disposition, could have been collected together where there were only one or two villages to choose from. [/]
[Narrator] In the background was one happy man dancing by himself, with closed eyes, totally oblivious of all the rest. [/]
[Narrator] A fire was burning under a pollard thorn a few paces off, over which three kettles hung in a row. [/]
[Narrator] Hard by was a table where elderly dames prepared tea, but Eustacia looked among them in vain for the cattle - dealer ’s wife who had suggested that she should come, and had promised to obtain a courteous welcome for her. [/]
[Narrator] This unexpected absence of the only local resident whom Eustacia knew considerably damaged her scheme for an afternoon of reckless gaiety. [/]
[Narrator] Joining in became a matter of difficulty, notwithstanding that, were she to advance, cheerful dames would come forward with cups of tea and make much of her as a stranger of superior grace and knowledge to themselves. [/]
[Narrator] Having watched the company through the figures of two dances, she decided to walk a little further, to a cottage where she might get some refreshment, and then return homeward in the shady time of evening. [/]
[Narrator] This she did, and by the time that she retraced her steps towards the scene of the gipsying, which it was necessary to repass on her way to Alderworth, the sun was going down. [/]
[Narrator] The air was now so still that she could hear the band afar off, and it seemed to be playing with more spirit, if that were possible, than when she had come away. [/]
[Narrator] On reaching the hill the sun had quite disappeared; but this made little difference either to Eustacia or to the revellers, for a round yellow moon was rising before her, though its rays had not yet outmastered those from the west. [/]
[Narrator] The dance was going on just the same, but strangers had arrived and formed a ring around the figure, so that Eustacia could stand among these without a chance of being recognized. [/]
[Narrator] A whole village - full of sensuous emotion, scattered abroad all the year long, surged here in a focus for an hour. [/]
[Narrator] The forty hearts of those waving couples were beating as they had not done since, twelve months before, they had come together in similar jollity. [/]
[Narrator] For the time paganism was revived in their hearts, the pride of life was all in all, and they adored none other than themselves. [/]
[Narrator] How many of those impassioned but temporary embraces were destined to become perpetual was possibly the wonder of some of those who indulged in them, as well as of Eustacia who looked on. [/]
[Narrator] She began to envy those pirouetters, to hunger for the hope and happiness which the fascination of the dance seemed to engender within them. [/]
[Narrator] Desperately fond of dancing herself, one of Eustacia ’s expectations of Paris had been the opportunity it might afford her of indulgence in this favourite pastime. [/]
[Narrator] Unhappily, that expectation was now extinct within her for ever. [/]
[Narrator] Whilst she abstractedly watched them spinning and fluctuating in the increasing moonlight she suddenly heard her name whispered by a voice over her shoulder. [/]
[Narrator] Turning in surprise, she beheld at her elbow one whose presence instantly caused her to flush to the temples. [/]
[Narrator] It was Wildeve. [/]
[Narrator] Till this moment he had not met her eye since the morning of his marriage, when she had been loitering in the church, and had startled him by lifting her veil and coming forward to sign the register as witness. [/]
[Narrator] Yet why the sight of him should have instigated that sudden rush of blood she could not tell. [/]
[Narrator] Before she could speak he whispered, “ Do you like dancing as much as ever? ” “ I think I do, ” she replied in a low voice. [/]
[Wildeve] “ Will you dance with me? ” “ It would be a great change for me; but will it not seem strange? ” [/]
[Wildeve] “ What strangeness can there be in relations dancing together? ” “ Ah — yes, relations. [/]
[Wildeve] Perhaps none. ” “ Still, if you do n’t like to be seen, pull down your veil; though there is not much risk of being known by this light. [/]
[Wildeve] Lots of strangers are here. ” [/]
[Narrator] She did as he suggested; and the act was a tacit acknowledgment that she accepted his offer. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve gave her his arm and took her down on the outside of the ring to the bottom of the dance, which they entered. [/]
[Narrator] In two minutes more they were involved in the figure and began working their way upwards to the top. [/]
[Narrator] Till they had advanced halfway thither Eustacia wished more than once that she had not yielded to his request; from the middle to the top she felt that, since she had come out to seek pleasure, she was only doing a natural thing to obtain it. [/]
[Narrator] Fairly launched into the ceaseless glides and whirls which their new position as top couple opened up to them, Eustacia ’s pulses began to move too quickly for long rumination of any kind. [/]
[Narrator] Through the length of five - and - twenty couples they threaded their giddy way, and a new vitality entered her form. [/]
[Narrator] The pale ray of evening lent a fascination to the experience. [/]
[Narrator] There is a certain degree and tone of light which tends to disturb the equilibrium of the senses, and to promote dangerously the tenderer moods; added to movement, it drives the emotions to rankness, the reason becoming sleepy and unperceiving in inverse proportion; and this light fell now upon these two from the disc of the moon. [/]
[Narrator] All the dancing girls felt the symptoms, but Eustacia most of all. [/]
[Narrator] The grass under their feet became trodden away, and the hard, beaten surface of the sod, when viewed aslant towards the moonlight, shone like a polished table. [/]
[Narrator] The air became quite still, the flag above the wagon which held the musicians clung to the pole, and the players appeared only in outline against the sky; except when the circular mouths of the trombone, ophicleide, and French horn gleamed out like huge eyes from the shade of their figures. [/]
[Narrator] The pretty dresses of the maids lost their subtler day colours and showed more or less of a misty white. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia floated round and round on Wildeve ’s arm, her face rapt and statuesque; her soul had passed away from and forgotten her features, which were left empty and quiescent, as they always are when feeling goes beyond their register. [/]
[Narrator] How near she was to Wildeve! [/]
[Narrator] it was terrible to think of. [/]
[Narrator] She could feel his breathing, and he, of course, could feel hers. [/]
[Narrator] How badly she had treated him! [/]
[Narrator] yet, here they were treading one measure. [/]
[Narrator] The enchantment of the dance surprised her. [/]
[Narrator] A clear line of difference divided like a tangible fence her experience within this maze of motion from her experience without it. [/]
[Narrator] Her beginning to dance had been like a change of atmosphere; outside, she had been steeped in arctic frigidity by comparison with the tropical sensations here. [/]
[Narrator] She had entered the dance from the troubled hours of her late life as one might enter a brilliant chamber after a night walk in a wood. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve by himself would have been merely an agitation; Wildeve added to the dance, and the moonlight, and the secrecy, began to be a delight. [/]
[Narrator] Whether his personality supplied the greater part of this sweetly compounded feeling, or whether the dance and the scene weighed the more therein, was a nice point upon which Eustacia herself was entirely in a cloud. [/]
[People] People began to say “ Who are they? ” [/]
[Narrator] but no invidious inquiries were made. [/]
[Narrator] Had Eustacia mingled with the other girls in their ordinary daily walks the case would have been different: here she was not inconvenienced by excessive inspection, for all were wrought to their brightest grace by the occasion. [/]
[Narrator] Like the planet Mercury surrounded by the lustre of sunset, her permanent brilliancy passed without much notice in the temporary glory of the situation. [/]
[Narrator] As for Wildeve, his feelings are easy to guess. [/]
[Narrator] Obstacles were a ripening sun to his love, and he was at this moment in a delirium of exquisite misery. [/]
[Narrator] To clasp as his for five minutes what was another man ’s through all the rest of the year was a kind of thing he of all men could appreciate. [/]
[Narrator] He had long since begun to sigh again for Eustacia; indeed, it may be asserted that signing the marriage register with Thomasin was the natural signal to his heart to return to its first quarters, and that the extra complication of Eustacia ’s marriage was the one addition required to make that return compulsory. [/]
[Narrator] Thus, for different reasons, what was to the rest an exhilarating movement was to these two a riding upon the whirlwind. [/]
[Narrator] The dance had come like an irresistible attack upon whatever sense of social order there was in their minds, to drive them back into old paths which were now doubly irregular. [/]
[Narrator] Through three dances in succession they spun their way; and then, fatigued with the incessant motion, Eustacia turned to quit the circle in which she had already remained too long. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve led her to a grassy mound a few yards distant, where she sat down, her partner standing beside her. [/]

[Narrator] he said tenderly. [/]

[Narrator] The moon had now waxed bright and silvery, but the heath was proof against such illumination, and there was to be observed the striking scene of a dark, rayless tract of country under an atmosphere charged from its zenith to its extremities with whitest light. [/]

[Narrator] On this account the irregularities of the path were not visible, and Wildeve occasionally stumbled; whilst Eustacia found it necessary to perform some graceful feats of balancing whenever a small tuft of heather or root of furze protruded itself through the grass of the narrow track and entangled her feet. [/]

[Narrator] In two or three minutes she met her husband and his companion. [/]

[Narrator] IV. [/]
[Venn] Rough Coercion Is Employed Those words of Thomasin, which seemed so little, but meant so much, remained in the ears of Diggory Venn: “ Help me to keep him home in the evenings. ” [/]
[Narrator] On this occasion Venn had arrived on Egdon Heath only to cross to the other side — he had no further connection with the interests of the Yeobright family, and he had a business of his own to attend to. [/]
[Narrator] Yet he suddenly began to feel himself drifting into the old track of manœuvring on Thomasin ’s account. [/]
[Narrator] He sat in his van and considered. [/]
[Narrator] From Thomasin ’s words and manner he had plainly gathered that Wildeve neglected her. [/]
[Narrator] For whom could he neglect her if not for Eustacia? [/]
[Narrator] Yet it was scarcely credible that things had come to such a head as to indicate that Eustacia systematically encouraged him. [/]
[Narrator] Venn resolved to reconnoitre somewhat carefully the lonely road which led along the vale from Wildeve ’s dwelling to Clym ’s house at Alderworth. [/]
[Narrator] At this time, as has been seen, Wildeve was quite innocent of any predetermined act of intrigue, and except at the dance on the green he had not once met Eustacia since her marriage. [/]
[Narrator] But that the spirit of intrigue was in him had been shown by a recent romantic habit of his — a habit of going out after dark and strolling towards Alderworth, there looking at the moon and stars, looking at Eustacia ’s house, and walking back at leisure. [/]
[Narrator] Accordingly, when watching on the night after the festival, the reddleman saw him ascend by the little path, lean over the front gate of Clym ’s garden, sigh, and turn to go back again. [/]
[Narrator] It was plain that Wildeve ’s intrigue was rather ideal than real. [/]
[Narrator] Venn retreated before him down the hill to a place where the path was merely a deep groove between the heather; here he mysteriously bent over the ground for a few minutes, and retired. [/]
[Narrator] When Wildeve came on to that spot his ankle was caught by something, and he fell headlong. [/]
[Narrator] As soon as he had recovered the power of respiration he sat up and listened. [/]
[Narrator] There was not a sound in the gloom beyond the spiritless stir of the summer wind. [/]
[Narrator] Feeling about for the obstacle which had flung him down, he discovered that two tufts of heath had been tied together across the path, forming a loop, which to a traveller was certain overthrow. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve pulled off the string that bound them, and went on with tolerable quickness. [/]
[Narrator] On reaching home he found the cord to be of a reddish colour. [/]
[Narrator] It was just what he had expected. [/]
[Narrator] Although his weaknesses were not specially those akin to physical fear, this species of coup - de - Jarnac from one he knew too well troubled the mind of Wildeve. [/]
[Narrator] But his movements were unaltered thereby. [/]
[Narrator] A night or two later he again went along the vale to Alderworth, taking the precaution of keeping out of any path. [/]
[Narrator] The sense that he was watched, that craft was employed to circumvent his errant tastes, added piquancy to a journey so entirely sentimental, so long as the danger was of no fearful sort. [/]
[Narrator] He imagined that Venn and Mrs. Yeobright were in league, and felt that there was a certain legitimacy in combating such a coalition. [/]
[Narrator] The heath tonight appeared to be totally deserted; and Wildeve, after looking over Eustacia ’s garden gate for some little time, with a cigar in his mouth, was tempted by the fascination that emotional smuggling had for his nature to advance towards the window, which was not quite closed, the blind being only partly drawn down. [/]
[Narrator] He could see into the room, and Eustacia was sitting there alone. [/]

[Narrator] Securing one, he returned to the window, and holding the moth to the chink, opened his hand. [/]
[Narrator] The moth made towards the candle upon Eustacia ’s table, hovered round it two or three times, and flew into the flame. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia started up. [/]
[Narrator] This had been a well - known signal in old times when Wildeve had used to come secretly wooing to Mistover. [/]
[Narrator] She at once knew that Wildeve was outside, but before she could consider what to do her husband came in from upstairs. [/]

[Narrator] Meanwhile a little drama had been acted outside which saved Eustacia from all possibility of compromising herself that evening at least. [/]

[Narrator] This man, who carried a gun in his hand, looked on for a moment at the other ’s operation by the window, walked up to the house, knocked at the door, and then vanished round the corner and over the hedge. [/]
