[Wildeve] “ He has been watching me again. ” [/]
[Narrator] As his signal had been rendered futile by this uproarious rapping Wildeve withdrew, passed out at the gate, and walked quickly down the path without thinking of anything except getting away unnoticed. [/]
[Narrator] Halfway down the hill the path ran near a knot of stunted hollies, which in the general darkness of the scene stood as the pupil in a black eye. [/]
[Narrator] When Wildeve reached this point a report startled his ear, and a few spent gunshots fell among the leaves around him. [/]
[Narrator] There was no doubt that he himself was the cause of that gun ’s discharge; and he rushed into the clump of hollies, beating the bushes furiously with his stick; but nobody was there. [/]
[Narrator] This attack was a more serious matter than the last, and it was some time before Wildeve recovered his equanimity. [/]
[Narrator] A new and most unpleasant system of menace had begun, and the intent appeared to be to do him grievous bodily harm. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve had looked upon Venn ’s first attempt as a species of horseplay, which the reddleman had indulged in for want of knowing better; but now the boundary line was passed which divides the annoying from the perilous. [/]
[Narrator] Had Wildeve known how thoroughly in earnest Venn had become he might have been still more alarmed. [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman had been almost exasperated by the sight of Wildeve outside Clym ’s house, and he was prepared to go to any lengths short of absolutely shooting him, to terrify the young innkeeper out of his recalcitrant impulses. [/]
[Narrator] The doubtful legitimacy of such rough coercion did not disturb the mind of Venn. [/]
[Narrator] It troubles few such minds in such cases, and sometimes this is not to be regretted. [/]
[Narrator] From the impeachment of Strafford to Farmer Lynch ’s short way with the scamps of Virginia there have been many triumphs of justice which are mockeries of law. [/]
[Narrator] About half a mile below Clym ’s secluded dwelling lay a hamlet where lived one of the two constables who preserved the peace in the parish of Alderworth, and Wildeve went straight to the constable ’s cottage. [/]
[Narrator] Almost the first thing that he saw on opening the door was the constable ’s truncheon hanging to a nail, as if to assure him that here were the means to his purpose. [/]
[Narrator] On inquiry, however, of the constable ’s wife he learnt that the constable was not at home. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve said he would wait. [/]
[Narrator] The minutes ticked on, and the constable did not arrive. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve cooled down from his state of high indignation to a restless dissatisfaction with himself, the scene, the constable ’s wife, and the whole set of circumstances. [/]
[Narrator] He arose and left the house. [/]
[Narrator] Altogether, the experience of that evening had had a cooling, not to say a chilling, effect on misdirected tenderness, and Wildeve was in no mood to ramble again to Alderworth after nightfall in hope of a stray glance from Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] Thus far the reddleman had been tolerably successful in his rude contrivances for keeping down Wildeve ’s inclination to rove in the evening. [/]
[Narrator] He had nipped in the bud the possible meeting between Eustacia and her old lover this very night. [/]
[Narrator] But he had not anticipated that the tendency of his action would be to divert Wildeve ’s movement rather than to stop it. [/]
[Narrator] The gambling with the guineas had not conduced to make him a welcome guest to Clym; but to call upon his wife ’s relative was natural, and he was determined to see Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] It was necessary to choose some less untoward hour than ten o’clock at night. [/]
[Wildeve] “ Since it is unsafe to go in the evening, ” he said, “ I ’ll go by day. ” [/]
[Narrator] Meanwhile Venn had left the heath and gone to call upon Mrs. Yeobright, with whom he had been on friendly terms since she had learnt what a providential countermove he had made towards the restitution of the family guineas. [/]

[Narrator] At this very time of the reddleman ’s conversation with Mrs. Yeobright at Blooms - End another conversation on the same subject was languidly proceeding at Alderworth. [/]
[Narrator] All the day Clym had borne himself as if his mind were too full of its own matter to allow him to care about outward things, and his words now showed what had occupied his thoughts. [/]
[Narrator] It was just after the mysterious knocking that he began the theme. [/]

[Narrator] “ I am only thinking. ” “ What of? ” “ Partly of that moth whose skeleton is getting burnt up in the wick of the candle, ” she said slowly. [/]
[Narrator] “ But you know I always take an interest in what you say. ” “ Very well, dear. [/]
[Clym] Then I think I must go and call upon her. ” [/]
[Clym] . .. He went on with tender feeling: “ It is a thing I am not at all too proud to do, and only a fear that I might irritate her has kept me away so long. [/]
[Clym] But I must do something. [/]
[Clym] It is wrong in me to allow this sort of thing to go on. ” [/]

[Clym] But this is beside the point. [/]

[/]

[MrsYeobright] V. The Journey across the Heath Thursday, the thirty - first of August, was one of a series of days during which snug houses were stifling, and when cool draughts were treats; when cracks appeared in clayey gardens, and were called “ earthquakes ” by apprehensive children; when loose spokes were discovered in the wheels of carts and carriages; and when stinging insects haunted the air, the earth, and every drop of water that was to be found. [/]
[Narrator] In Mrs. Yeobright ’s garden large - leaved plants of a tender kind flagged by ten o’clock in the morning; rhubarb bent downward at eleven; and even stiff cabbages were limp by noon. [/]
[Narrator] It was about eleven o’clock on this day that Mrs. Yeobright started across the heath towards her son ’s house, to do her best in getting reconciled with him and Eustacia, in conformity with her words to the reddleman. [/]
[Narrator] She had hoped to be well advanced in her walk before the heat of the day was at its highest, but after setting out she found that this was not to be done. [/]
[Narrator] The sun had branded the whole heath with its mark, even the purple heath - flowers having put on a brownness under the dry blazes of the few preceding days. [/]
[Narrator] Every valley was filled with air like that of a kiln, and the clean quartz sand of the winter water - courses, which formed summer paths, had undergone a species of incineration since the drought had set in. [/]
[Narrator] In cool, fresh weather Mrs. Yeobright would have found no inconvenience in walking to Alderworth, but the present torrid attack made the journey a heavy undertaking for a woman past middle age; and at the end of the third mile she wished that she had hired Fairway to drive her a portion at least of the distance. [/]
[Narrator] But from the point at which she had arrived it was as easy to reach Clym ’s house as to get home again. [/]
[Narrator] So she went on, the air around her pulsating silently, and oppressing the earth with lassitude. [/]
[Narrator] She looked at the sky overhead, and saw that the sapphirine hue of the zenith in spring and early summer had been replaced by a metallic violet. [/]
[Narrator] Occasionally she came to a spot where independent worlds of ephemerons were passing their time in mad carousal, some in the air, some on the hot ground and vegetation, some in the tepid and stringy water of a nearly dried pool. [/]
[Narrator] All the shallower ponds had decreased to a vaporous mud amid which the maggoty shapes of innumerable obscure creatures could be indistinctly seen, heaving and wallowing with enjoyment. [/]
[Narrator] Being a woman not disinclined to philosophize she sometimes sat down under her umbrella to rest and to watch their happiness, for a certain hopefulness as to the result of her visit gave ease to her mind, and between important thoughts left it free to dwell on any infinitesimal matter which caught her eyes. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright had never before been to her son ’s house, and its exact position was unknown to her. [/]
[Narrator] She tried one ascending path and another, and found that they led her astray. [/]
[Narrator] Retracing her steps, she came again to an open level, where she perceived at a distance a man at work. [/]
[Narrator] She went towards him and inquired the way. [/]
[TheLabourer] The labourer pointed out the direction, and added, “ Do you see that furze - cutter, ma’am, going up that footpath yond? ” Mrs. Yeobright strained her eyes, and at last said that she did perceive him. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ Well, if you follow him you can make no mistake. [/]
[MrsYeobright] He ’s going to the same place, ma’am. ” [/]
[Narrator] She followed the figure indicated. [/]
[Narrator] He appeared of a russet hue, not more distinguishable from the scene around him than the green caterpillar from the leaf it feeds on. [/]
[Narrator] His progress when actually walking was more rapid than Mrs. Yeobright ’s; but she was enabled to keep at an equable distance from him by his habit of stopping whenever he came to a brake of brambles, where he paused awhile. [/]
[Narrator] On coming in her turn to each of these spots she found half a dozen long limp brambles which he had cut from the bush during his halt and laid out straight beside the path. [/]
[Narrator] They were evidently intended for furze - faggot bonds which he meant to collect on his return. [/]
[Narrator] The silent being who thus occupied himself seemed to be of no more account in life than an insect. [/]
[Narrator] He appeared as a mere parasite of the heath, fretting its surface in his daily labour as a moth frets a garment, entirely engrossed with its products, having no knowledge of anything in the world but fern, furze, heath, lichens, and moss. [/]
[Narrator] The furze - cutter was so absorbed in the business of his journey that he never turned his head; and his leather - legged and gauntleted form at length became to her as nothing more than a moving handpost to show her the way. [/]
[Narrator] Suddenly she was attracted to his individuality by observing peculiarities in his walk. [/]
[Narrator] It was a gait she had seen somewhere before; and the gait revealed the man to her, as the gait of Ahimaaz in the distant plain made him known to the watchman of the king. [/]
[MrsYeobright] “ His walk is exactly as my husband ’s used to be, ” she said; and then the thought burst upon her that the furze - cutter was her son. [/]
[Narrator] She was scarcely able to familiarize herself with this strange reality. [/]
[Narrator] She had been told that Clym was in the habit of cutting furze, but she had supposed that he occupied himself with the labour only at odd times, by way of useful pastime; yet she now beheld him as a furze - cutter and nothing more — wearing the regulation dress of the craft, and thinking the regulation thoughts, to judge by his motions. [/]
[Narrator] Planning a dozen hasty schemes for at once preserving him and Eustacia from this mode of life, she throbbingly followed the way, and saw him enter his own door. [/]
[Narrator] At one side of Clym ’s house was a knoll, and on the top of the knoll a clump of fir trees so highly thrust up into the sky that their foliage from a distance appeared as a black spot in the air above the crown of the hill. [/]
[Narrator] On reaching this place Mrs. Yeobright felt distressingly agitated, weary, and unwell. [/]
[Narrator] She ascended, and sat down under their shade to recover herself, and to consider how best to break the ground with Eustacia, so as not to irritate a woman underneath whose apparent indolence lurked passions even stronger and more active than her own. [/]
[Narrator] The trees beneath which she sat were singularly battered, rude, and wild, and for a few minutes Mrs. Yeobright dismissed thoughts of her own storm - broken and exhausted state to contemplate theirs. [/]
[Narrator] Not a bough in the nine trees which composed the group but was splintered, lopped, and distorted by the fierce weather that there held them at its mercy whenever it prevailed. [/]
[Narrator] Some were blasted and split as if by lightning, black stains as from fire marking their sides, while the ground at their feet was strewn with dead fir - needles and heaps of cones blown down in the gales of past years. [/]
[Narrator] The place was called the Devil ’s Bellows, and it was only necessary to come there on a March or November night to discover the forcible reasons for that name. [/]
[Narrator] On the present heated afternoon, when no perceptible wind was blowing, the trees kept up a perpetual moan which one could hardly believe to be caused by the air. [/]
[Narrator] Here she sat for twenty minutes or more ere [/]
[Narrator] she could summon resolution to go down to the door, her courage being lowered to zero by her physical lassitude. [/]
[Narrator] To any other person than a mother it might have seemed a little humiliating that she, the elder of the two women, should be the first to make advances. [/]
[Narrator] But Mrs. Yeobright had well considered all that, and she only thought how best to make her visit appear to Eustacia not abject but wise. [/]
[Narrator] From her elevated position the exhausted woman could perceive the roof of the house below, and the garden and the whole enclosure of the little domicile. [/]
[Narrator] And now, at the moment of rising, she saw a second man approaching the gate. [/]
[Narrator] His manner was peculiar, hesitating, and not that of a person come on business or by invitation. [/]
[Narrator] He surveyed the house with interest, and then walked round and scanned the outer boundary of the garden, as one might have done had it been the birthplace of Shakespeare, the prison of Mary Stuart, or the Château of Hougomont. [/]
[Narrator] After passing round and again reaching the gate he went in. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright was vexed at this, having reckoned on finding her son and his wife by themselves; but a moment ’s thought showed her that the presence of an acquaintance would take off the awkwardness of her first appearance in the house, by confining the talk to general matters until she had begun to feel comfortable with them. [/]
[Narrator] She came down the hill to the gate, and looked into the hot garden. [/]
[Narrator] There lay the cat asleep on the bare gravel of the path, as if beds, rugs, and carpets were unendurable. [/]
[Narrator] The leaves of the hollyhocks hung like half - closed umbrellas, the sap almost simmered in the stems, and foliage with a smooth surface glared like metallic mirrors. [/]
[Narrator] A small apple tree, of the sort called Ratheripe, grew just inside the gate, the only one which throve in the garden, by reason of the lightness of the soil; and among the fallen apples on the ground beneath were wasps rolling drunk with the juice, or creeping about the little caves in each fruit which they had eaten out before stupefied by its sweetness. [/]
[Narrator] By the door lay Clym ’s furze - hook and the last handful of faggot - bonds she had seen him gather; they had plainly been thrown down there as he entered the house. VI. [/]
[Narrator] A Conjuncture, and Its Result upon the Pedestrian Wildeve, as has been stated, was determined to visit Eustacia boldly, by day, and on the easy terms of a relation, since the reddleman had spied out and spoilt his walks to her by night. [/]
[Narrator] The spell that she had thrown over him in the moonlight dance made it impossible for a man having no strong puritanic force within him to keep away altogether. [/]
[Narrator] He merely calculated on meeting her and her husband in an ordinary manner, chatting a little while, and leaving again. [/]
[Narrator] Every outward sign was to be conventional; but the one great fact would be there to satisfy him — he would see her. [/]
[Narrator] He did not even desire Clym ’s absence, since it was just possible that Eustacia might resent any situation which could compromise her dignity as a wife, whatever the state of her heart towards him. [/]
[Narrator] Women were often so. [/]

[Narrator] Although the heat of summer was yet intense the days had considerably shortened, and before he had advanced a mile on his way all the heath purples, browns, and greens had merged in a uniform dress without airiness or graduation, and broken only by touches of white where the little heaps of clean quartz sand showed the entrance to a rabbit burrow, or where the white flints of a footpath lay like a thread over the slopes. [/]
[Narrator] In almost every one of the isolated and stunted thorns which grew here and there a nighthawk revealed his presence by whirring like the clack of a mill as long as he could hold his breath, then stopping, flapping his wings, wheeling round the bush, alighting, and after a silent interval of listening beginning to whirr again. [/]

[Olly] “ I ’ve used it when I used to go out nursing. ” [/]
[Clym] “ Then we must pray for daylight, to catch them, ” said Clym gloomily. [/]
[Sam] “ I will see what I can do, ” said Sam. [/]
[Narrator] He took a green hazel which he had used as a walking stick, split it at the end, inserted a small pebble, and with the lantern in his hand went out into the heath. [/]
[Narrator] Clym had by this time lit a small fire, and despatched Susan Nunsuch for a frying pan. [/]
[Narrator] Before she had returned Sam came in with three adders, one briskly coiling and uncoiling in the cleft of the stick, and the other two hanging dead across it. [/]
[Sam] “ I have only been able to get one alive and fresh as he ought to be, ” said Sam. [/]
[Sam] “ These limp ones are two I killed today at work; but as they do n’t die till the sun goes down they ca n’t be very stale meat. ” [/]
[Narrator] The live adder regarded the assembled group with a sinister look in its small black eye, and the beautiful brown and jet pattern on its back seemed to intensify with indignation. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Yeobright saw the creature, and the creature saw her — she quivered throughout, and averted her eyes. [/]

[Christian] “ Neighbours, how do we know but that something of the old serpent in God ’s garden, that gied the apple to the young woman with no clothes, lives on in adders and snakes still? [/]

[Christian] ’ Tis to be hoped he ca n’t ill - wish us! [/]
[Christian] There ’s folks in heath who ’ve been overlooked already. [/]

[GrandferCantle] “ ’ Twould have saved me many a brave danger in my time. ” [/]

[GrandferCantle] “ Now, if I had been stung by ten adders I should hardly have lost a day ’s work for’t, ” said Grandfer Cantle. [/]

[Narrator] VIII. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia Hears of Good Fortune, and Beholds Evil In the meantime Eustacia, left alone in her cottage at Alderworth, had become considerably depressed by the posture of affairs. [/]
[Narrator] The consequences which might result from Clym ’s discovery that his mother had been turned from his door that day were likely to be disagreeable, and this was a quality in events which she hated as much as the dreadful. [/]
[Narrator] To be left to pass the evening by herself was irksome to her at any time, and this evening it was more irksome than usual by reason of the excitements of the past hours. [/]
[Narrator] The two visits had stirred her into restlessness. [/]
[Narrator] She was not wrought to any great pitch of uneasiness by the probability of appearing in an ill light in the discussion between Clym and his mother, but she was wrought to vexation, and her slumbering activities were quickened to the extent of wishing that she had opened the door. [/]
[Narrator] She had certainly believed that Clym was awake, and the excuse would be an honest one as far as it went; but nothing could save her from censure in refusing to answer at the first knock. [/]
[Narrator] Yet, instead of blaming herself for the issue she laid the fault upon the shoulders of some indistinct, colossal Prince of the World, who had framed her situation and ruled her lot. [/]
[Narrator] At this time of the year it was pleasanter to walk by night than by day, and when Clym had been absent about an hour she suddenly resolved to go out in the direction of Blooms - End, on the chance of meeting him on his return. [/]
[Narrator] When she reached the garden gate she heard wheels approaching, and looking round beheld her grandfather coming up in his car. [/]
[HerGrandfather] “ I ca n’t stay a minute, thank ye, ” he answered to her greeting. [/]
[HerGrandfather] “ I am driving to East Egdon; but I came round here just to tell you the news. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps you have heard — about Mr. Wildeve ’s fortune? ” “ No, ” said Eustacia blankly. [/]

[Narrator] Eustacia stood motionless awhile. [/]

[Narrator] she asked. [/]

[HerGrandfather] What a fool you were, Eustacia! ” [/]

[HerGrandfather] “ I did not know there had ever been anything between you till lately; and, faith, I should have been hot and strong against it if I had known; but since it seems that there was some sniffing between ye, why the deuce did n’t you stick to him? ” Eustacia made no reply, but she looked as if she could say as much upon that subject as he if she chose. [/]

[TheOldMan] “ Not a bad fellow either, as far as he goes. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ He is quite well. ” “ It is a good thing for his cousin what - d’ye - call - her? [/]

[Narrator] And the captain drove on. [/]
[Narrator] When her grandfather was gone Eustacia went on her way mechanically; but her thoughts were no longer concerning her mother - in - law and Clym. Wildeve, notwithstanding his complaints against his fate, had been seized upon by destiny and placed in the sunshine once more. [/]
[Narrator] Eleven thousand pounds! [/]
[Narrator] From every Egdon point of view he was a rich man. [/]
[Narrator] In Eustacia ’s eyes, too, it was an ample sum — one sufficient to supply those wants of hers which had been stigmatized by Clym in his more austere moods as vain and luxurious. [/]
[Narrator] Though she was no lover of money she loved what money could bring; and the new accessories she imagined around him clothed Wildeve with a great deal of interest. [/]
[Narrator] She recollected now how quietly well - dressed he had been that morning — he had probably put on his newest suit, regardless of damage by briars and thorns. [/]
[Narrator] And then she thought of his manner towards herself. [/]

[Wildeve] “ Had he been a man to bear a jilt ill - will he would have told me of his good fortune in crowing tones; instead of doing that he mentioned not a word, in deference to my misfortunes, and merely implied that he loved me still, as one superior to him. ” Wildeve ’s silence that day on what had happened to him was just the kind of behaviour calculated to make an impression on such a woman. [/]
[Narrator] Those delicate touches of good taste were, in fact, one of the strong points in his demeanour towards the other sex. [/]
[Narrator] The peculiarity of Wildeve was that, while at one time passionate, upbraiding, and resentful towards a woman, at another he would treat her with such unparalleled grace as to make previous neglect appear as no discourtesy, injury as no insult, interference as a delicate attention, and the ruin of her honour as excess of chivalry. [/]
[Narrator] This man, whose admiration today Eustacia had disregarded, whose good wishes she had scarcely taken the trouble to accept, whom she had shown out of the house by the back door, was the possessor of eleven thousand pounds — a man of fair professional education, and one who had served his articles with a civil engineer. [I] BOOK FIFTH — THE DISCOVERY I. “ Wherefore Is Light Given to Him That Is in Misery ” One evening, about three weeks after the funeral of Mrs. Yeobright, when the silver face of the moon sent a bundle of beams directly upon the floor of Clym ’s house at Alderworth, a woman came forth from within. [/]
[Narrator] She reclined over the garden gate as if to refresh herself awhile. [/]
[Narrator] The pale lunar touches which make beauties of hags lent divinity to this face, already beautiful. [/]
[Narrator] She had not long been there when a man came up the road and with some hesitation said to her, “ How is he tonight, ma’am, if you please? ” “ He is better, though still very unwell, Humphrey, ” replied Eustacia. [/]
[AMan] “ Is he light - headed, ma’am? ” “ No. [/]
[AMan] He is quite sensible now. ” [/]
[Humphrey] “ Do he rave about his mother just the same, poor fellow? ” continued Humphrey. [/]
[AWoman] “ Just as much, though not quite so wildly, ” she said in a low voice. [/]
[AMan] “ It was very unfortunate, ma’am, that the boy Johnny should ever ha ’ told him his mother ’s dying words, about her being broken - hearted and cast off by her son. ’ [/]
[AMan] Twas enough to upset any man alive. ” [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia made no reply beyond that of a slight catch in her breath, as of one who fain would speak but could not; and Humphrey, declining her invitation to come in, went away. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia turned, entered the house, and ascended to the front bedroom, where a shaded light was burning. [/]
[Narrator] In the bed lay Clym, pale, haggard, wide awake, tossing to one side and to the other, his eyes lit by a hot light, as if the fire in their pupils were burning up their substance. [/]
[Clym] “ Is it you, Eustacia? ” [/]
[Narrator] he said as she sat down. [/]
[Clym] “ Yes, Clym. [/]
[Clym] I have been down to the gate. [/]
[Clym] The moon is shining beautifully, and there is not a leaf stirring. ” [/]
[Clym] “ Shining, is it? [/]
[Clym] What ’s the moon to a man like me? [/]
[Clym] Let it shine — let anything be, so that I never see another day! .. . [/]
[Clym] Eustacia, I do n’t know where to look — my thoughts go through me like swords. [/]
[Clym] O, if any man wants to make himself immortal by painting a picture of wretchedness, let him come here! ” [/]
[Clym] “ Why do you say so? ” “ I can not help feeling that I did my best to kill her. ” [/]
[Clym] “ No, Clym. ” “ Yes, it was so; it is useless to excuse me! [/]
[Clym] My conduct to her was too hideous — I made no advances; and she could not bring herself to forgive me. [/]
[Clym] Now she is dead! [/]
[Clym] If I had only shown myself willing to make it up with her sooner, and we had been friends, and then she had died, it would n’t be so hard to bear. [/]
[Clym] But I never went near her house, so she never came near mine, and did n’t know how welcome she would have been — that ’s what troubles me. [/]
[Clym] She did not know I was going to her house that very night, for she was too insensible to understand me. [/]
[Clym] If she had only come to see me! [/]
[Clym] I longed that she would. [/]
[Clym] But it was not to be. ” [/]
[Narrator] There escaped from Eustacia one of those shivering sighs which used to shake her like a pestilent blast. [/]
[Narrator] She had not yet told. [/]
[Narrator] But Yeobright was too deeply absorbed in the ramblings incidental to his remorseful state to notice her. [/]
[Narrator] During his illness he had been continually talking thus. [/]
[Narrator] Despair had been added to his original grief by the unfortunate disclosure of the boy who had received the last words of Mrs. Yeobright — words too bitterly uttered in an hour of misapprehension. [/]
[Narrator] Then his distress had overwhelmed him, and he longed for death as a field labourer longs for the shade. [/]
[Narrator] It was the pitiful sight of a man standing in the very focus of sorrow. [/]
[Narrator] He continually bewailed his tardy journey to his mother ’s house, because it was an error which could never be rectified, and insisted that he must have been horribly perverted by some fiend not to have thought before that it was his duty to go to her, since she did not come to him. [/]
[Eustacia] He would ask Eustacia to agree with him in his self - condemnation; and when she, seared inwardly by a secret she dared not tell, declared that she could not give an opinion, he would say, “ That ’s because you did n’t know my mother ’s nature. [/]

[Eustacia] Yet not unyielding — she was proud and reserved, no more. .. . [/]
[Eustacia] Yes, I can understand why she held out against me so long. [/]
[Eustacia] She was waiting for me. [/]
[Eustacia] I dare say she said a hundred times in her sorrow, ‘ What a return he makes for all the sacrifices I have made for him! ’ [/]
[Eustacia] I never went to her! [/]
[Eustacia] When I set out to visit her it was too late. [/]
[Eustacia] To think of that is nearly intolerable! ” [/]

[Narrator] “ Single men have, no doubt, a right to curse themselves as much as they please; but men with wives involve two in the doom they pray down. ” “ I am in too sorry a state to understand what you are refining on, ” said the wretched man. [/]

[Narrator] Eustacia was always anxious to avoid the sight of her husband in such a state as this, which had become as dreadful to her as the trial scene was to Judas Iscariot. [/]
[Narrator] It brought before her eyes the spectre of a worn - out woman knocking at a door which she would not open; and she shrank from contemplating it. [/]
[Narrator] Yet it was better for Yeobright himself when he spoke openly of his sharp regret, for in silence he endured infinitely more, and would sometimes remain so long in a tense, brooding mood, consuming himself by the gnawing of his thought, that it was imperatively necessary to make him talk aloud, that his grief might in some degree expend itself in the effort. [Narrator] A Lurid Light Breaks in upon a Darkened Understanding Clym ’s grief became mitigated by wearing itself out. [/]
[Narrator] His strength returned, and a month after the visit of Thomasin he might have been seen walking about the garden. [/]
[Narrator] Endurance and despair, equanimity and gloom, the tints of health and the pallor of death, mingled weirdly in his face. [/]
[Narrator] He was now unnaturally silent upon all of the past that related to his mother; and though Eustacia knew that he was thinking of it none the less, she was only too glad to escape the topic ever to bring it up anew. [/]
[Narrator] When his mind had been weaker his heart had led him to speak out; but reason having now somewhat recovered itself he sank into taciturnity. [/]
[Narrator] One evening when he was thus standing in the garden, abstractedly spudding up a weed with his stick, a bony figure turned the corner of the house and came up to him. [/]
[Clym] “ Christian, is n’t it? ” said Clym. [/]

[Narrator] The next day Yeobright set out for the old house at Blooms - End, which, with the garden, was now his own. [/]
[Narrator] His severe illness had hindered all preparations for his removal thither; but it had become necessary that he should go and overlook its contents, as administrator to his mother ’s little property; for which purpose he decided to pass the next night on the premises. [/]
[Narrator] He journeyed onward, not quickly or decisively, but in the slow walk of one who has been awakened from a stupefying sleep. [/]
[Narrator] It was early afternoon when he reached the valley. [/]
[Narrator] The expression of the place, the tone of the hour, were precisely those of many such occasions in days gone by; and these antecedent similarities fostered the illusion that she, who was there no longer, would come out to welcome him. [/]
[Narrator] The garden gate was locked and the shutters were closed, just as he himself had left them on the evening after the funeral. [/]
[Narrator] He unlocked the gate, and found that a spider had already constructed a large web, tying the door to the lintel, on the supposition that it was never to be opened again. [/]
[Narrator] When he had entered the house and flung back the shutters he set about his task of overhauling the cupboards and closets, burning papers, and considering how best to arrange the place for Eustacia ’s reception, until such time as he might be in a position to carry out his long - delayed scheme, should that time ever arrive. [/]
[Narrator] As he surveyed the rooms he felt strongly disinclined for the alterations which would have to be made in the time - honoured furnishing of his parents and grandparents, to suit Eustacia ’s modern ideas. [/]
[Narrator] The gaunt oak - cased clock, with the picture of the Ascension on the door panel and the Miraculous Draught of Fishes on the base; his grandmother ’s corner cupboard with the glass door, through which the spotted china was visible; the dumb - waiter; the wooden tea trays; the hanging fountain with the brass tap — whither would these venerable articles have to be banished? [/]

[Narrator] He continued in the same state all the afternoon. [/]

[Narrator] In after days he thought of it as a thing of singular significance. [/]

[Narrator] Eustacia Dresses Herself on a Black Morning A consciousness of a vast impassivity in all which lay around him took possession even of Yeobright in his wild walk towards Alderworth. [/]

[Narrator] Eustacia had not long been gone when there came a knock at the door of the bedroom; and Yeobright said, “ Well? ” It was the servant; and she replied, “ Somebody from Mrs. Wildeve ’s have called to tell ’ ee that the mis’ess and the baby are getting on wonderful well, and the baby ’s name is to be Eustacia Clementine. ” [/]
[Narrator] And the girl retired. [/]
[Clym] “ What a mockery! ” said Clym. [/]
[Clym] “ This unhappy marriage of mine to be perpetuated in that child ’s name! ” IV. [/]
[Narrator] The Ministrations of a Half - forgotten One Eustacia ’s journey was at first as vague in direction as that of thistledown on the wind. [/]
[Narrator] She did not know what to do. [/]
[Narrator] She wished it had been night instead of morning, that she might at least have borne her misery without the possibility of being seen. [/]
[Narrator] Tracing mile after mile along between the dying ferns and the wet white spiders ’ webs, she at length turned her steps towards her grandfather ’s house. [/]
[Narrator] She found the front door closed and locked. [/]
[Narrator] Mechanically she went round to the end where the stable was, and on looking in at the stable door she saw Charley standing within. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Captain Vye is not at home? ” [/]
[Narrator] she said. [/]
[TheLad] “ No, ma’am, ” said the lad in a flutter of feeling; “ he ’s gone to Weatherbury, and wo n’t be home till night. [/]
[TheLad] And the servant is gone home for a holiday. [/]
[TheLad] So the house is locked up. ” [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia ’s face was not visible to Charley as she stood at the doorway, her back being to the sky, and the stable but indifferently lighted; but the wildness of her manner arrested his attention. [/]
[Narrator] She turned and walked away across the enclosure to the gate, and was hidden by the bank. [/]
[Narrator] When she had disappeared Charley, with misgiving in his eyes, slowly came from the stable door, and going to another point in the bank he looked over. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia was leaning against it on the outside, her face covered with her hands, and her head pressing the dewy heather which bearded the bank ’s outer side. [/]
[Narrator] She appeared to be utterly indifferent to the circumstance that her bonnet, hair, and garments were becoming wet and disarranged by the moisture of her cold, harsh pillow. [/]
[Narrator] Clearly something was wrong. [/]
[Narrator] Charley had always regarded Eustacia as Eustacia had regarded Clym when she first beheld him — as a romantic and sweet vision, scarcely incarnate. [/]
[Narrator] He had been so shut off from her by the dignity of her look and the pride of her speech, except at that one blissful interval when he was allowed to hold her hand, that he had hardly deemed her a woman, wingless and earthly, subject to household conditions and domestic jars. [/]
[Narrator] The inner details of her life he had only conjectured. [/]
[Narrator] She had been a lovely wonder, predestined to an orbit in which the whole of his own was but a point; and this sight of her leaning like a helpless, despairing creature against a wild wet bank filled him with an amazed horror. [/]
[Narrator] He could no longer remain where he was. [/]

[Narrator] she said. [/]

[Narrator] V. An Old Move Inadvertently Repeated Charley ’s attentions to his former mistress were unbounded. [/]
[Narrator] The only solace to his own trouble lay in his attempts to relieve hers. [/]
[Narrator] Hour after hour he considered her wants; he thought of her presence there with a sort of gratitude, and, while uttering imprecations on the cause of her unhappiness, in some measure blessed the result. [/]
[Narrator] Perhaps she would always remain there, he thought, and then he would be as happy as he had been before. [/]
[Narrator] His dread was lest she should think fit to return to Alderworth, and in that dread his eyes, with all the inquisitiveness of affection, frequently sought her face when she was not observing him, as he would have watched the head of a stockdove to learn if it contemplated flight. [/]
[Narrator] Having once really succoured her, and possibly preserved her from the rashest of acts, he mentally assumed in addition a guardian ’s responsibility for her welfare. [/]
[Narrator] For this reason he busily endeavoured to provide her with pleasant distractions, bringing home curious objects which he found in the heath, such as white trumpet - shaped mosses, redheaded lichens, stone arrowheads used by the old tribes on Egdon, and faceted crystals from the hollows of flints. [/]
[Narrator] These he deposited on the premises in such positions that she should see them as if by accident. [/]
[Narrator] A week passed, Eustacia never going out of the house. [/]
[Narrator] Then she walked into the enclosed plot and looked through her grandfather ’s spyglass, as she had been in the habit of doing before her marriage. [/]
[Narrator] One day she saw, at a place where the highroad crossed the distant valley, a heavily laden wagon passing along. [/]
[Narrator] It was piled with household furniture. [/]
[Narrator] She looked again and again, and recognized it to be her own. [/]
[Narrator] In the evening her grandfather came indoors with a rumour that Yeobright had removed that day from Alderworth to the old house at Blooms - End. [/]
[Narrator] On another occasion when reconnoitring thus she beheld two female figures walking in the vale. [/]
[Narrator] The day was fine and clear; and the persons not being more than half a mile off she could see their every detail with the telescope. [/]
[Narrator] The woman walking in front carried a white bundle in her arms, from one end of which hung a long appendage of drapery; and when the walkers turned, so that the sun fell more directly upon them, Eustacia could see that the object was a baby. [/]
[Narrator] She called Charley, and asked him if he knew who they were, though she well guessed. [/]
[Charley] “ Mrs. Wildeve and the nurse - girl, ” said Charley. [/]

[Narrator] The lad was in good spirits that day, for the Fifth of November had again come round, and he was planning yet another scheme to divert her from her too absorbing thoughts. [/]
[Narrator] For two successive years his mistress had seemed to take pleasure in lighting a bonfire on the bank overlooking the valley; but this year she had apparently quite forgotten the day and the customary deed. [/]
[Narrator] He was careful not to remind her, and went on with his secret preparations for a cheerful surprise, the more zealously that he had been absent last time and unable to assist. [/]

[Narrator] Thomasin Argues with Her Cousin, and He Writes a Letter Yeobright was at this time at Blooms - End, hoping that Eustacia would return to him. [/]
[Narrator] The removal of furniture had been accomplished only that day, though Clym had lived in the old house for more than a week. [/]
[Narrator] He had spent the time in working about the premises, sweeping leaves from the garden paths, cutting dead stalks from the flower beds, and nailing up creepers which had been displaced by the autumn winds. [/]
[Narrator] He took no particular pleasure in these deeds, but they formed a screen between himself and despair. [/]
[Narrator] Moreover, it had become a religion with him to preserve in good condition all that had lapsed from his mother ’s hands to his own. [/]
[Narrator] During these operations he was constantly on the watch for Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] That there should be no mistake about her knowing where to find him he had ordered a notice board to be affixed to the garden gate at Alderworth, signifying in white letters whither he had removed. [/]
[Narrator] When a leaf floated to the earth he turned his head, thinking it might be her foot - fall. [/]
[Narrator] A bird searching for worms in the mould of the flower - beds sounded like her hand on the latch of the gate; and at dusk, when soft, strange ventriloquisms came from holes in the ground, hollow stalks, curled dead leaves, and other crannies wherein breezes, worms, and insects can work their will, he fancied that they were Eustacia, standing without and breathing wishes of reconciliation. [/]
[Narrator] Up to this hour he had persevered in his resolve not to invite her back. [/]
[Narrator] At the same time the severity with which he had treated her lulled the sharpness of his regret for his mother, and awoke some of his old solicitude for his mother ’s supplanter. [/]
[Narrator] Harsh feelings produce harsh usage, and this by reaction quenches the sentiments that gave it birth. [/]
[Narrator] The more he reflected the more he softened. [/]
[Narrator] But to look upon his wife as innocence in distress was impossible, though he could ask himself whether he had given her quite time enough — if he had not come a little too suddenly upon her on that sombre morning. [/]
[Narrator] Now that the first flush of his anger had paled he was disinclined to ascribe to her more than an indiscreet friendship with Wildeve, for there had not appeared in her manner the signs of dishonour. [/]
[Narrator] And this once admitted, an absolutely dark interpretation of her act towards his mother was no longer forced upon him. [/]
[Narrator] On the evening of the fifth November his thoughts of Eustacia were intense. [/]
[Narrator] Echoes from those past times when they had exchanged tender words all the day long came like the diffused murmur of a seashore left miles behind. [/]
[Clym] “ Surely, ” he said, “ she might have brought herself to communicate with me before now, and confess honestly what Wildeve was to her. ” [/]
[Narrator] Instead of remaining at home that night he determined to go and see Thomasin and her husband. [/]
[Narrator] If he found opportunity he would allude to the cause of the separation between Eustacia and himself, keeping silence, however, on the fact that there was a third person in his house when his mother was turned away. [/]
[Narrator] If it proved that Wildeve was innocently there he would doubtless openly mention it. [/]
[Narrator] If he were there with unjust intentions Wildeve, being a man of quick feeling, might possibly say something to reveal the extent to which Eustacia was compromised. [/]
[Narrator] But on reaching his cousin ’s house he found that only Thomasin was at home, Wildeve being at that time on his way towards the bonfire innocently lit by Charley at Mistover. [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin then, as always, was glad to see Clym, and took him to inspect the sleeping baby, carefully screening the candlelight from the infant ’s eyes with her hand. [/]
[Clym] “ Tamsin, have you heard that Eustacia is not with me now? ” [/]
[Narrator] he said when they had sat down again. [/]
[Thomasin] “ No, ” said Thomasin, alarmed. [/]
[Clym] “ And not that I have left Alderworth? ” “ No. I never hear tidings from Alderworth unless you bring them. [/]
[Clym] What is the matter? ” Clym in a disturbed voice related to her his visit to Susan Nunsuch ’s boy, the revelation he had made, and what had resulted from his charging Eustacia with having wilfully and heartlessly done the deed. [/]
[Narrator] He suppressed all mention of Wildeve ’s presence with her. [/]
[Thomasin] “ All this, and I not knowing it! ” murmured Thomasin in an awestruck tone, “ Terrible! [/]
[Thomasin] What could have made her — O, Eustacia! [/]
[Thomasin] And when you found it out you went in hot haste to her? [/]
[Narrator] Were you too cruel? —or is she really so wicked as she seems? ” “ Can a man be too cruel to his mother ’s enemy? ” “ I can fancy so. ” “ Very well, then — I’ll admit that he can. [/]
[Narrator] But now what is to be done? ” “ Make it up again — if a quarrel so deadly can ever be made up. [/]
[Thomasin] I almost wish you had not told me. [/]
[Thomasin] But do try to be reconciled. [/]
[Narrator] There are ways, after all, if you both wish to. ” “ I do n’t know that we do both wish to make it up, ” said Clym. [/]
[Narrator] “ If she had wished it, would she not have sent to me by this time? ” “ You seem to wish to, and yet you have not sent to her. ” “ True; but I have been tossed to and fro in doubt if I ought, after such strong provocation. [/]
[Clym] To see me now, Thomasin, gives you no idea of what I have been; of what depths I have descended to in these few last days. [/]
[Clym] O, it was a bitter shame to shut out my mother like that! [/]
[Narrator] Can I ever forget it, or even agree to see her again? ” “ She might not have known that anything serious would come of it, and perhaps she did not mean to keep Aunt out altogether. ” [/]
[Clym] “ She says herself that she did not. [/]
[Narrator] But the fact remains that keep her out she did. ” “ Believe her sorry, and send for her. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ How if she will not come? ” “ It will prove her guilty, by showing that it is her habit to nourish enmity. [/]
[Narrator] But I do not think that for a moment. ” “ I will do this. [/]
[Clym] I will wait for a day or two longer — not longer than two days certainly; and if she does not send to me in that time I will indeed send to her. [/]
[Clym] I thought to have seen Wildeve here tonight. [/]
[Clym] Is he from home? ” Thomasin blushed a little. [/]
[Thomasin] “ No, ” she said. [/]
[Thomasin] “ He is merely gone out for a walk. ” “ Why did n’t he take you with him? [/]
[Thomasin] The evening is fine. [/]
[Thomasin] You want fresh air as well as he. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ Oh, I do n’t care for going anywhere; besides, there is baby. ” [/]
[Clym] “ Yes, yes. [/]
[Clym] Well, I have been thinking whether I should not consult your husband about this as well as you, ” said Clym steadily. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I fancy I would not, ” she quickly answered. [/]
[Thomasin] “ It can do no good. ” [/]
[Narrator] Her cousin looked her in the face. [/]
[Narrator] No doubt Thomasin was ignorant that her husband had any share in the events of that tragic afternoon; but her countenance seemed to signify that she concealed some suspicion or thought of the reputed tender relations between Wildeve and Eustacia in days gone by. [/]
[Narrator] Clym, however, could make nothing of it, and he rose to depart, more in doubt than when he came. [/]
[TheYoungWoman] “ You will write to her in a day or two? ” said the young woman earnestly. [/]
[TheYoungWoman] “ I do so hope the wretched separation may come to an end. ” [/]
[Clym] “ I will, ” said Clym; “ I do n’t rejoice in my present state at all. ” [/]
[Narrator] And he left her and climbed over the hill to Blooms - End. [/]
[Narrator] Before going to bed he sat down and wrote the following letter: — MY DEAR EUSTACIA, —I must obey my heart without consulting my reason too closely. [/]
[Narrator] Will you come back to me? [/]
[Narrator] Do so, and the past shall never be mentioned. [/]
[Narrator] I was too severe; but O, Eustacia, the provocation! [/]
[Narrator] You do n’t know, you never will know, what those words of anger cost me which you drew down upon yourself. [/]
[Narrator] All that an honest man can promise you I promise now, which is that from me you shall never suffer anything on this score again. [/]
[Narrator] After all the vows we have made, Eustacia, I think we had better pass the remainder of our lives in trying to keep them. [/]
[Narrator] Come to me, then, even if you reproach me. [/]
[Narrator] I have thought of your sufferings that morning on which I parted from you; I know they were genuine, and they are as much as you ought to bear. [/]
[Narrator] Our love must still continue. [/]
[Narrator] Such hearts as ours would never have been given us but to be concerned with each other. [/]
[Narrator] I could not ask you back at first, Eustacia, for I was unable to persuade myself that he who was with you was not there as a lover. [/]
[Narrator] But if you will come and explain distracting appearances I do not question that you can show your honesty to me. [/]
[Narrator] Why have you not come before? [/]
[Narrator] Do you think I will not listen to you? [/]
[Narrator] Surely not, when you remember the kisses and vows we exchanged under the summer moon. [/]
[Narrator] Return then, and you shall be warmly welcomed. [/]
[Narrator] I can no longer think of you to your prejudice — I am but too much absorbed in justifying you. —Your husband as ever, CLYM. [/]
[Clym] “ There, ” he said, as he laid it in his desk, “ that ’s a good thing done. [/]
[Clym] If she does not come before tomorrow night I will send it to her. ” [/]
[Narrator] Meanwhile, at the house he had just left Thomasin sat sighing uneasily. [/]
[Narrator] Fidelity to her husband had that evening induced her to conceal all suspicion that Wildeve ’s interest in Eustacia had not ended with his marriage. [/]
[Narrator] But she knew nothing positive; and though Clym was her well - beloved cousin there was one nearer to her still. [/]
[Thomasin] When, a little later, Wildeve returned from his walk to Mistover, Thomasin said, “ Damon, where have you been? [/]
[Thomasin] I was getting quite frightened, and thought you had fallen into the river. [/]
[Narrator] I dislike being in the house by myself. ” “ Frightened? ” [/]
[Narrator] he said, touching her cheek as if she were some domestic animal. [/]
[HerHusband] “ Why, I thought nothing could frighten you. [/]
[HerHusband] It is that you are getting proud, I am sure, and do n’t like living here since we have risen above our business. [/]
[HerHusband] Well, it is a tedious matter, this getting a new house; but I could n’t have set about it sooner, unless our ten thousand pounds had been a hundred thousand, when we could have afforded to despise caution. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ No — I do n’t mind waiting — I would rather stay here twelve months longer than run any risk with baby. [/]
[Thomasin] But I do n’t like your vanishing so in the evenings. [/]
[Thomasin] There ’s something on your mind — I know there is, Damon. [/]
[Thomasin] You go about so gloomily, and look at the heath as if it were somebody ’s gaol instead of a nice wild place to walk in. ” [/]
[Narrator] He looked towards her with pitying surprise. [/]
[He] “ What, do you like Egdon Heath? ” he said. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I like what I was born near to; I admire its grim old face. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ Pooh, my dear. [/]
[Thomasin] You do n’t know what you like. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ I am sure I do. [/]
[Thomasin] There ’s only one thing unpleasant about Egdon. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ What ’s that? ” “ You never take me with you when you walk there. [/]
[Thomasin] Why do you wander so much in it yourself if you so dislike it? ” [/]
[Narrator] The inquiry, though a simple one, was plainly disconcerting, and he sat down before replying. [/]
[He] “ I do n’t think you often see me there. [/]
[He] Give an instance. ” [/]
[I] “ I will, ” she answered triumphantly. [/]
[I] “ When you went out this evening I thought that as baby was asleep I would see where you were going to so mysteriously without telling me. [/]
[I] So I ran out and followed behind you. [/]
[I] You stopped at the place where the road forks, looked round at the bonfires, and then said, ‘ Damn it, I ’ll go! ’ [/]
[I] And you went quickly up the left - hand road. [/]
[I] Then I stood and watched you. ” [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve frowned, afterwards saying, with a forced smile, “ Well, what wonderful discovery did you make? ” “ There — now you are angry, and we wo n’t talk of this any more. ” [/]
[Narrator] She went across to him, sat on a footstool, and looked up in his face. [/]
[He] “ Nonsense! ” [/]
[He] he said, “ that ’s how you always back out. [/]
[He] We will go on with it now we have begun. [/]
[He] What did you next see? [/]
[He] I particularly want to know. ” [/]
[I] “ Do n’t be like that, Damon! ” [/]
[Narrator] she murmured. [/]
[I] “ I did n’t see anything. [/]
[I] You vanished out of sight, and then I looked round at the bonfires and came in. ” [/]
[Wildeve] “ Perhaps this is not the only time you have dogged my steps. [/]
[Narrator] Are you trying to find out something bad about me? ” “ Not at all! [/]
[Narrator] I have never done such a thing before, and I should n’t have done it now if words had not sometimes been dropped about you. ” “ What _ do _ you mean? ” he impatiently asked. [/]
[I] “ They say — they say you used to go to Alderworth in the evenings, and it puts into my mind what I have heard about — ” Wildeve turned angrily and stood up in front of her. [/]
[He] “ Now, ” he said, flourishing his hand in the air, “ just out with it, madam! [/]
[He] I demand to know what remarks you have heard. ” [/]
[He] “ Well, I heard that you used to be very fond of Eustacia — nothing more than that, though dropped in a bit - by - bit way. [/]
[He] You ought not to be angry! ” [/]
[Narrator] He observed that her eyes were brimming with tears. [/]
[He] “ Well, ” he said, “ there is nothing new in that, and of course I do n’t mean to be rough towards you, so you need not cry. [/]
[He] Now, do n’t let us speak of the subject any more. ” [/]
[Narrator] And no more was said, Thomasin being glad enough of a reason for not mentioning Clym ’s visit to her that evening, and his story. [/]
[Narrator] VII. [/]
[Narrator] The Night of the Sixth of November Having resolved on flight Eustacia at times seemed anxious that something should happen to thwart her own intention. [/]
[Narrator] The only event that could really change her position was the appearance of Clym. [/]
[Narrator] The glory which had encircled him as her lover was departed now; yet some good simple quality of his would occasionally return to her memory and stir a momentary throb of hope that he would again present himself before her. [/]
[Narrator] But calmly considered it was not likely that such a severance as now existed would ever close up — she would have to live on as a painful object, isolated, and out of place. [/]
[Narrator] She had used to think of the heath alone as an uncongenial spot to be in; she felt it now of the whole world. [/]
[Narrator] Towards evening on the sixth her determination to go away again revived. [/]
[Narrator] About four o’clock she packed up anew the few small articles she had brought in her flight from Alderworth, and also some belonging to her which had been left here; the whole formed a bundle not too large to be carried in her hand for a distance of a mile or two. [/]
[Narrator] The scene without grew darker; mud - coloured clouds bellied downwards from the sky like vast hammocks slung across it, and with the increase of night a stormy wind arose; but as yet there was no rain. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia could not rest indoors, having nothing more to do, and she wandered to and fro on the hill, not far from the house she was soon to leave. [/]
[Narrator] In these desultory ramblings she passed the cottage of Susan Nunsuch, a little lower down than her grandfather ’s. [/]
[Narrator] The door was ajar, and a riband of bright firelight fell over the ground without. [/]
[Narrator] As Eustacia crossed the firebeams she appeared for an instant as distinct as a figure in a phantasmagoria — a creature of light surrounded by an area of darkness; the moment passed, and she was absorbed in night again. [/]
[Narrator] A woman who was sitting inside the cottage had seen and recognized her in that momentary irradiation. [/]
[Narrator] This was Susan herself, occupied in preparing a posset for her little boy, who, often ailing, was now seriously unwell. [/]
[Narrator] Susan dropped the spoon, shook her fist at the vanished figure, and then proceeded with her work in a musing, absent way. [/]
[Narrator] At eight o’clock, the hour at which Eustacia had promised to signal Wildeve if ever she signalled at all, she looked around the premises to learn if the coast was clear, went to the furze - rick, and pulled thence a long - stemmed bough of that fuel. [/]
[Narrator] This she carried to the corner of the bank, and, glancing behind to see if the shutters were all closed, she struck a light, and kindled the furze. [/]
[Narrator] When it was thoroughly ablaze Eustacia took it by the stem and waved it in the air above her head till it had burned itself out. [/]
[Narrator] She was gratified, if gratification were possible to such a mood, by seeing a similar light in the vicinity of Wildeve ’s residence a minute or two later. [/]
[Narrator] Having agreed to keep watch at this hour every night, in case she should require assistance, this promptness proved how strictly he had held to his word. [/]
[Narrator] Four hours after the present time, that is, at midnight, he was to be ready to drive her to Budmouth, as prearranged. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia returned to the house. [/]
[Narrator] Supper having been got over she retired early, and sat in her bedroom waiting for the time to go by. [/]
[Narrator] The night being dark and threatening, Captain Vye had not strolled out to gossip in any cottage or to call at the inn, as was sometimes his custom on these long autumn nights; and he sat sipping grog alone downstairs. [/]
[Narrator] About ten o’clock there was a knock at the door. [/]
[Narrator] When the servant opened it the rays of the candle fell upon the form of Fairway. [/]
[TheServant] “ I was a - forced to go to Lower Mistover tonight, ” he said, “ and Mr. Yeobright asked me to leave this here on my way; but, faith, I put it in the lining of my hat, and thought no more about it till I got back and was hasping my gate before going to bed. [/]
[TheServant] So I have run back with it at once. ” [/]
[Narrator] He handed in a letter and went his way. [/]
[Narrator] The girl brought it to the captain, who found that it was directed to Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] He turned it over and over, and fancied that the writing was her husband ’s, though he could not be sure. [/]
[Narrator] However, he decided to let her have it at once if possible, and took it upstairs for that purpose; but on reaching the door of her room and looking in at the keyhole he found there was no light within, the fact being that Eustacia, without undressing, had flung herself upon the bed, to rest and gather a little strength for her coming journey. [/]
[Narrator] Her grandfather concluded from what he saw that he ought not to disturb her; and descending again to the parlour he placed the letter on the mantelpiece to give it to her in the morning. [/]
[Narrator] At eleven o’clock he went to bed himself, smoked for some time in his bedroom, put out his light at half - past eleven, and then, as was his invariable custom, pulled up the blind before getting into bed, that he might see which way the wind blew on opening his eyes in the morning, his bedroom window commanding a view of the flagstaff and vane. [/]
[Narrator] Just as he had lain down he was surprised to observe the white pole of the staff flash into existence like a streak of phosphorus drawn downwards across the shade of night without. [/]
[Narrator] Only one explanation met this — a light had been suddenly thrown upon the pole from the direction of the house. [/]
[Narrator] As everybody had retired to rest the old man felt it necessary to get out of bed, open the window softly, and look to the right and left. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia ’s bedroom was lighted up, and it was the shine from her window which had lighted the pole. [/]
[Narrator] Wondering what had aroused her, he remained undecided at the window, and was thinking of fetching the letter to slip it under her door, when he heard a slight brushing of garments on the partition dividing his room from the passage. [/]
[Narrator] The captain concluded that Eustacia, feeling wakeful, had gone for a book, and would have dismissed the matter as unimportant if he had not also heard her distinctly weeping as she passed. [/]
[TheCaptain] “ She is thinking of that husband of hers, ” he said to himself. [/]
[TheCaptain] “ Ah, the silly goose! [/]
[TheCaptain] she had no business to marry him. [/]
[TheCaptain] I wonder if that letter is really his? ” He arose, threw his boat - cloak round him, opened the door, and said, “ Eustacia! ” [/]
[Narrator] There was no answer. [/]
[TheCaptain] “ Eustacia! ” [/]
[TheCaptain] he repeated louder, “ there is a letter on the mantelpiece for you. ” [/]
[Narrator] But no response was made to this statement save an imaginary one from the wind, which seemed to gnaw at the corners of the house, and the stroke of a few drops of rain upon the windows. [/]
[Narrator] He went on to the landing, and stood waiting nearly five minutes. [/]
[Narrator] Still she did not return. [/]
[Narrator] He went back for a light, and prepared to follow her; but first he looked into her bedroom. [/]
[Narrator] There, on the outside of the quilt, was the impression of her form, showing that the bed had not been opened; and, what was more significant, she had not taken her candlestick downstairs. [/]
[Narrator] He was now thoroughly alarmed; and hastily putting on his clothes he descended to the front door, which he himself had bolted and locked. [/]
[Narrator] It was now unfastened. [/]
[Narrator] There was no longer any doubt that Eustacia had left the house at this midnight hour; and whither could she have gone? [/]
[Narrator] To follow her was almost impossible. [/]
[Narrator] Had the dwelling stood in an ordinary road, two persons setting out, one in each direction, might have made sure of overtaking her; but it was a hopeless task to seek for anybody on a heath in the dark, the practicable directions for flight across it from any point being as numerous as the meridians radiating from the pole. [/]
[Narrator] Perplexed what to do, he looked into the parlour, and was vexed to find that the letter still lay there untouched. [/]
[Narrator] At half - past eleven, finding that the house was silent, Eustacia had lighted her candle, put on some warm outer wrappings, taken her bag in her hand, and, extinguishing the light again, descended the staircase. [/]
[Narrator] When she got into the outer air she found that it had begun to rain, and as she stood pausing at the door it increased, threatening to come on heavily. [/]
[Narrator] But having committed herself to this line of action there was no retreating for bad weather. [/]
[Narrator] Even the receipt of Clym ’s letter would not have stopped her now. [/]
[Narrator] The gloom of the night was funereal; all nature seemed clothed in crape. [/]
[Narrator] The spiky points of the fir trees behind the house rose into the sky like the turrets and pinnacles of an abbey. [/]
[Narrator] Nothing below the horizon was visible save a light which was still burning in the cottage of Susan Nunsuch. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia opened her umbrella and went out from the enclosure by the steps over the bank, after which she was beyond all danger of being perceived. [/]
[Narrator] Skirting the pool, she followed the path towards Rainbarrow, occasionally stumbling over twisted furze roots, tufts of rushes, or oozing lumps of fleshy fungi, which at this season lay scattered about the heath like the rotten liver and lungs of some colossal animal. [/]
[Narrator] The moon and stars were closed up by cloud and rain to the degree of extinction. [/]
[Narrator] It was a night which led the traveller ’s thoughts instinctively to dwell on nocturnal scenes of disaster in the chronicles of the world, on all that is terrible and dark in history and legend — the last plague of Egypt, the destruction of Sennacherib ’s host, the agony in Gethsemane. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia at length reached Rainbarrow, and stood still there to think. [/]
[Narrator] Never was harmony more perfect than that between the chaos of her mind and the chaos of the world without. [/]
[Narrator] A sudden recollection had flashed on her this moment — she had not money enough for undertaking a long journey. [/]
[Narrator] Amid the fluctuating sentiments of the day her unpractical mind had not dwelt on the necessity of being well - provided, and now that she thoroughly realized the conditions she sighed bitterly and ceased to stand erect, gradually crouching down under the umbrella as if she were drawn into the Barrow by a hand from beneath. [/]
[Narrator] Could it be that she was to remain a captive still? [/]
[Narrator] Money — she had never felt its value before. [/]
[Narrator] Even to efface herself from the country means were required. [/]
[Narrator] To ask Wildeve for pecuniary aid without allowing him to accompany her was impossible to a woman with a shadow of pride left in her; to fly as his mistress — and she knew that he loved her — was of the nature of humiliation. [/]
[Narrator] Anyone who had stood by now would have pitied her, not so much on account of her exposure to weather, and isolation from all of humanity except the mouldered remains inside the tumulus; but for that other form of misery which was denoted by the slightly rocking movement that her feelings imparted to her person. [/]
[Narrator] Extreme unhappiness weighed visibly upon her. [/]
[Narrator] Between the drippings of the rain from her umbrella to her mantle, from her mantle to the heather, from the heather to the earth, very similar sounds could be heard coming from her lips; and the tearfulness of the outer scene was repeated upon her face. [/]
[Narrator] The wings of her soul were broken by the cruel obstructiveness of all about her; and even had she seen herself in a promising way of getting to Budmouth, entering a steamer, and sailing to some opposite port, she would have been but little more buoyant, so fearfully malignant were other things. [/]
[Narrator] She uttered words aloud. [/]
[Narrator] When a woman in such a situation, neither old, deaf, crazed, nor whimsical, takes upon herself to sob and soliloquize aloud there is something grievous the matter. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Can I go, can I go? ” [/]
[Narrator] she moaned. [/]
[I] “ He ’s not _ great _ enough for me to give myself to — he does not suffice for my desire! .. . [/]
[I] If he had been a Saul or a Bonaparte — ah! [/]
[I] But to break my marriage vow for him — it is too poor a luxury! .. . [/]
[I] And I have no money to go alone! [/]
[I] And if I could, what comfort to me? [/]
[I] I must drag on next year, as I have dragged on this year, and the year after that as before. [/]
[I] How I have tried and tried to be a splendid woman, and how destiny has been against me! .. . [/]
[I] I do not deserve my lot! ” [/]
[Narrator] she cried in a frenzy of bitter revolt. [/]
[I] “ O, the cruelty of putting me into this ill - conceived world! [/]
[I] I was capable of much; but I have been injured and blighted and crushed by things beyond my control! [/]
[I] O, how hard it is of Heaven to devise such tortures for me, who have done no harm to Heaven at all! ” [/]
[Narrator] The distant light which Eustacia had cursorily observed in leaving the house came, as she had divined, from the cottage window of Susan Nunsuch. [/]
[Narrator] What Eustacia did not divine was the occupation of the woman within at that moment. [/]
[Susan] Susan ’s sight of her passing figure earlier in the evening, not five minutes after the sick boy ’s exclamation, “ Mother, I do feel so bad! ” persuaded the matron that an evil influence was certainly exercised by Eustacia ’s propinquity. [/]
[Narrator] On this account Susan did not go to bed as soon as the evening ’s work was over, as she would have done at ordinary times. [/]
[Narrator] To counteract the malign spell which she imagined poor Eustacia to be working, the boy ’s mother busied herself with a ghastly invention of superstition, calculated to bring powerlessness, atrophy, and annihilation on any human being against whom it was directed. [/]
[Narrator] It was a practice well known on Egdon at that date, and one that is not quite extinct at the present day. [/]
[Narrator] She passed with her candle into an inner room, where, among other utensils, were two large brown pans, containing together perhaps a hundredweight of liquid honey, the produce of the bees during the foregoing summer. [/]
[Narrator] On a shelf over the pans was a smooth and solid yellow mass of a hemispherical form, consisting of beeswax from the same take of honey. [/]
[Narrator] Susan took down the lump, and cutting off several thin slices, heaped them in an iron ladle, with which she returned to the living - room, and placed the vessel in the hot ashes of the fireplace. [/]
[Narrator] As soon as the wax had softened to the plasticity of dough she kneaded the pieces together. [/]
[Narrator] And now her face became more intent. [/]
[Narrator] She began moulding the wax; and it was evident from her manner of manipulation that she was endeavouring to give it some preconceived form. [/]
[Narrator] The form was human. [/]
[Narrator] By warming and kneading, cutting and twisting, dismembering and re - joining the incipient image she had in about a quarter of an hour produced a shape which tolerably well resembled a woman, and was about six inches high. [/]
[Narrator] She laid it on the table to get cold and hard. [/]
[Narrator] Meanwhile she took the candle and went upstairs to where the little boy was lying. [/]
[Susan] “ Did you notice, my dear, what Mrs. Eustacia wore this afternoon besides the dark dress? ” “ A red ribbon round her neck. ” [/]
[Susan] “ Anything else? ” “ No — except sandal - shoes. ” “ A red ribbon and sandal - shoes, ” she said to herself. [/]
[Narrator] Mrs. Nunsuch went and searched till she found a fragment of the narrowest red ribbon, which she took downstairs and tied round the neck of the image. [/]
[Narrator] Then fetching ink and a quilt from the rickety bureau by the window, she blackened the feet of the image to the extent presumably covered by shoes; and on the instep of each foot marked cross - lines in the shape taken by the sandalstrings of those days. [/]
[Narrator] Finally she tied a bit of black thread round the upper part of the head, in faint resemblance to a snood worn for confining the hair. [/]
[Narrator] Susan held the object at arm ’s length and contemplated it with a satisfaction in which there was no smile. [/]
[Narrator] To anybody acquainted with the inhabitants of Egdon Heath the image would have suggested Eustacia Yeobright. [/]
[Narrator] From her workbasket in the window - seat the woman took a paper of pins, of the old long and yellow sort, whose heads were disposed to come off at their first usage. [/]
[Narrator] These she began to thrust into the image in all directions, with apparently excruciating energy. [/]
[Narrator] Probably as many as fifty were thus inserted, some into the head of the wax model, some into the shoulders, some into the trunk, some upwards through the soles of the feet, till the figure was completely permeated with pins. [/]
[Narrator] She turned to the fire. [/]
[Narrator] It had been of turf; and though the high heap of ashes which turf fires produce was somewhat dark and dead on the outside, upon raking it abroad with the shovel the inside of the mass showed a glow of red heat. [/]
[Narrator] She took a few pieces of fresh turf from the chimney - corner and built them together over the glow, upon which the fire brightened. [/]
[Narrator] Seizing with the tongs the image that she had made of Eustacia, she held it in the heat, and watched it as it began to waste slowly away. [/]
[Narrator] And while she stood thus engaged there came from between her lips a murmur of words. [/]
[Narrator] It was a strange jargon — the Lord ’s Prayer repeated backwards — the incantation usual in proceedings for obtaining unhallowed assistance against an enemy. [/]
[Narrator] Susan uttered the lugubrious discourse three times slowly, and when it was completed the image had considerably diminished. [/]
[Narrator] As the wax dropped into the fire a long flame arose from the spot, and curling its tongue round the figure ate still further into its substance. [/]
[Narrator] A pin occasionally dropped with the wax, and the embers heated it red as it lay. VIII. [/]
[Narrator] Rain, Darkness, and Anxious Wanderers While the effigy of Eustacia was melting to nothing, and the fair woman herself was standing on Rainbarrow, her soul in an abyss of desolation seldom plumbed by one so young, Yeobright sat lonely at Blooms - End. [/]
[Narrator] He had fulfilled his word to Thomasin by sending off Fairway with the letter to his wife, and now waited with increased impatience for some sound or signal of her return. [/]
[Narrator] Were Eustacia still at Mistover the very least he expected was that she would send him back a reply tonight by the same hand; though, to leave all to her inclination, he had cautioned Fairway not to ask for an answer. [/]
[Narrator] If one were handed to him he was to bring it immediately; if not, he was to go straight home without troubling to come round to Blooms - End again that night. [/]
[Narrator] But secretly Clym had a more pleasing hope. [/]
[Narrator] Eustacia might possibly decline to use her pen — it was rather her way to work silently — and surprise him by appearing at his door. [/]
[Narrator] How fully her mind was made up to do otherwise he did not know. [/]
[Narrator] To Clym ’s regret it began to rain and blow hard as the evening advanced. [/]
[Narrator] The wind rasped and scraped at the corners of the house, and filliped the eavesdroppings like peas against the panes. [/]
[Narrator] He walked restlessly about the untenanted rooms, stopping strange noises in windows and doors by jamming splinters of wood into the casements and crevices, and pressing together the leadwork of the quarries where it had become loosened from the glass. [/]
[Narrator] It was one of those nights when cracks in the walls of old churches widen, when ancient stains on the ceilings of decayed manor houses are renewed and enlarged from the size of a man ’s hand to an area of many feet. [/]
[Narrator] The little gate in the palings before his dwelling continually opened and clicked together again, but when he looked out eagerly nobody was there; it was as if invisible shapes of the dead were passing in on their way to visit him. [/]
[Narrator] Between ten and eleven o’clock, finding that neither Fairway nor anybody else came to him, he retired to rest, and despite his anxieties soon fell asleep. [/]
[Narrator] His sleep, however, was not very sound, by reason of the expectancy he had given way to, and he was easily awakened by a knocking which began at the door about an hour after. [/]
[Narrator] Clym arose and looked out of the window. [/]
[Narrator] Rain was still falling heavily, the whole expanse of heath before him emitting a subdued hiss under the downpour. [/]
[Narrator] It was too dark to see anything at all. [/]
