[Narrator] Clym crossed by the right - hand track towards the inn. Thomasin, being left alone, took off some of her wet garments, carried the baby upstairs to Clym ’s bed, and then came down to the sitting - room again, where she made a larger fire, and began drying herself. [/]
[Narrator] The fire soon flared up the chimney, giving the room an appearance of comfort that was doubled by contrast with the drumming of the storm without, which snapped at the windowpanes and breathed into the chimney strange low utterances that seemed to be the prologue to some tragedy. [/]
[Narrator] But the least part of Thomasin was in the house, for her heart being at ease about the little girl upstairs she was mentally following Clym on his journey. [/]
[Narrator] Having indulged in this imaginary peregrination for some considerable interval, she became impressed with a sense of the intolerable slowness of time. [/]
[Narrator] But she sat on. [/]
[Narrator] The moment then came when she could scarcely sit longer, and it was like a satire on her patience to remember that Clym could hardly have reached the inn as yet. [/]
[Narrator] At last she went to the baby ’s bedside. [/]
[Narrator] The child was sleeping soundly; but her imagination of possibly disastrous events at her home, the predominance within her of the unseen over the seen, agitated her beyond endurance. [/]
[Narrator] She could not refrain from going down and opening the door. [/]
[Narrator] The rain still continued, the candlelight falling upon the nearest drops and making glistening darts of them as they descended across the throng of invisible ones behind. [/]
[Narrator] To plunge into that medium was to plunge into water slightly diluted with air. [/]
[Narrator] But the difficulty of returning to her house at this moment made her all the more desirous of doing so — anything was better than suspense. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I have come here well enough, ” she said, “ and why should n’t I go back again? [/]
[Thomasin] It is a mistake for me to be away. ” [/]
[Narrator] She hastily fetched the infant, wrapped it up, cloaked herself as before, and shoveling the ashes over the fire, to prevent accidents, went into the open air. [/]
[Narrator] Pausing first to put the door key in its old place behind the shutter, she resolutely turned her face to the confronting pile of firmamental darkness beyond the palings, and stepped into its midst. [/]
[Narrator] But Thomasin ’s imagination being so actively engaged elsewhere, the night and the weather had for her no terror beyond that of their actual discomfort and difficulty. [/]
[Narrator] She was soon ascending Blooms - End valley and traversing the undulations on the side of the hill. [/]
[Narrator] The noise of the wind over the heath was shrill, and as if it whistled for joy at finding a night so congenial as this. [/]
[Narrator] Sometimes the path led her to hollows between thickets of tall and dripping bracken, dead, though not yet prostrate, which enclosed her like a pool. [/]
[Narrator] When they were more than usually tall she lifted the baby to the top of her head, that it might be out of the reach of their drenching fronds. [/]
[Narrator] On higher ground, where the wind was brisk and sustained, the rain flew in a level flight without sensible descent, so that it was beyond all power to imagine the remoteness of the point at which it left the bosoms of the clouds. [/]
[Narrator] Here self - defence was impossible, and individual drops stuck into her like the arrows into Saint Sebastian. [/]
[Narrator] She was enabled to avoid puddles by the nebulous paleness which signified their presence, though beside anything less dark than the heath they themselves would have appeared as blackness. [/]
[Narrator] Yet in spite of all this Thomasin was not sorry that she had started. [/]
[Narrator] To her there were not, as to Eustacia, demons in the air, and malice in every bush and bough. [/]
[Narrator] The drops which lashed her face were not scorpions, but prosy rain; Egdon in the mass was no monster whatever, but impersonal open ground. [/]
[Narrator] Her fears of the place were rational, her dislikes of its worst moods reasonable. [/]
[Narrator] At this time it was in her view a windy, wet place, in which a person might experience much discomfort, lose the path without care, and possibly catch cold. [/]
[Narrator] If the path is well known the difficulty at such times of keeping therein is not altogether great, from its familiar feel to the feet; but once lost it is irrecoverable. [/]
[Narrator] Owing to her baby, who somewhat impeded Thomasin ’s view forward and distracted her mind, she did at last lose the track. [/]
[Narrator] This mishap occurred when she was descending an open slope about two - thirds home. [/]
[Narrator] Instead of attempting, by wandering hither and thither, the hopeless task of finding such a mere thread, she went straight on, trusting for guidance to her general knowledge of the contours, which was scarcely surpassed by Clym ’s or by that of the heath - croppers themselves. [/]

[Narrator] And then her heart rose into her throat, and she could say no more. [/]
[Narrator] IX. [/]
[Narrator] Sights and Sounds Draw the Wanderers Together Having seen Eustacia ’s signal from the hill at eight o’clock, Wildeve immediately prepared to assist her in her flight, and, as he hoped, accompany her. [/]
[Narrator] He was somewhat perturbed, and his manner of informing Thomasin that he was going on a journey was in itself sufficient to rouse her suspicions. [/]
[Narrator] When she had gone to bed he collected the few articles he would require, and went upstairs to the money - chest, whence he took a tolerably bountiful sum in notes, which had been advanced to him on the property he was so soon to have in possession, to defray expenses incidental to the removal. [/]
[Narrator] He then went to the stable and coach - house to assure himself that the horse, gig, and harness were in a fit condition for a long drive. [/]
[Narrator] Nearly half an hour was spent thus, and on returning to the house Wildeve had no thought of Thomasin being anywhere but in bed. [/]
[Narrator] He had told the stable lad not to stay up, leading the boy to understand that his departure would be at three or four in the morning; for this, though an exceptional hour, was less strange than midnight, the time actually agreed on, the packet from Budmouth sailing between one and two. [/]
[Narrator] At last all was quiet, and he had nothing to do but to wait. [/]
[Narrator] By no effort could he shake off the oppression of spirits which he had experienced ever since his last meeting with Eustacia, but he hoped there was that in his situation which money could cure. [/]
[Narrator] He had persuaded himself that to act not ungenerously towards his gentle wife by settling on her the half of his property, and with chivalrous devotion towards another and greater woman by sharing her fate, was possible. [/]
[Narrator] And though he meant to adhere to Eustacia ’s instructions to the letter, to deposit her where she wished and to leave her, should that be her will, the spell that she had cast over him intensified, and his heart was beating fast in the anticipated futility of such commands in the face of a mutual wish that they should throw in their lot together. [/]
[Narrator] He would not allow himself to dwell long upon these conjectures, maxims, and hopes, and at twenty minutes to twelve he again went softly to the stable, harnessed the horse, and lit the lamps; whence, taking the horse by the head, he led him with the covered car out of the yard to a spot by the roadside some quarter of a mile below the inn. [/]
[Narrator] Here Wildeve waited, slightly sheltered from the driving rain by a high bank that had been cast up at this place. [/]
[Narrator] Along the surface of the road where lit by the lamps the loosened gravel and small stones scudded and clicked together before the wind, which, leaving them in heaps, plunged into the heath and boomed across the bushes into darkness. [/]
[Narrator] Only one sound rose above this din of weather, and that was the roaring of a ten - hatch weir to the southward, from a river in the meads which formed the boundary of the heath in this direction. [/]
[Narrator] He lingered on in perfect stillness till he began to fancy that the midnight hour must have struck. [/]
[Narrator] A very strong doubt had arisen in his mind if Eustacia would venture down the hill in such weather; yet knowing her nature he felt that she might. [/]
[He] “ Poor thing! ’ tis like her ill - luck, ” he murmured. [/]
[Narrator] At length he turned to the lamp and looked at his watch. [/]
[Narrator] To his surprise it was nearly a quarter past midnight. [/]
[Narrator] He now wished that he had driven up the circuitous road to Mistover, a plan not adopted because of the enormous length of the route in proportion to that of the pedestrian ’s path down the open hillside, and the consequent increase of labour for the horse. [/]
[Narrator] At this moment a footstep approached; but the light of the lamps being in a different direction the comer was not visible. [/]
[Narrator] The step paused, then came on again. [/]
[Wildeve] “ Eustacia? ” said Wildeve. [/]
[Narrator] The person came forward, and the light fell upon the form of Clym, glistening with wet, whom Wildeve immediately recognized; but Wildeve, who stood behind the lamp, was not at once recognized by Yeobright. [/]
[Narrator] He stopped as if in doubt whether this waiting vehicle could have anything to do with the flight of his wife or not. [/]
[Narrator] The sight of Yeobright at once banished Wildeve ’s sober feelings, who saw him again as the deadly rival from whom Eustacia was to be kept at all hazards. [/]
[Narrator] Hence Wildeve did not speak, in the hope that Clym would pass by without particular inquiry. [/]
[Narrator] While they both hung thus in hesitation a dull sound became audible above the storm and wind. [/]
[Narrator] Its origin was unmistakable — it was the fall of a body into the stream in the adjoining mead, apparently at a point near the weir. [/]
[Narrator] Both started. [/]
[Clym] “ Good God! [/]
[Clym] can it be she? ” said Clym. [/]
[Wildeve] “ Why should it be she? ” said Wildeve, in his alarm forgetting that he had hitherto screened himself. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Ah! —that ’s you, you traitor, is it? ” cried Yeobright. [/]
[Eustacia] “ Why should it be she? [/]
[Eustacia] Because last week she would have put an end to her life if she had been able. [/]
[Eustacia] She ought to have been watched! [/]
[Eustacia] Take one of the lamps and come with me. ” [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright seized the one on his side and hastened on; Wildeve did not wait to unfasten the other, but followed at once along the meadow track to the weir, a little in the rear of Clym. [/]
[Narrator] Shadwater Weir had at its foot a large circular pool, fifty feet in diameter, into which the water flowed through ten huge hatches, raised and lowered by a winch and cogs in the ordinary manner. [/]
[Narrator] The sides of the pool were of masonry, to prevent the water from washing away the bank; but the force of the stream in winter was sometimes such as to undermine the retaining wall and precipitate it into the hole. [/]
[Narrator] Clym reached the hatches, the framework of which was shaken to its foundations by the velocity of the current. [/]
[Narrator] Nothing but the froth of the waves could be discerned in the pool below. [/]
[Narrator] He got upon the plank bridge over the race, and holding to the rail, that the wind might not blow him off, crossed to the other side of the river. [/]
[Narrator] There he leant over the wall and lowered the lamp, only to behold the vortex formed at the curl of the returning current. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve meanwhile had arrived on the former side, and the light from Yeobright ’s lamp shed a flecked and agitated radiance across the weir pool, revealing to the ex - engineer the tumbling courses of the currents from the hatches above. [/]
[Narrator] Across this gashed and puckered mirror a dark body was slowly borne by one of the backward currents. [/]
[Wildeve] “ O, my darling! ” exclaimed Wildeve in an agonized voice; and, without showing sufficient presence of mind even to throw off his greatcoat, he leaped into the boiling caldron. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright could now also discern the floating body, though but indistinctly; and imagining from Wildeve ’s plunge that there was life to be saved he was about to leap after. [/]
[Narrator] Bethinking himself of a wiser plan, he placed the lamp against a post to make it stand upright, and running round to the lower part of the pool, where there was no wall, he sprang in and boldly waded upwards towards the deeper portion. [/]
[Narrator] Here he was taken off his legs, and in swimming was carried round into the centre of the basin, where he perceived Wildeve struggling. [/]
[Narrator] While these hasty actions were in progress here, Venn and Thomasin had been toiling through the lower corner of the heath in the direction of the light. [/]
[Narrator] They had not been near enough to the river to hear the plunge, but they saw the removal of the carriage lamp, and watched its motion into the mead. [/]
[Narrator] As soon as they reached the car and horse Venn guessed that something new was amiss, and hastened to follow in the course of the moving light. [/]
[Narrator] Venn walked faster than Thomasin, and came to the weir alone. [/]
[Narrator] The lamp placed against the post by Clym still shone across the water, and the reddleman observed something floating motionless. [/]
[Narrator] Being encumbered with the infant, he ran back to meet Thomasin. [/]
[TheReddleman] “ Take the baby, please, Mrs. Wildeve, ” he said hastily. [/]
[TheReddleman] “ Run home with her, call the stable lad, and make him send down to me any men who may be living near. [/]
[TheReddleman] Somebody has fallen into the weir. ” [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin took the child and ran. [/]
[Narrator] When she came to the covered car the horse, though fresh from the stable, was standing perfectly still, as if conscious of misfortune. [/]
[Narrator] She saw for the first time whose it was. [/]
[Narrator] She nearly fainted, and would have been unable to proceed another step but that the necessity of preserving the little girl from harm nerved her to an amazing self - control. [/]
[Narrator] In this agony of suspense she entered the house, put the baby in a place of safety, woke the lad and the female domestic, and ran out to give the alarm at the nearest cottage. [/]
[Narrator] Diggory, having returned to the brink of the pool, observed that the small upper hatches or floats were withdrawn. [/]
[Narrator] He found one of these lying upon the grass, and taking it under one arm, and with his lantern in his hand, entered at the bottom of the pool as Clym had done. [/]
[Narrator] As soon as he began to be in deep water he flung himself across the hatch; thus supported he was able to keep afloat as long as he chose, holding the lantern aloft with his disengaged hand. [/]
[Narrator] Propelled by his feet, he steered round and round the pool, ascending each time by one of the back streams and descending in the middle of the current. [/]
[Narrator] At first he could see nothing. [/]
[Narrator] Then amidst the glistening of the whirlpools and the white clots of foam he distinguished a woman ’s bonnet floating alone. [/]
[Narrator] His search was now under the left wall, when something came to the surface almost close beside him. [/]
[Narrator] It was not, as he had expected, a woman, but a man. [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman put the ring of the lantern between his teeth, seized the floating man by the collar, and, holding on to the hatch with his remaining arm, struck out into the strongest race, by which the unconscious man, the hatch, and himself were carried down the stream. [/]
[Narrator] As soon as Venn found his feet dragging over the pebbles of the shallower part below he secured his footing and waded towards the brink. [/]
[Narrator] There, where the water stood at about the height of his waist, he flung away the hatch, and attempted to drag forth the man. [/]
[Narrator] This was a matter of great difficulty, and he found as the reason that the legs of the unfortunate stranger were tightly embraced by the arms of another man, who had hitherto been entirely beneath the surface. [/]
[Narrator] At this moment his heart bounded to hear footsteps running towards him, and two men, roused by Thomasin, appeared at the brink above. [/]
[Narrator] They ran to where Venn was, and helped him in lifting out the apparently drowned persons, separating them, and laying them out upon the grass. [/]
[Narrator] Venn turned the light upon their faces. [/]
[Narrator] The one who had been uppermost was Yeobright; he who had been completely submerged was Wildeve. [/]
[Venn] “ Now we must search the hole again, ” said Venn. [/]
[Venn] “ A woman is in there somewhere. [/]
[Venn] Get a pole. ” [/]
[Narrator] One of the men went to the footbridge and tore off the handrail. [/]
[Narrator] The reddleman and the two others then entered the water together from below as before, and with their united force probed the pool forwards to where it sloped down to its central depth. [/]
[Narrator] Venn was not mistaken in supposing that any person who had sunk for the last time would be washed down to this point, for when they had examined to about halfway across something impeded their thrust. [/]
[Venn] “ Pull it forward, ” said Venn, and they raked it in with the pole till it was close to their feet. [/]
[Narrator] Venn vanished under the stream, and came up with an armful of wet drapery enclosing a woman ’s cold form, which was all that remained of the desperate Eustacia. [/]
[Narrator] When they reached the bank there stood Thomasin, in a stress of grief, bending over the two unconscious ones who already lay there. [/]
[Narrator] The horse and cart were brought to the nearest point in the road, and it was the work of a few minutes only to place the three in the vehicle. [/]
[Narrator] Venn led on the horse, supporting Thomasin upon his arm, and the two men followed, till they reached the inn. [/]
[Narrator] The woman who had been shaken out of her sleep by Thomasin had hastily dressed herself and lighted a fire, the other servant being left to snore on in peace at the back of the house. [/]
[Narrator] The insensible forms of Eustacia, Clym, and Wildeve were then brought in and laid on the carpet, with their feet to the fire, when such restorative processes as could be thought of were adopted at once, the stableman being in the meantime sent for a doctor. [/]
[Narrator] But there seemed to be not a whiff of life in either of the bodies. [/]
[Narrator] Then Thomasin, whose stupor of grief had been thrust off awhile by frantic action, applied a bottle of hartshorn to Clym ’s nostrils, having tried it in vain upon the other two. [/]
[Narrator] He sighed. [/]
[TheWomanWhoHadBeenShakenOutOfHerSleepByThomasin] “ Clym ’s alive! ” [/]
[Narrator] she exclaimed. [/]
[Narrator] He soon breathed distinctly, and again and again did she attempt to revive her husband by the same means; but Wildeve gave no sign. [/]
[Narrator] There was too much reason to think that he and Eustacia both were for ever beyond the reach of stimulating perfumes. [/]
[Narrator] Their exertions did not relax till the doctor arrived, when one by one, the senseless three were taken upstairs and put into warm beds. [/]
[Narrator] Venn soon felt himself relieved from further attendance, and went to the door, scarcely able yet to realize the strange catastrophe that had befallen the family in which he took so great an interest. [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin surely would be broken down by the sudden and overwhelming nature of this event. [/]
[Narrator] No firm and sensible Mrs. Yeobright lived now to support the gentle girl through the ordeal; and, whatever an unimpassioned spectator might think of her loss of such a husband as Wildeve, there could be no doubt that for the moment she was distracted and horrified by the blow. [/]
[Narrator] As for himself, not being privileged to go to her and comfort her, he saw no reason for waiting longer in a house where he remained only as a stranger. [/]
[Narrator] He returned across the heath to his van. [/]
[Narrator] The fire was not yet out, and everything remained as he had left it. [/]
[Narrator] Venn now bethought himself of his clothes, which were saturated with water to the weight of lead. [/]
[Narrator] He changed them, spread them before the fire, and lay down to sleep. [/]
[Narrator] But it was more than he could do to rest here while excited by a vivid imagination of the turmoil they were in at the house he had quitted, and, blaming himself for coming away, he dressed in another suit, locked up the door, and again hastened across to the inn. [/]
[Narrator] Rain was still falling heavily when he entered the kitchen. [/]
[Narrator] A bright fire was shining from the hearth, and two women were bustling about, one of whom was Olly Dowden. [/]
[Venn] “ Well, how is it going on now? ” said Venn in a whisper. [/]
[TwoWomen] “ Mr. Yeobright is better; but Mrs. Yeobright and Mr. Wildeve are dead and cold. [/]
[TwoWomen] The doctor says they were quite gone before they were out of the water. ” “ Ah! [/]
[TwoWomen] I thought as much when I hauled ’em up. [/]
[TwoWomen] And Mrs. Wildeve? ” “ She is as well as can be expected. [/]
[TwoWomen] The doctor had her put between blankets, for she was almost as wet as they that had been in the river, poor young thing. [/]
[TwoWomen] You do n’t seem very dry, reddleman. ” [/]
[TwoWomen] “ Oh, ’ tis not much. [/]
[TwoWomen] I have changed my things. [/]

[TwoWomen] Mis’ess says you be to have whatever you want, and she was sorry when she was told that you ’d gone away. ” [/]
[Narrator] Venn drew near to the fireplace, and looked into the flames in an absent mood. [/]
[Narrator] The steam came from his leggings and ascended the chimney with the smoke, while he thought of those who were upstairs. [/]
[Narrator] Two were corpses, one had barely escaped the jaws of death, another was sick and a widow. [/]
[Narrator] The last occasion on which he had lingered by that fireplace was when the raffle was in progress; when Wildeve was alive and well; Thomasin active and smiling in the next room; Yeobright and Eustacia just made husband and wife, and Mrs. Yeobright living at Blooms - End. [/]
[Narrator] It had seemed at that time that the then position of affairs was good for at least twenty years to come. [/]
[Narrator] Yet, of all the circle, he himself was the only one whose situation had not materially changed. [/]
[Narrator] While he ruminated a footstep descended the stairs. [/]

[Narrator] The woman was so engrossed with her occupation that she hardly saw Venn. [/]

[/]
[Venn] “ What be they? ” said Venn. [/]
[TheWoman] “ Poor master ’s banknotes, ” she answered. [/]
[TheWoman] “ They were found in his pocket when they undressed him. ” [/]

[TheWoman] “ That we shall never know, ” said she. [/]
[Narrator] Venn was loth to depart, for all on earth that interested him lay under this roof. [/]
[Narrator] As nobody in the house had any more sleep that night, except the two who slept for ever, there was no reason why he should not remain. [/]

[Narrator] Then the woman came and unpinned them, and, folding them together, carried the handful upstairs. [/]
[Narrator] Presently the doctor appeared from above with the look of a man who could do no more, and, pulling on his gloves, went out of the house, the trotting of his horse soon dying away upon the road. [/]
[Narrator] At four o’clock there was a gentle knock at the door. [/]
[Narrator] It was from Charley, who had been sent by Captain Vye to inquire if anything had been heard of Eustacia. [/]
[Venn] The girl who admitted him looked in his face as if she did not know what answer to return, and showed him in to where Venn was seated, saying to the reddleman, “ Will you tell him, please? ” Venn told. [/]
[Narrator] Charley ’s only utterance was a feeble, indistinct sound. [/]
[Narrator] He stood quite still; then he burst out spasmodically, “ I shall see her once more? ” “ I dare say you may see her, ” said Diggory gravely. [/]
[Narrator] “ But had n’t you better run and tell Captain Vye? ” “ Yes, yes. [/]
[Charley] Only I do hope I shall see her just once again. ” [/]

[Narrator] It was Yeobright. [/]
[Clym] Neither Venn nor Charley spoke, and Clym continued, “ You shall see her. [/]
[Clym] There will be time enough to tell the captain when it gets daylight. [/]
[Clym] You would like to see her too — would you not, Diggory? [/]
[Clym] She looks very beautiful now. ” [/]
[Narrator] Venn assented by rising to his feet, and with Charley he followed Clym to the foot of the staircase, where he took off his boots; Charley did the same. [/]

[Narrator] Here he went to the bedside and folded back the sheet. [/]
[Narrator] They stood silently looking upon Eustacia, who, as she lay there still in death, eclipsed all her living phases. [/]
[Narrator] Pallor did not include all the quality of her complexion, which seemed more than whiteness; it was almost light. [/]
[Narrator] The expression of her finely carved mouth was pleasant, as if a sense of dignity had just compelled her to leave off speaking. [/]
[Narrator] Eternal rigidity had seized upon it in a momentary transition between fervour and resignation. [/]
[Narrator] Her black hair was looser now than either of them had ever seen it before, and surrounded her brow like a forest. [/]
[Narrator] The stateliness of look which had been almost too marked for a dweller in a country domicile had at last found an artistically happy background. [/]
[Narrator] Nobody spoke, till at length Clym covered her and turned aside. [/]
[Clym] “ Now come here, ” he said. [/]
[Narrator] They went to a recess in the same room, and there, on a smaller bed, lay another figure — Wildeve. [/]
[Narrator] Less repose was visible in his face than in Eustacia ’s, but the same luminous youthfulness overspread it, and the least sympathetic observer would have felt at sight of him now that he was born for a higher destiny than this. [/]
[Narrator] The only sign upon him of his recent struggle for life was in his fingertips, which were worn and sacrificed in his dying endeavours to obtain a hold on the face of the weir - wall. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright ’s manner had been so quiet, he had uttered so few syllables since his reappearance, that Venn imagined him resigned. [/]
[Narrator] It was only when they had left the room and stood upon the landing that the true state of his mind was apparent. [/]
[Wildeve] Here he said, with a wild smile, inclining his head towards the chamber in which Eustacia lay, “ She is the second woman I have killed this year. [/]
[Wildeve] I was a great cause of my mother ’s death, and I am the chief cause of hers. ” [/]
[Venn] “ How? ” said Venn. [/]
[Wildeve] “ I spoke cruel words to her, and she left my house. [/]
[Wildeve] I did not invite her back till it was too late. [/]
[Wildeve] It is I who ought to have drowned myself. [/]
[Wildeve] It would have been a charity to the living had the river overwhelmed me and borne her up. [/]
[Wildeve] But I can not die. [/]
[Wildeve] Those who ought to have lived lie dead; and here am I alive! ” [/]
[Venn] “ But you ca n’t charge yourself with crimes in that way, ” said Venn. [/]
[Venn] “ You may as well say that the parents be the cause of a murder by the child, for without the parents the child would never have been begot. ” [/]
[Wildeve] “ Yes, Venn, that is very true; but you do n’t know all the circumstances. [/]
[Wildeve] If it had pleased God to put an end to me it would have been a good thing for all. [/]
[Wildeve] But I am getting used to the horror of my existence. [/]
[Wildeve] They say that a time comes when men laugh at misery through long acquaintance with it. [/]
[Narrator] Surely that time will soon come to me! ” “ Your aim has always been good, ” said Venn. [/]
[Narrator] “ Why should you say such desperate things? ” “ No, they are not desperate. [/]
[Wildeve] They are only hopeless; and my great regret is that for what I have done no man or law can punish me! ” BOOK SIXTH — AFTERCOURSES I. The Inevitable Movement Onward The story of the deaths of Eustacia and Wildeve was told throughout Egdon, and far beyond, for many weeks and months. [/]
[Narrator] All the known incidents of their love were enlarged, distorted, touched up, and modified, till the original reality bore but a slight resemblance to the counterfeit presentation by surrounding tongues. [/]
[Narrator] Yet, upon the whole, neither the man nor the woman lost dignity by sudden death. [/]
[Narrator] Misfortune had struck them gracefully, cutting off their erratic histories with a catastrophic dash, instead of, as with many, attenuating each life to an uninteresting meagreness, through long years of wrinkles, neglect, and decay. [/]
[Narrator] On those most nearly concerned the effect was somewhat different. [/]
[Narrator] Strangers who had heard of many such cases now merely heard of one more; but immediately where a blow falls no previous imaginings amount to appreciable preparation for it. [/]
[Narrator] The very suddenness of her bereavement dulled, to some extent, Thomasin ’s feelings; yet irrationally enough, a consciousness that the husband she had lost ought to have been a better man did not lessen her mourning at all. [/]
[Narrator] On the contrary, this fact seemed at first to set off the dead husband in his young wife ’s eyes, and to be the necessary cloud to the rainbow. [/]
[Narrator] But the horrors of the unknown had passed. [/]
[Narrator] Vague misgivings about her future as a deserted wife were at an end. [/]
[Narrator] The worst had once been matter of trembling conjecture; it was now matter of reason only, a limited badness. [/]
[Narrator] Her chief interest, the little Eustacia, still remained. [/]
[Narrator] There was humility in her grief, no defiance in her attitude; and when this is the case a shaken spirit is apt to be stilled. [/]
[Narrator] Could Thomasin ’s mournfulness now and Eustacia ’s serenity during life have been reduced to common measure, they would have touched the same mark nearly. [/]
[Narrator] But Thomasin ’s former brightness made shadow of that which in a sombre atmosphere was light itself. [/]
[Narrator] The spring came and calmed her; the summer came and soothed her; the autumn arrived, and she began to be comforted, for her little girl was strong and happy, growing in size and knowledge every day. [/]
[Narrator] Outward events flattered Thomasin not a little. [/]
[Narrator] Wildeve had died intestate, and she and the child were his only relatives. [/]
[Narrator] When administration had been granted, all the debts paid, and the residue of her husband ’s uncle ’s property had come into her hands, it was found that the sum waiting to be invested for her own and the child ’s benefit was little less than ten thousand pounds. [/]
[Narrator] Where should she live? [/]
[Narrator] The obvious place was Blooms - End. [/]
[Narrator] The old rooms, it is true, were not much higher than the between - decks of a frigate, necessitating a sinking in the floor under the new clock - case she brought from the inn, and the removal of the handsome brass knobs on its head, before there was height for it to stand; but, such as the rooms were, there were plenty of them, and the place was endeared to her by every early recollection. [/]
[Narrator] Clym very gladly admitted her as a tenant, confining his own existence to two rooms at the top of the back staircase, where he lived on quietly, shut off from Thomasin and the three servants she had thought fit to indulge in now that she was a mistress of money, going his own ways, and thinking his own thoughts. [/]
[Narrator] His sorrows had made some change in his outward appearance; and yet the alteration was chiefly within. [/]
[Narrator] It might have been said that he had a wrinkled mind. [/]
[Narrator] He had no enemies, and he could get nobody to reproach him, which was why he so bitterly reproached himself. [/]
[Narrator] He did sometimes think he had been ill - used by fortune, so far as to say that to be born is a palpable dilemma, and that instead of men aiming to advance in life with glory they should calculate how to retreat out of it without shame. [/]
[Narrator] But that he and his had been sarcastically and pitilessly handled in having such irons thrust into their souls he did not maintain long. [/]
[Narrator] It is usually so, except with the sternest of men. [/]
[Narrator] Human beings, in their generous endeavour to construct a hypothesis that shall not degrade a First Cause, have always hesitated to conceive a dominant power of lower moral quality than their own; and, even while they sit down and weep by the waters of Babylon, invent excuses for the oppression which prompts their tears. [/]
[Narrator] Thus, though words of solace were vainly uttered in his presence, he found relief in a direction of his own choosing when left to himself. [/]
[Narrator] For a man of his habits the house and the hundred and twenty pounds a year which he had inherited from his mother were enough to supply all worldly needs. [/]
[Narrator] Resources do not depend upon gross amounts, but upon the proportion of spendings to takings. [/]
[Narrator] He frequently walked the heath alone, when the past seized upon him with its shadowy hand, and held him there to listen to its tale. [/]
[Narrator] His imagination would then people the spot with its ancient inhabitants — forgotten Celtic tribes trod their tracks about him, and he could almost live among them, look in their faces, and see them standing beside the barrows which swelled around, untouched and perfect as at the time of their erection. [/]
[Narrator] Those of the dyed barbarians who had chosen the cultivable tracts were, in comparison with those who had left their marks here, as writers on paper beside writers on parchment. [/]
[Narrator] Their records had perished long ago by the plough, while the works of these remained. [/]
[Narrator] Yet they all had lived and died unconscious of the different fates awaiting their relics. [/]
[Narrator] It reminded him that unforeseen factors operate in the evolution of immortality. [/]
[Narrator] Winter again came round, with its winds, frosts, tame robins, and sparkling starlight. [/]
[Narrator] The year previous Thomasin had hardly been conscious of the season ’s advance; this year she laid her heart open to external influences of every kind. [/]
[Narrator] The life of this sweet cousin, her baby, and her servants, came to Clym ’s senses only in the form of sounds through a wood partition as he sat over books of exceptionally large type; but his ear became at last so accustomed to these slight noises from the other part of the house that he almost could witness the scenes they signified. [/]
[Narrator] A faint beat of half - seconds conjured up Thomasin rocking the cradle, a wavering hum meant that she was singing the baby to sleep, a crunching of sand as between millstones raised the picture of Humphrey ’s, Fairway ’s, or Sam ’s heavy feet crossing the stone floor of the kitchen; a light boyish step, and a gay tune in a high key, betokened a visit from Grandfer Cantle; a sudden break - off in the Grandfer ’s utterances implied the application to his lips of a mug of small beer, a bustling and slamming of doors meant starting to go to market; for Thomasin, in spite of her added scope of gentility, led a ludicrously narrow life, to the end that she might save every possible pound for her little daughter. [/]
[Narrator] One summer day Clym was in the garden, immediately outside the parlour window, which was as usual open. [/]
[Narrator] He was looking at the pot - flowers on the sill; they had been revived and restored by Thomasin to the state in which his mother had left them. [/]
[Narrator] He heard a slight scream from Thomasin, who was sitting inside the room. [/]
[Thomasin] “ O, how you frightened me! ” [/]
[Narrator] she said to someone who had entered. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I thought you were the ghost of yourself. ” [/]
[Narrator] Clym was curious enough to advance a little further and look in at the window. [/]
[Narrator] To his astonishment there stood within the room Diggory Venn, no longer a reddleman, but exhibiting the strangely altered hues of an ordinary Christian countenance, white shirt - front, light flowered waistcoat, blue - spotted neckerchief, and bottle - green coat. [/]
[Narrator] Nothing in this appearance was at all singular but the fact of its great difference from what he had formerly been. [/]
[Narrator] Red, and all approach to red, was carefully excluded from every article of clothes upon him; for what is there that persons just out of harness dread so much as reminders of the trade which has enriched them? [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright went round to the door and entered. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I was so alarmed! ” said Thomasin, smiling from one to the other. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I could n’t believe that he had got white of his own accord! [/]
[Thomasin] It seemed supernatural. ” [/]
[Venn] “ I gave up dealing in reddle last Christmas, ” said Venn. [/]
[Venn] “ It was a profitable trade, and I found that by that time I had made enough to take the dairy of fifty cows that my father had in his lifetime. [/]
[Venn] I always thought of getting to that place again if I changed at all, and now I am there. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ How did you manage to become white, Diggory? ” [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin asked. [/]
[Venn] “ I turned so by degrees, ma’am. ” “ You look much better than ever you did before. ” [/]
[Narrator] Venn appeared confused; and Thomasin, seeing how inadvertently she had spoken to a man who might possibly have tender feelings for her still, blushed a little. [/]
[Narrator] Clym saw nothing of this, and added good - humouredly — “ What shall we have to frighten Thomasin ’s baby with, now you have become a human being again? ” “ Sit down, Diggory, ” said Thomasin, “ and stay to tea. ” [/]
[Thomasin] Venn moved as if he would retire to the kitchen, when Thomasin said with pleasant pertness as she went on with some sewing, “ Of course you must sit down here. [/]
[Narrator] And where does your fifty - cow dairy lie, Mr. Venn? ” “ At Stickleford — about two miles to the right of Alderworth, ma’am, where the meads begin. [/]
[Venn] I have thought that if Mr. Yeobright would like to pay me a visit sometimes he should n’t stay away for want of asking. [/]
[Venn] I ’ll not bide to tea this afternoon, thank’ee, for I ’ve got something on hand that must be settled. ’ [/]
[Venn] Tis Maypole - day tomorrow, and the Shadwater folk have clubbed with a few of your neighbours here to have a pole just outside your palings in the heath, as it is a nice green place. ” [/]
[Narrator] Venn waved his elbow towards the patch in front of the house. [/]
[Venn] “ I have been talking to Fairway about it, ” he continued, “ and I said to him that before we put up the pole it would be as well to ask Mrs. Wildeve. ” [/]
[I] “ I can say nothing against it, ” she answered. [/]
[I] “ Our property does not reach an inch further than the white palings. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ But you might not like to see a lot of folk going crazy round a stick, under your very nose? ” “ I shall have no objection at all. ” Venn soon after went away, and in the evening Yeobright strolled as far as Fairway ’s cottage. [/]
[Narrator] It was a lovely May sunset, and the birch trees which grew on this margin of the vast Egdon wilderness had put on their new leaves, delicate as butterflies ’ wings, and diaphanous as amber. [/]
[Narrator] Beside Fairway ’s dwelling was an open space recessed from the road, and here were now collected all the young people from within a radius of a couple of miles. [/]
[Narrator] The pole lay with one end supported on a trestle, and women were engaged in wreathing it from the top downwards with wild - flowers. [/]
[Narrator] The instincts of merry England lingered on here with exceptional vitality, and the symbolic customs which tradition has attached to each season of the year were yet a reality on Egdon. [/]
[Narrator] Indeed, the impulses of all such outlandish hamlets are pagan still — in these spots homage to nature, self - adoration, frantic gaieties, fragments of Teutonic rites to divinities whose names are forgotten, seem in some way or other to have survived mediæval doctrine. [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright did not interrupt the preparations, and went home again. [/]
[Narrator] The next morning, when Thomasin withdrew the curtains of her bedroom window, there stood the Maypole in the middle of the green, its top cutting into the sky. [/]
[Narrator] It had sprung up in the night, or rather early morning, like Jack ’s bean - stalk. [/]
[Narrator] She opened the casement to get a better view of the garlands and posies that adorned it. [/]
[Narrator] The sweet perfume of the flowers had already spread into the surrounding air, which, being free from every taint, conducted to her lips a full measure of the fragrance received from the spire of blossom in its midst. [/]
[Narrator] At the top of the pole were crossed hoops decked with small flowers; beneath these came a milk - white zone of Maybloom; then a zone of bluebells, then of cowslips, then of lilacs, then of ragged - robins, daffodils, and so on, till the lowest stage was reached. [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin noticed all these, and was delighted that the May revel was to be so near. [/]
[Narrator] When afternoon came people began to gather on the green, and Yeobright was interested enough to look out upon them from the open window of his room. [/]
[Narrator] Soon after this Thomasin walked out from the door immediately below and turned her eyes up to her cousin ’s face. [/]
[Narrator] She was dressed more gaily than Yeobright had ever seen her dressed since the time of Wildeve ’s death, eighteen months before; since the day of her marriage even she had not exhibited herself to such advantage. [/]
[MrYeobright] “ How pretty you look today, Thomasin! ” [/]
[Narrator] he said. [/]
[Narrator] “ Is it because of the Maypole? ” “ Not altogether. ” [/]
[Narrator] And then she blushed and dropped her eyes, which he did not specially observe, though her manner seemed to him to be rather peculiar, considering that she was only addressing himself. [/]
[Narrator] Could it be possible that she had put on her summer clothes to please him? [/]
[Narrator] He recalled her conduct towards him throughout the last few weeks, when they had often been working together in the garden, just as they had formerly done when they were boy and girl under his mother ’s eye. [/]
[Narrator] What if her interest in him were not so entirely that of a relative as it had formerly been? [/]
[Narrator] To Yeobright any possibility of this sort was a serious matter; and he almost felt troubled at the thought of it. [/]
[Narrator] Every pulse of loverlike feeling which had not been stilled during Eustacia ’s lifetime had gone into the grave with her. [/]
[Narrator] His passion for her had occurred too far on in his manhood to leave fuel enough on hand for another fire of that sort, as may happen with more boyish loves. [/]
[Narrator] Even supposing him capable of loving again, that love would be a plant of slow and laboured growth, and in the end only small and sickly, like an autumn - hatched bird. [/]
[Narrator] He was so distressed by this new complexity that when the enthusiastic brass band arrived and struck up, which it did about five o’clock, with apparently wind enough among its members to blow down his house, he withdrew from his rooms by the back door, went down the garden, through the gate in the hedge, and away out of sight. [/]
[Narrator] He could not bear to remain in the presence of enjoyment today, though he had tried hard. [/]
[Narrator] Nothing was seen of him for four hours. [/]
[Narrator] When he came back by the same path it was dusk, and the dews were coating every green thing. [/]
[Narrator] The boisterous music had ceased; but, entering the premises as he did from behind, he could not see if the May party had all gone till he had passed through Thomasin ’s division of the house to the front door. [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin was standing within the porch alone. [/]
[Narrator] She looked at him reproachfully. [/]
[Thomasin] “ You went away just when it began, Clym, ” she said. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Yes. [/]
[Thomasin] I felt I could not join in. [/]
[Thomasin] You went out with them, of course? ” “ No, I did not. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ You appeared to be dressed on purpose. ” “ Yes, but I could not go out alone; so many people were there. [/]
[Thomasin] One is there now. ” [/]
[Narrator] Yeobright strained his eyes across the dark - green patch beyond the paling, and near the black form of the Maypole he discerned a shadowy figure, sauntering idly up and down. [/]
[MrYeobright] “ Who is it? ” [/]
[Narrator] he said. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Mr. Venn, ” said Thomasin. [/]
[TheMaypole] “ You might have asked him to come in, I think, Tamsie. [/]
[Narrator] He has been very kind to you first and last. ” “ I will now, ” she said; and, acting on the impulse, went through the wicket to where Venn stood under the Maypole. [/]
[Thomasin] “ It is Mr. Venn, I think? ” [/]
[Narrator] she inquired. [/]
[Venn] Venn started as if he had not seen her — artful man that he was — and said, “ Yes. ” [/]
[Venn] “ Will you come in? ” “ I am afraid that I — ” “ I have seen you dancing this evening, and you had the very best of the girls for your partners. [/]
[Venn] Is it that you wo n’t come in because you wish to stand here, and think over the past hours of enjoyment? ” “ Well, that ’s partly it, ” said Mr. Venn, with ostentatious sentiment. [/]

[Narrator] she asked, in a voice which revealed that he had made himself considerably more interesting to her by this disclosure. [/]

[Narrator] Thomasin went back to the porch. [/]

[Narrator] When Clym was gone Thomasin crept upstairs in the dark, and, just listening by the cot, to assure herself that the child was asleep, she went to the window, gently lifted the corner of the white curtain, and looked out. [/]
[Narrator] Venn was still there. [/]
[Narrator] She watched the growth of the faint radiance appearing in the sky by the eastern hill, till presently the edge of the moon burst upwards and flooded the valley with light. [/]
[Narrator] Diggory ’s form was now distinct on the green; he was moving about in a bowed attitude, evidently scanning the grass for the precious missing article, walking in zigzags right and left till he should have passed over every foot of the ground. [/]
[Thomasin] “ How very ridiculous! ” [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin murmured to herself, in a tone which was intended to be satirical. [/]
[Thomasin] “ To think that a man should be so silly as to go mooning about like that for a girl ’s glove! [/]
[Thomasin] A respectable dairyman, too, and a man of money as he is now. [/]
[Thomasin] What a pity! ” [/]
[Narrator] At last Venn appeared to find it; whereupon he stood up and raised it to his lips. [/]
[Narrator] Then placing it in his breastpocket — the nearest receptacle to a man ’s heart permitted by modern raiment — he ascended the valley in a mathematically direct line towards his distant home in the meadows. [/]
[Narrator] II. [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin Walks in a Green Place by the Roman Road Clym saw little of Thomasin for several days after this; and when they met she was more silent than usual. [/]
[Narrator] At length he asked her what she was thinking of so intently. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I am thoroughly perplexed, ” she said candidly. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I can not for my life think who it is that Diggory Venn is so much in love with. [/]
[Thomasin] None of the girls at the Maypole were good enough for him, and yet she must have been there. ” Clym tried to imagine Venn ’s choice for a moment; but ceasing to be interested in the question he went on again with his gardening. [/]
[Narrator] No clearing up of the mystery was granted her for some time. [/]
[Thomasin] But one afternoon Thomasin was upstairs getting ready for a walk, when she had occasion to come to the landing and call “ Rachel. ” [/]
[Narrator] Rachel was a girl about thirteen, who carried the baby out for airings; and she came upstairs at the call. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Have you seen one of my last new gloves about the house, Rachel? ” inquired Thomasin. [/]
[Thomasin] “ It is the fellow to this one. ” [/]
[Narrator] Rachel did not reply. [/]
[HerMistress] “ Why do n’t you answer? ” said her mistress. [/]
[Narrator] “ I think it is lost, ma’am. ” “ Lost? [/]
[HerMistress] Who lost it? [/]
[HerMistress] I have never worn them but once. ” [/]

[Rachel] “ Please, ma’am, on the day of the Maypole I had none to wear, and I seed yours on the table, and I thought I would borrow ’em. [/]
[Rachel] I did not mean to hurt ’em at all, but one of them got lost. [/]
[Narrator] Somebody gave me some money to buy another pair for you, but I have not been able to go anywhere to get ’em. ” “ Who ’s somebody? ” “ Mr. Venn. ” [/]
[Narrator] “ Did he know it was my glove? ” “ Yes. [/]
[Rachel] I told him. ” [/]

[Narrator] How she managed to work hard, and yet do no more than she had done at the end of two hours, would have been a mystery to anyone not aware that the recent incident was of a kind likely to divert her industry from a manual to a mental channel. [/]
[Narrator] Next day she went her ways as usual, and continued her custom of walking in the heath with no other companion than little Eustacia, now of the age when it is a matter of doubt with such characters whether they are intended to walk through the world on their hands or on their feet; so that they get into painful complications by trying both. [/]
[Narrator] It was very pleasant to Thomasin, when she had carried the child to some lonely place, to give her a little private practice on the green turf and shepherd’s - thyme, which formed a soft mat to fall headlong upon them when equilibrium was lost. [/]
[Narrator] Once, when engaged in this system of training, and stooping to remove bits of stick, fern - stalks, and other such fragments from the child ’s path, that the journey might not be brought to an untimely end by some insuperable barrier a quarter of an inch high, she was alarmed by discovering that a man on horseback was almost close beside her, the soft natural carpet having muffled the horse ’s tread. [/]
[Narrator] The rider, who was Venn, waved his hat in the air and bowed gallantly. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Diggory, give me my glove, ” said Thomasin, whose manner it was under any circumstances to plunge into the midst of a subject which engrossed her. [/]
[Narrator] Venn immediately dismounted, put his hand in his breastpocket, and handed the glove. [/]
[Venn] “ Thank you. [/]
[Venn] It was very good of you to take care of it. ” [/]
[Venn] “ It is very good of you to say so. ” [/]
[Venn] “ O no. [/]
[Venn] I was quite glad to find you had it. [/]
[Venn] Everybody gets so indifferent that I was surprised to know you thought of me. ” [/]
[Venn] “ If you had remembered what I was once you would n’t have been surprised. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ Ah, no, ” she said quickly. [/]
[Thomasin] “ But men of your character are mostly so independent. ” [/]
[My] “ What is my character? ” he asked. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I do n’t exactly know, ” said Thomasin simply, “ except it is to cover up your feelings under a practical manner, and only to show them when you are alone. ” [/]
[Venn] “ Ah, how do you know that? ” said Venn strategically. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Because, ” said she, stopping to put the little girl, who had managed to get herself upside down, right end up again, “ because I do. ” [/]
[Venn] “ You must n’t judge by folks in general, ” said Venn. [/]
[Venn] “ Still I do n’t know much what feelings are nowadays. [/]
[Venn] I have got so mixed up with business of one sort and t’other that my soft sentiments are gone off in vapour like. [/]
[Venn] Yes, I am given up body and soul to the making of money. [/]
[Venn] Money is all my dream. ” [/]

[Narrator] “ You, who used to be so nice! ” “ Well, that ’s an argument I rather like, because what a man has once been he may be again. ” [/]

[Thomasin] “ O no — not much. [/]

[Venn] “ I am rather glad of that, ” said Venn softly, and regarding her from the corner of his eye, “ for it makes it easier for us to be friendly. ” [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin blushed again, and, when a few more words had been said of a not unpleasing kind, Venn mounted his horse and rode on. [/]
[Narrator] This conversation had passed in a hollow of the heath near the old Roman road, a place much frequented by Thomasin. [/]
[Narrator] And it might have been observed that she did not in future walk that way less often from having met Venn there now. [/]
[Narrator] Whether or not Venn abstained from riding thither because he had met Thomasin in the same place might easily have been guessed from her proceedings about two months later in the same year. [/]
[Narrator] III. [/]
[Narrator] The Serious Discourse of Clym with His Cousin Throughout this period Yeobright had more or less pondered on his duty to his cousin Thomasin. [/]
[Narrator] He could not help feeling that it would be a pitiful waste of sweet material if the tender - natured thing should be doomed from this early stage of her life onwards to dribble away her winsome qualities on lonely gorse and fern. [/]
[Narrator] But he felt this as an economist merely, and not as a lover. [/]
[Narrator] His passion for Eustacia had been a sort of conserve of his whole life, and he had nothing more of that supreme quality left to bestow. [/]
[Narrator] So far the obvious thing was not to entertain any idea of marriage with Thomasin, even to oblige her. [/]
[Narrator] But this was not all. [/]
[Narrator] Years ago there had been in his mother ’s mind a great fancy about Thomasin and himself. [/]
[Narrator] It had not positively amounted to a desire, but it had always been a favourite dream. [/]
[Narrator] That they should be man and wife in good time, if the happiness of neither were endangered thereby, was the fancy in question. [/]
[Narrator] So that what course save one was there now left for any son who reverenced his mother ’s memory as Yeobright did? [/]
[Narrator] It is an unfortunate fact that any particular whim of parents, which might have been dispersed by half an hour ’s conversation during their lives, becomes sublimated by their deaths into a fiat the most absolute, with such results to conscientious children as those parents, had they lived, would have been the first to decry. [/]
[Narrator] Had only Yeobright ’s own future been involved he would have proposed to Thomasin with a ready heart. [/]
[Narrator] He had nothing to lose by carrying out a dead mother ’s hope. [/]
[Narrator] But he dreaded to contemplate Thomasin wedded to the mere corpse of a lover that he now felt himself to be. [/]
[Narrator] He had but three activities alive in him. [/]
[Narrator] One was his almost daily walk to the little graveyard wherein his mother lay; another, his just as frequent visits by night to the more distant enclosure which numbered his Eustacia among its dead; the third was self - preparation for a vocation which alone seemed likely to satisfy his cravings — that of an itinerant preacher of the eleventh commandment. [/]
[Narrator] It was difficult to believe that Thomasin would be cheered by a husband with such tendencies as these. [/]
[Narrator] Yet he resolved to ask her, and let her decide for herself. [/]
[Narrator] It was even with a pleasant sense of doing his duty that he went downstairs to her one evening for this purpose, when the sun was printing on the valley the same long shadow of the housetop that he had seen lying there times out of number while his mother lived. [/]
[Narrator] Thomasin was not in her room, and he found her in the front garden. [/]
[Venn] “ I have long been wanting, Thomasin, ” he began, “ to say something about a matter that concerns both our futures. ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ And you are going to say it now? ” [/]
[Narrator] she remarked quickly, colouring as she met his gaze. [/]
[Thomasin] “ Do stop a minute, Clym, and let me speak first, for oddly enough, I have been wanting to say something to you. ” “ By all means say on, Tamsie. ” “ I suppose nobody can overhear us? ” [/]
[Narrator] she went on, casting her eyes around and lowering her voice. [/]
[Narrator] “ Well, first you will promise me this — that you wo n’t be angry and call me anything harsh if you disagree with what I propose? ” Yeobright promised, and she continued: “ What I want is your advice, for you are my relation — I mean, a sort of guardian to me — aren’t you, Clym? ” “ Well, yes, I suppose I am; a sort of guardian. [/]
[I] In fact, I am, of course, ” he said, altogether perplexed as to her drift. [/]
[Thomasin] “ I am thinking of marrying, ” she then observed blandly. [/]
[Thomasin] “ But I shall not marry unless you assure me that you approve of such a step. [/]
[Narrator] Why do n’t you speak? ” “ I was taken rather by surprise. [/]
[I] But, nevertheless, I am very glad to hear such news. [/]
[I] I shall approve, of course, dear Tamsie. [/]
[I] Who can it be? [/]
[I] I am quite at a loss to guess. [/]
[I] No I am not—’tis the old doctor! —not that I mean to call him old, for he is not very old after all. [/]
[I] Ah — I noticed when he attended you last time! ” [/]
[Thomasin] “ No, no, ” she said hastily. [/]
[Thomasin] “ ’ Tis Mr. Venn. ” [/]
[Narrator] Clym ’s face suddenly became grave. [/]
[Thomasin] “ There, now, you do n’t like him, and I wish I had n’t mentioned him! ” [/]
[Narrator] she exclaimed almost petulantly. [/]
[Thomasin] “ And I should n’t have done it, either, only he keeps on bothering me so till I do n’t know what to do! ” [/]
[Narrator] Clym looked at the heath. [/]

[Narrator] Cheerfulness Again Asserts Itself at Blooms - End, and Clym Finds His Vocation Anybody who had passed through Blooms - End about eleven o’clock on the morning fixed for the wedding would have found that, while Yeobright ’s house was comparatively quiet, sounds denoting great activity came from the dwelling of his nearest neighbour, Timothy Fairway. [/]
[Narrator] It was chiefly a noise of feet, briskly crunching hither and thither over the sanded floor within. [/]
[Narrator] One man only was visible outside, and he seemed to be later at an appointment than he had intended to be, for he hastened up to the door, lifted the latch, and walked in without ceremony. [/]
[Narrator] The scene within was not quite the customary one. [/]
[Narrator] Standing about the room was the little knot of men who formed the chief part of the Egdon coterie, there being present Fairway himself, Grandfer Cantle, Humphrey, Christian, and one or two turf - cutters. [/]
[Narrator] It was a warm day, and the men were as a matter of course in their shirtsleeves, except Christian, who had always a nervous fear of parting with a scrap of his clothing when in anybody ’s house but his own. [/]
[Narrator] Across the stout oak table in the middle of the room was thrown a mass of striped linen, which Grandfer Cantle held down on one side, and Humphrey on the other, while Fairway rubbed its surface with a yellow lump, his face being damp and creased with the effort of the labour. [/]
[TheNewcomer] “ Waxing a bed - tick, souls? ” said the newcomer. [/]

[Narrator] On the Sunday after this wedding an unusual sight was to be seen on Rainbarrow. [/]
[Narrator] From a distance there simply appeared to be a motionless figure standing on the top of the tumulus, just as Eustacia had stood on that lonely summit some two years and a half before. [/]
[Narrator] But now it was fine warm weather, with only a summer breeze blowing, and early afternoon instead of dull twilight. [/]
[Narrator] Those who ascended to the immediate neighbourhood of the Barrow perceived that the erect form in the centre, piercing the sky, was not really alone. [/]
[Narrator] Round him upon the slopes of the Barrow a number of heathmen and women were reclining or sitting at their ease. [/]
[Narrator] They listened to the words of the man in their midst, who was preaching, while they abstractedly pulled heather, stripped ferns, or tossed pebbles down the slope. [/]
[Narrator] This was the first of a series of moral lectures or Sermons on the Mount, which were to be delivered from the same place every Sunday afternoon as long as the fine weather lasted. [/]
[Narrator] The commanding elevation of Rainbarrow had been chosen for two reasons: first, that it occupied a central position among the remote cottages around; secondly, that the preacher thereon could be seen from all adjacent points as soon as he arrived at his post, the view of him being thus a convenient signal to those stragglers who wished to draw near. [/]
[Narrator] The speaker was bareheaded, and the breeze at each waft gently lifted and lowered his hair, somewhat too thin for a man of his years, these still numbering less than thirty - three. [/]
[Narrator] He wore a shade over his eyes, and his face was pensive and lined; but, though these bodily features were marked with decay there was no defect in the tones of his voice, which were rich, musical, and stirring. [/]
[Narrator] He stated that his discourses to people were to be sometimes secular, and sometimes religious, but never dogmatic; and that his texts would be taken from all kinds of books. [/]
[TheSpeaker] This afternoon the words were as follows: — “ ‘ And the king rose up to meet her, and bowed himself unto her, and sat down on his throne, and caused a seat to be set for the king ’s mother; and she sat on his right hand. [/]
[TheSpeaker] Then she said, I desire one small petition of thee; I pray thee say me not nay. [/]
[TheSpeaker] And the king said unto her, Ask, on, my mother: for I will not say thee nay. ’ ” Yeobright had, in fact, found his vocation in the career of an itinerant open - air preacher and lecturer on morally unimpeachable subjects; and from this day he laboured incessantly in that office, speaking not only in simple language on Rainbarrow and in the hamlets round, but in a more cultivated strain elsewhere — from the steps and porticoes of town halls, from market - crosses, from conduits, on esplanades and on wharves, from the parapets of bridges, in barns and outhouses, and all other such places in the neighbouring Wessex towns and villages. [/]
[Narrator] He left alone creeds and systems of philosophy, finding enough and more than enough to occupy his tongue in the opinions and actions common to all good men. [/]
[Narrator] Some believed him, and some believed not; some said that his words were commonplace, others complained of his want of theological doctrine; while others again remarked that it was well enough for a man to take to preaching who could not see to do anything else. [/]
[Narrator] But everywhere he was kindly received, for the story of his life had become generally known. [/]
